MITIE · MTO-GB · the new Memo conveyor's card of 22 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote in the layers behind the MITIE memo

These are the Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions it worked through before the Stock-specific Investment Case A&J Memo on MITIE was written: 52,111 words, against 12,347 words in the memo Analyst's draft.

Word for word. Every answer below is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written. Two things are changed, both layout only: each group's judgement sits above the questions it sums up, because it was written last; and each score is shown as a pill. A script checked that every word of every answer is on this page, in order.

The Investment Analyst's terms: it calls Layer 1 "the first set", Layer 2 "the second set", and Layer 3 and Layer 4 "the third and fourth sets". "The Consumer" is the Investment Analyst who works the next layer. "Our" means our own forecasts, as opposed to the sell-side's. Acronyms are explained under "Terms used" at the end, and on a computer by hovering over a dotted term.

On request: a clarity-edited version, with a summary of the most important messages and a comparison with the memo, as made for Xvivo Perfusion on 30 October 2026.

How to read the scores

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning below its title.

The letter beside each score is the system's own grade of that score (A from 90, B from 75, C from 50, D from 25, F below 25). ❌ marks a D, ❌❌ an F.

Layer 1

Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

82 questions in 10 groups · 23,078 words · written 23 October 2026, 11:07 to 11:26 UK, Opus, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
MITIE Group PLC (MTO-GB): Set 1 answers, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 22-Oct-2026 by the Investment Analyst for Set 1, for the Investment Analyst answering Set 2 (the operating and financial model, 18 months to four years).

Preliminary: what the Set 2 model is for, and the base it must start from

The single fact that governs everything below. On 21-Jul-2026 the board recommended a cash offer from OCS Group (owned by the private equity firm CD&R): 218.5p in cash plus the 3.1p FY26 final dividend, 221.6p in all, a 45% to 47% premium to the undisturbed price of about 151p. Shareholders approved the scheme on 16-Sep-2026 with overwhelming support; completion is expected in calendar Q1 2027, subject to regulatory clearance, chiefly the Competition and Markets Authority. The files hold no Competition and Markets Authority Phase 1 decision as of 22-Oct-2026. The share price is 210.8p (21-Oct-2026), 3.7% below the 218.5p cash (5.1% below 221.6p if the 3.1p dividend has not yet been received; the files do not settle whether it has been paid, so verify it). On my arithmetic that spread, set against an annual risk-free rate of about 4% and a break price of 150p to 160p, implies the market prices a break probability of roughly 6% to 12%.

What this means for the Set 2 model. The standalone operating and financial model does two jobs. First, it is the value a holder falls back to if the deal breaks, so it sets the downside. Second, it tests whether the 218.5p offer is fair, which matters to the Set 4 judgement of narrative and to Richard. Beyond completion there is no listed equity, so the 18-month to four-year forecasts matter only in the break case. Build the standalone model honestly; do not let the offer price anchor the forecasts.

The FY26 base (year to 31-Mar-2026), company basis unless stated.

  • Revenue £5,619m (+10.5%; organic +5.3%, acquisitions +5.2%). Business Services £2,985m (+17.6%) at a 6.2% to 6.3% margin before other items; Technical Services £2,634m (+3.5%) at 5.1% to 5.2%; implied central costs about £60m.
  • Operating profit before other items £264m (+13%), margin 4.7% (4.6% FY25, 4.7% FY24, 2.3% FY21).
  • Statutory operating profit £151m; other items £113m (FY25 £73m), of which amortisation of acquired intangibles £42m, restructuring £27m to £29m, acquisition and disposal costs £75m including £22m Marlowe transaction costs.
  • Net interest £27.7m (FY25 £16.2m); FactSet shows £31.0m. Reported tax rate 26.9%.
  • Free cash flow (company definition) £162m against guidance of at least £120m. Average daily net debt £440m, 1.2x EBITDA (some sources say 1.3x; FactSet period-end net debt including leases £444m, 1.37x); covenant leverage 0.82x against a 3.0x limit.
  • Q1 FY27 (to June 2026): revenue £1,406m (+10%), organic +4% (the same as H2 FY26); Business Services +23% to £829m; Technical Services down 5% (implied about £577m); Facilities Compliance £127m (+144%, mostly Marlowe); order intake £1.6bn (+33%), book-to-bill 1.14x; pipeline a record £32.5bn, over 70% to be awarded within 18 months; renewal rate 91%.

Company guidance and targets. FY27 operating margin of at least 5%; free cash flow of £150m a year; FY27 net inflation headwind of £10m to £12m (including about £2m of fuel); Project PRIO (agentic artificial intelligence) upfront cost £20m to £25m in FY27; Department for Work and Pensions demobilisation a £15m profit headwind in FY27; Marlowe cost synergies £15m run-rate by FY27 and £30m by FY28; leverage range 0.75x to 1.5x; return on invested capital above 20% (FY26 18.1%).

Consensus (FactSet median, only three brokers: Jefferies, Deutsche Bank, Investec; treat as "three brokers expect", not as a broad consensus). Revenue £6,000m / £6,288m / £6,586m for FY27 / FY28 / FY29; EBIT £301m / £331m / £353m (margin 5.0% / 5.3% / 5.4%); EPS (brokers' adjusted basis) 14.5p / 16.6p / 17.9p, growth +15.1% / +14.1% / +7.9% from a like-for-like FY26 base of 12.6p; free cash flow £150m / £178m / £198m; dividend 4.8p / 5.4p / 5.7p. Implied net finance cost about £34m / £31m / £29m; implied share count about 1.30bn to 1.31bn throughout (no buyback assumed).

Data traps the Consumer must not fall into.

  1. EPS basis. The company's headline FY26 "13.6p" (basic, before other items) is not the brokers' basis. FactSet's brokers'-basis FY26 actual is 12.6p, as is Deutsche Bank's. The widely quoted "10% beat against 12.3p consensus" compares different bases; the like-for-like beat was about 3% (12.6p against Deutsche Bank's 12.2p). Model from 12.6p if comparing with consensus.
  2. Fiscal-year labels in the FactSet weekly revision table are shifted by one year: its "FY2026" row (0.147) is the year to March 2027.
  3. FY25 free cash flow appears as £120m, £134m and £143m in different sources; FactSet's own definition (before leases) gives £144m for FY25 and £161m for FY26. Use one definition throughout and state it.
  4. Share count. FactSet 1,255m at March 2026; voting rights 1,301.7m at September 2026 (option exercises and Marlowe shares); fully diluted 1,372m; consensus implies about 1,310m.
  5. Employer National Insurance. Final gross cost £48.5m (earlier estimates £50m and £60m); 76% (£36.8m) recovered; net £11.7m. Older sources quote a "£15m net headwind"; that was a forecast.
  6. Public sector share of revenue is given as 45% and as 50%; use 45% to 50%.
  7. Interest. Company £27.7m against FactSet £31.0m (FactSet includes items the company presents differently).

Reliability of the Earnings Reviews. The caution about the latest Earnings Review's date is resolved: it states explicitly that it covers the Q1 FY27 trading update and AGM of 21-Jul-2026, i.e. after the March 2026 period FactSet shows as the last reported; the earlier Earnings Review covers the FY26 results of 4-Jun-2026. Its summary table carries some figures that conflict with other sources (FY25 free cash flow £134m; FY26 leverage 1.30x); I use company-disclosed figures where they conflict.

External change forces

C5710 questions
C-0163C-0254C-0357C-0438C-0560C-0658C-0750C-0864C-0960C-1058

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The external environment is moderately supportive in the near term and mildly negative in the medium term. Near term (to mid-2027): demand indicators are at records (pipeline £32.5bn, order intake +33%, renewals 91%), the statutory wage shock is fading (net inflation drag down from £18.6m to £10m to £12m), automation favours the largest operator, and the sector's strongest theme has already produced a recommended cash bid at a 47% premium. Medium term (FY28-FY30): the UK political turn against outsourcing, expressed through the Public Interest Test from April 2027 on public contracts above £1m and a proposed review of £400bn of contracts, threatens renewal rates on the 45% to 50% of revenue that is public, while the cost-plus contract structure caps margins near 5%. For Set 2 the external forces justify: organic growth of about 4% in FY27 rising modestly to 4.5% to 5% in FY28 if order intake converts; price contribution falling with wage inflation; no structural margin gain from external forces beyond the fading of the cost shock; and an explicit public-renewal haircut from FY28 in the downside case.

Horizon: the last 6 months and the next 6 months, compared with the last 12 to 18 months.

C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility?

C63

Question asked To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

Judgement. End-market demand for Mitie's services is growing structurally at a modest rate (the UK facilities management market grows about 2% to 3% a year) and has not weakened in the last six months; what has changed is the composition and the quality of Mitie's own growth, not the market. The market is supported by four structural drivers with multi-year visibility: first-time outsourcing (over half of new contract wins industry-wide), regulation-led compliance demand (Building Safety Act 2022, Martyn's Law), decarbonisation (let commercial buildings must reach EPC B by 2030 and over 80% of UK commercial stock is below it; UK commercial heat decarbonisation a £1.1bn market growing about 30% a year; the water industry's £104bn AMP8 cycle 2025-2030), and public capital programmes (£10bn prison capacity strategy; NHS capital budgets up £3.1bn; education up 19% in real terms). Against these sit two slower structural negatives: public bodies asked to deliver "more for less", and a political turn toward insourcing (the Public Interest Test from April 2027, covered at C-04). Visibility is high for the core: about 80% of revenue is recurring facilities management under contracts of 5 to 10 years (public) and 3 to 5 years (private), an order book of £16.3bn (about 2.9 times revenue, 60% of it recognised within one to three years) and renewals running at 84% (FY26) and 91% (Q1 FY27). Visibility is medium for the 20% that is projects and variable work, which follows client capital spending and has shown phasing delays in healthcare, local government and education.

The insight that matters for the model. Mitie's FY26 organic growth of 5.3% was led by projects, not by the recurring core: organic projects (Facilities Transformation: decarbonisation and data-centre engineering) contributed 2.5 percentage points (£125m), core facilities management only 1.0 point (£54m), against drags of £40m from the completed Department for Work and Pensions contract and £20m from telecoms exits. Growth has therefore shifted toward the more cyclical, more working-capital-hungry, less visible part of the business. In addition, part of every year's organic growth is price that merely passes on wage inflation (management cites 3% contract price increases in FY26 and 2% in Q1 FY27). With the National Living Wage rising 4.1% in April 2026 against 6.7% a year earlier, the price element of organic growth is falling, so a steady 4% organic rate now implies slightly faster volume growth than a year ago.

Last 6 months and next 6 months against the prior 12 to 18 months. Leading indicators improved: order intake rose 33% to £1.6bn in Q1 FY27, the pipeline reached £32.5bn (up 34% year on year), and Technical Services had secured 70% of its FY27 sales target by Q1. Over the next six months the main demand events are awards from the pipeline and the run-up to the Public Interest Test; no cyclical downturn in demand is visible. A cyclical swing is not present: the demand pattern is structural with medium-to-high visibility.

For the model. Recurring core revenue can be modelled with confidence at market growth plus share gain (about 3% to 4% including price); projects should be modelled separately with wider error bands and their own working capital.

C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C54

Judgement. Competitive intensity is broadly stable, easing slightly among the large national operators and intensifying at the edges (mid-market bidders on contested public contracts, and private-equity-backed roll-ups in compliance). The net change over the horizon is small and does not alter the margin ceiling of about 5% that the cost-plus contract structure imposes.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Easing at the top: the large peers have turned margin-first. Sodexo has reset its strategy and does not target operating margins above 5% until FY2030; ISS under Kasper Fangel is "margin-selective" and walks away from low-margin bids; Capita has withdrawn from central government bidding and rejects contracts below its 6% to 8% margin hurdle; Serco is disciplined and indexed. The Earnings Review judges that peer resets have "reduced aggressive price competition". The OCS-Mitie combination itself removes a top-five UK competitor.
  • Intensifying at the edges: competitors bidding low on contested contracts cost about 30 basis points of margin (peer-relative analysis); Mitie lost Defra and Rolls-Royce on price; ISS appears to have won the Department for Work and Pensions facilities contract that Mitie completed in H1 FY26 (ISS's Q1 2026 growth was "primarily driven by the mobilisation of the DWP contract", while Mitie's Central Government revenue fell 14% on "completion" of its high-margin DWP contract; this is my inference from the two files, not a stated fact). In compliance, private equity accounted for 58% of the 90 UK facilities management deals in H1 2026, and backs PTSG and Andwis, which compete with Marlowe. Experts expect un-leveraged mid-market firms filling local pipelines to cause "localised price wars".
  • The state as competitor: the Public Interest Test makes insourcing a competing bid on every public contract over £1m.

For the model. No change to the structural margin ceiling; assume no improvement in contract-level pricing and a small, persistent bid-pricing drag on renewals of contested public contracts.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

C57

Judgement. The disruption force is automation (agentic artificial intelligence in scheduling, helpdesk and back office; cleaning robots; remote monitoring and remote guarding). It is strengthening, and it acts on Mitie in two opposite directions: it lowers Mitie's own cost to serve and strengthens its tender position against smaller rivals (positive), but clients claim most front-line savings at once on cost-plus contracts and at the next tender on fixed-price ones, so it deflates revenue per contract and does not raise margins durably (negative for revenue, neutral for margin). On balance it is modestly positive for Mitie relative to the sector because Mitie is the UK leader in deploying it and scale is what lets an outsourcer keep a share of the saving.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Mitie's deployment is real and measurable: about 70 live AI agents under Project PRIO (McKinsey and QuantumBlack, 70 digital staff); the Aria app handles about 40% of service requests without human intervention; IBM Maximo MAS 9.1 automates about 43% of engineering service requests; over 1,000 cleaning robots; £200m invested in technology since 2017; Mozaic360 used by over 140 strategic clients. Margin enhancement savings were £25m in FY26.
  • The Sector Digest's judgement: in labour-heavy outsourcing clients claim most automation savings at renewal; the lasting gain is scale, which lets the largest outsourcers bid below smaller rivals while holding margins. The Industry Digest adds that where staff cost half of revenue, a 2% staff-cost saving adds about one percentage point of margin, but only until re-tender.
  • The threat side: Securitas cites remote guarding cutting client security budgets by up to 80% against manned guarding; security is a large part of Mitie's Business Services, so automation shrinks the revenue pool in guarding even if Mitie wins share of what remains. A former Mitie project lead says smaller firms "cannot fund up-front technology", which supports the scale argument.
  • Proof is still thin: PRIO costs £20m to £25m upfront in FY27 and management has no formal savings profile yet; price-sensitive clients may refuse to pay for outcome-based, automation-heavy contracts.

For the model. Treat automation as a source of back-office savings (tens of millions a year at most, partly reinvested) and of tender wins, not as a source of structural margin expansion; allow a slight deflation of revenue per contract in security and helpdesk work.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

❌ D38

Judgement. Political and regulatory change is substantial and on balance negative, but it is slow-acting for revenue and largely absorbed for costs. The biggest change is political: a UK government turning against outsourcing by default. The Public Interest Test, from April 2027, requires departments to assess public contracts over £1m (cleaning and security named) for insourcing before re-tendering; the Prime Minister, Andy Burnham, has proposed reviewing £400bn of annual contracts to increase insourcing; a parliamentary secretary said the era of public outsourcing must draw to a close; the Two-Tier Workforce Code returns from October 2026; Transport for London is piloting insourced cleaning. With 45% to 50% of Mitie's revenue public, this is the single largest structural threat to the revenue base, but it bites through renewals, which on 5-to-10-year public contracts means the effect builds from FY28 onward rather than within the next six months.

Cost-side regulation. Employer National Insurance (£48.5m gross, 76% recovered, £11.7m net in FY26), the National Living Wage (+6.7% FY26; +4.1% to £12.71 from April 2026), the Employment Rights Act 2025 (Mitie's own disclosure says its net risk exposure rose; restrictions on short-notice shift changes affect security staffing), the Growth and Skills Levy, and the Procurement Act 2023 (mandatory 30-day payment to subcontractors on government frameworks, an £8m working capital outflow in FY26). The shocks of FY25-FY26 have mostly passed; the next risk is the Autumn Budget of November 2026.

Regulation that helps. Building safety, fire and water compliance rules (the Marlowe market); Martyn's Law (security); EPC B by 2030 and net zero (projects); capital budgets for NHS, education, prisons and defence; "Buy British" procurement preferences. Rising compliance burdens favour scale operators that can absorb them.

Merger control. The Competition and Markets Authority review of OCS-Mitie is the near-term event. Deutsche Bank judges the combined share below 20% of the UK market and the intervention risk limited; Jefferies flags overlap in public sector work as the likely focus.

Mitigants within the public book. Much of Mitie's public work is in defence (AWE, Landmarc), justice and immigration, and secure government sites, where security vetting and specialist capability make insourcing harder than for office cleaning. The files do not split the 45% by contract type; that is a gap.

For the model. No revenue effect before FY28 in the base case; from FY28 a renewal-rate haircut on public cleaning and security contracts is the right way to express the risk (Set 2 should size it once the public book is split).

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

C60

Judgement. The net effect is turning from a strong headwind into a mild headwind, which relative to the last 12 to 18 months is a tailwind. Labour is about 60% of costs. The statutory wage shock is decelerating (National Living Wage +4.1% in FY27 against +6.7% in FY26; no new employer National Insurance step in FY27 so far; private-sector wage growth slowing to about 3%), and management guides FY27 net inflation (after pass-through) to £10m to £12m against an unrecovered £18.6m in FY26 out of a £170m gross cost hurdle. Availability of technical labour (engineers, electricians, heating and refrigeration technicians) remains tight and is the binding constraint in urban centres. Supplier concentration is low and falling in importance.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • FY26: gross inflation £121.5m (94% recovered, £6.9m net) plus employer National Insurance £48.5m (76% recovered, £11.7m net); £25m of margin enhancement savings covered the gap.
  • Fuel: £5m to £6m gross drag, about £2m net, recurring in FY27; Middle East energy risk keeps this live (the market regime digest notes energy-led inflation re-accelerating).
  • Suppliers: about 8,800 suppliers; a Preferred Supplier List of 900 covers 70% of spend; £2bn of annual spend on the Coupa platform; transactions down 15%. Builders' merchants' margins are contracting (Grafton's Great Britain margin -160 basis points, SIG gross margin -30 basis points), so supplier pricing power is weak. "Mitie First" targets insourcing £20m of third-party spend, and Deutsche Bank estimates £170m of subcontractor cost historically sat inside legacy compliance work, a reservoir of margin to recapture.
  • Risks: the Autumn Budget (November 2026) could add statutory costs; the Employment Rights Act and Two-Tier Code raise wage baselines on public contracts; a tight engineer market could force a return to expensive subcontractors.

For the model. FY27 net inflation drag £10m to £12m (management) is a reasonable central case; the main tail risk is a new statutory cost in the November 2026 Budget, which on the FY26 precedent would be only about 76% recovered.

C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

C58

Judgement. Mitie's own organic growth cycle decelerated from about 8% in Q1 FY26 to 4% in Q2 FY26 and has held at 4% for three quarters (Q2 FY26, H2 FY26, Q1 FY27). It is at or near its trough and the leading indicators point to a modest upturn, but the turn is not yet visible in reported organic growth. Reported growth will, however, slow mechanically in H2 FY27 as the Marlowe acquisition (completed August 2025) annualises: Q1 FY27 growth of 10% contained about 6 points of acquisitions, which fall to about 1 point from Q3 FY27. The Set 2 model must not mistake that mechanical slowdown for a deterioration.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • What caused the deceleration: tough comparisons with non-recurring surge security contracts, completion of the high-margin Department for Work and Pensions contract (Central Government revenue -14%), planned telecoms exits (telecoms revenue -35% to £37m), lower military training hours at Landmarc, project phasing delays, and slower inflation-linked price increases.
  • What points to a turn: order intake +33% (book-to-bill 1.14x); renewals 84% to 91%; pipeline £32.5bn; Technical Services, which fell 5% in Q1 FY27 (engineering maintenance -7%), has secured 70% of its FY27 sales target, 150% more than a year earlier, under its new managing director; the drag from the Department for Work and Pensions contract laps during FY27; Business Services is growing strongly (+23% with acquisitions; Spain +30%).
  • What argues for caution: the 4% includes about 2 points of price; the price component falls as wage inflation falls; Jefferies expects organic growth to slow to 5% as surge contracts complete; the profitable public contracts lost (Defra, the Department for Work and Pensions facilities contract) show the core still leaks contracts at renewal.

For the model. Organic growth of about 4% for FY27 (H1 nearer 4%, H2 possibly 4% to 5%), with an upturn toward 4.5% to 5% in FY28 only if the Q1 order intake converts; reported FY27 revenue growth about 7% (acquisitions about 3 points), falling to organic-only from FY28.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

C50

Judgement. The macro force acting now is sticky, energy-led UK inflation with Bank of England rate rises pulled forward (market expectations of Bank Rate about 4.25% by February 2027), tight public finances and slowing private-sector wage growth. It bites hardest at the company level through statutory labour costs (which are policy, not market, and are not fully indexed) and at the equity level through the discount rate, which matters only if the deal breaks. It bites least through demand, because most of Mitie's services are non-discretionary.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Company: interest sensitivity is small (£1.2m a year per 50 basis points); currency exposure is immaterial (93.8% of revenue in sterling, costs matched); inflation is roughly neutral for profit because 90% of contracts carry indexation, with a lag; private clients could downsize estates in a prolonged stagflation, which Deutsche Bank names as the main macro demand risk.
  • Sector: UK listed outsourcers trade on low multiples (Sector median 10.3 times earnings 24 months ahead, 32nd percentile of its range), which is what invites private equity bids; the market regime (a discount-rate shock with US 10-year yields about 5.1% and "Fragility" rated Double Underweight) refuses re-ratings and leverage. A break would return Mitie to that regime at a low multiple.
  • Industry: the professional services industry's multiples are held down by the fear that artificial intelligence replaces labour; facility services have fared better than recruitment or customer-service outsourcing.
  • Public finances: a squeezed Exchequer pushes departments to demand more value, intensifying public bidding; it also funds selected capital programmes (prisons, NHS, education, defence).

For the model. Macro moves revenue mainly through the price element (wage-driven indexation) and moves profit mainly through statutory cost shocks; build the National Living Wage and any Budget measure explicitly into cost and price lines rather than using a generic inflation assumption.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

C64

Judgement. Yes, two industry drivers are accelerating Mitie's demand, one visibly and one so far only in pipeline. Decarbonisation and building modernisation, plus data-centre engineering, are visibly accelerating the projects business: organic projects added 2.5 points of FY26 group organic growth; the Facilities Transformation pipeline grew to £6.8bn, ten times its level three years ago; management raised the projects ambition to a £2bn business from £1.5bn. Regulation-led compliance demand is accelerating the pipeline (the Marlowe cross-sell pipeline grew ten-fold to about £700m of annual contract value; over 80% of Mitie's accounts do not yet buy compliance from it; only 20% of core facilities management clients use Mitie compliance services), but Jefferies notes revenue synergies "have been slower to materialise", so organic compliance revenue acceleration is not yet proven.

Qualification. The driver that most strongly accelerates the industry's volume, first-time outsourcing, is working against Mitie in the UK public sector, where the political direction is insourcing. The acceleration is therefore concentrated in projects and compliance (about a quarter of revenue) while the recurring public core faces a slow headwind.

For the model. Projects can plausibly grow faster than the group (high single digit to low double digit) but with lumpier timing and working capital needs of about £25m a year (Jefferies); compliance cross-sell should be modelled as an upside option, not a base-case revenue line, until reported compliance organic growth confirms it.

C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

C60

Judgement. Four further external dynamics matter; the first is decisive and positive, the others are negative or two-sided.

  1. Private equity de-equitisation of UK outsourcers (positive, and already realised for Mitie). Listed European business-services companies trade at about 9.1 times EV/EBITDA against 12.0 times paid for unlisted ones since 2020 and an average 13.5 times at a 57% premium in deals since 2021; the window is judged open through 2027 unless listed valuations recover. On my arithmetic the OCS offer values Mitie at about 8.6 times FY27 consensus EBITDA (equity about £3.0bn at 218.5p on 1,372m diluted shares, plus about £0.45bn net debt), below those private averages though 15 times FY27 earnings. That supports the view that the price is full by Mitie's own history but not generous by private-market precedent, and it leaves a counter-bid theoretically possible, although Deutsche Bank judges one unlikely given rivals' lower valuations and priorities, and a long Competition and Markets Authority review would deter other bidders.
  2. Sector reputational contagion (negative). Capita's Civil Service Pension Scheme failure (£25m to £40m of remediation) led the government to insource another contract Capita expected to win; such failures feed the insourcing narrative that hits all public outsourcers.
  3. Labour relations and reputation (negative). HMP Millsike was found unsafe and understaffed on opening (Independent Monitoring Board, September 2026); UNISON says one in five payslips of transferred NHS cleaners were wrong, with strike action; GMB warned of over 300 security redundancies on the Asda contract. These feed political risk on public renewals.
  4. Credit conditions for leveraged buyouts (two-sided). The market regime digest reports the first credit crack inside the artificial intelligence financing chain. UK Takeover Code cash-confirmation rules make OCS's financing of this offer committed, so the direct risk is low, but tighter leveraged finance would reduce the chance of any alternative bidder in a break scenario.

For the model. Only dynamic 1 changes the near-term outcome; dynamics 2 and 3 are inputs to the public-sector renewal haircut.

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

C58

Judgement. Over the 6-to-12-month horizon the external picture is a moderate net tailwind: the statutory cost shock is fading, demand indicators are strong, and the dominant sector theme (private bids for cheap listed outsourcers) has already delivered a 47% premium. Over 2027-2030 a slowly building political headwind (insourcing of public work) and a structural margin cap offset much of that, so the external environment supports steady low-single-digit volume growth and a stable-to-slightly-rising margin, not a re-rating story.

Theme by theme (Sector and Industry Digests).

  • Private bids and buybacks at low listed multiples (rated "Early", the strongest current fit): strong tailwind, realised.
  • Artificial intelligence savings turned into tender wins by the largest outsourcers ("Early and smaller"): modest tailwind for Mitie as the UK's largest and most automated operator; revenue-deflationary for hours-billed work.
  • Statutory labour costs that indexation does not pass on: headwind, easing in FY27 (net drag £10m to £12m against £18.6m), with a November 2026 Budget tail risk.
  • UK public-service rules (Public Interest Test, Two-Tier Code, Employment Rights Act): headwind, intensifying from 2027.
  • First-time outsourcing: tailwind in private sector, reversed in UK public sector.
  • Scrutiny of adjusted against statutory earnings (Industry Digest): headwind to perception; Mitie's 106% gap between adjusted and basic EPS is the Industry's named example.

By level. Value chain: suppliers weak and supportive; labour tight in technical trades; public clients more demanding; private clients resilient. Industry: fragmentation and private equity consolidation favour scale. Sector: low listed multiples and no common cycle; facility outsourcers +21.9% over 12 months, pausing over three.

Internal change forces

C5813 questions
C-1145C-1262C-1358C-1460C-1554C-1660C-1744C-1862C-1955C-2062C-8638C-8760C-2160

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Internally Mitie is a company in strong, well-resourced positive change whose owners have chosen to sell it. The standalone changes are real: the pivot to compliance and projects (Marlowe synergies £7m in FY26, £15m run-rate FY27, £30m FY28, with an Interserve precedent of £55m against a £30m target), £25m of annual cost savings, measurable automation, a commercial recovery and a record of accretive buybacks. They have been spent so far on absorbing an exceptional statutory cost shock, which is why the margin has been flat and why consensus now expects the payoff in FY27-FY28 (margin to 5.0% and 5.3%). Against them sit a recurring burden of restructuring costs below the adjusted line, drifting working capital and execution failures on public contracts. The decisive internal change is the change of control: the chief executive, finance director and largest active shareholder all backed a cash exit at 15 times FY27 earnings rather than a standalone succession. For the Set 2 model this means: build the standalone case on the improving trajectory, but price in a leadership vacuum and a lower multiple if the deal breaks, and treat a share of "other items" as a recurring operating cost.

Horizon: the last 6 months and the next 6 months, compared with the last 12 to 18 months.

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders or other key actors changed, and what does the change signal?

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

Judgement. Leadership is changing completely, and the change signals the end of the Bentley era by sale rather than by succession. On completion of the OCS offer, OCS's chief executive Rob Legge becomes chief executive of the enlarged group and OCS's finance director Gary McGaghey replaces Simon Kirkpatrick; Phil Bentley (chief executive since November 2016) and Kirkpatrick leave the board. Bentley had already announced retirement for spring 2027 and the board had retained Egon Zehnder to find a successor, but no successor had been named when the board chose the cash offer. The board and the largest active shareholder judged a 47% premium in cash better than the standalone path through a chief executive succession; that is a validation of value created, and also a sign that insiders did not expect the standalone path to deliver more than the offer within a reasonable time.

Other changes in the last 18 months. Chair: Christopher Rogers succeeded Derek Mapp (July 2025). Audit and Risk Committee chair: Penny James succeeded Mary Reilly. Senior Independent Director: Jennifer Duvalier from January 2026 (Roger Yates retired), staying past nine years on a comply-or-explain basis. New non-executive Andrew Davies, former Kier chief executive (August 2026). Divisions: Alice Woodwark (Communities) left in February 2026 to run VP plc; Sam White joined from Costain to run Technical Services (December 2025); Kathryn Dolan became Chief People Officer (June 2025). Key actor: Oasis Management, holding about 11% (stated in one source as Oasis and the directors together), gave irrevocable undertakings for the offer.

What it signals for each scenario.

  • If the deal completes (base case, the market implies roughly 88% to 94%): leadership change is irrelevant to listed holders.
  • If the deal breaks: Mitie would have no chief executive succession in place, a retiring chief executive, a departing finance director's successor not appointed, and senior staff on retention terms tied to completion (cash retention bonuses equal to 150% of salary for top executives on completion). The leadership gap would be the single largest operational risk in the break case and would justify a lower standalone multiple for at least 12 months.

Incentive read. Bentley holds over 18 million shares (2,497% of salary; about £39m at 218.5p) and bought 195,000 shares in November 2025, so his alignment with the offer price is strong; the retention bonuses and his 600% salary long-term incentive award (granted in FY25 to defer retirement) mean the executives are well rewarded by completion.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced?

C62

Question asked To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

Judgement. The standalone strategy changed materially in the FY25-FY27 plan, from "facilities management" to "facilities transformation": move up the value chain into regulation-driven compliance (the £350m to £366m Marlowe acquisition, August 2025) and capital projects (decarbonisation, data centres), raise self-delivery, and use technology to cut cost to serve. It was clearly articulated (two divisions instead of three; named targets; projects ambition raised to £2bn from £1.5bn; compliance ambition stated as £1bn and later £1.5bn; a post-FY27 aim of winning more of a £1.5bn identified wallet across the top 50 clients; "water is the new energy") and well resourced (Marlowe funded with £228m cash and 86.6m new shares; £200m of technology investment since 2017; Project PRIO with 70 digital staff and £20m to £25m of FY27 spend). The recommended sale then superseded the standalone strategy: OCS's stated rationale is to combine two UK businesses into an £8.5bn revenue group and expand internationally (Middle East, Asia-Pacific).

Quality of the change. The direction is right for a low-margin labour business: compliance businesses earn 30% to 45% gross margins and 6% to 15% EBIT margins against Mitie's legacy 4.6% to 4.7%; self-delivered projects earn above 9%. The risks are two. First, Marlowe's customer base is small and fragmented (average contract about £11,000 a year against £1.7m for Mitie's mid-market accounts), so integrating it into a large-account culture risks churn and erodes the density benefit (about 300 Marlowe staff left in Q1 FY27; the Salford Quays office closes by December 2026). Second, Jefferies warned that the Marlowe deal moves Mitie toward a "riskier roll-up conglomerate" model, away from the disciplined organic story.

For the model. In a break case the standalone strategy would continue; model the mix shift as gradual (compliance and projects rising from about a quarter of revenue) rather than as a step change.

C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised?

C58

Question asked To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

Judgement. Headline financial targets have been reiterated rather than raised, while adjacent ambitions have been raised and one target has been beaten a year early. The October 2023 Capital Markets Day set the FY25-FY27 plan: high single-digit revenue CAGR, an operating margin of at least 5% by FY27, £150m of annual free cash flow, return on invested capital above 20%. Mitie delivered the £1.2bn revenue addition in two years rather than three; the FY26 free cash flow guide of at least £120m was beaten by 35% (£162m); the cost-saving target was raised to £25m to absorb tax rises; the projects ambition rose to £2bn from £1.5bn and compliance to £1.5bn. But the margin target was never raised (still "at least 5%" for FY27), the free cash flow target (£150m) sits below the FY26 outturn, and return on invested capital has fallen below target (18.1% against above 20%) after Marlowe.

Reading. This is a management that sets conservative headline floors, beats them, and raises adjacent ambitions instead of the headline number. That pattern makes the reiterated FY27 margin floor of 5% a probable minimum rather than a central case, and makes the £150m free cash flow target conservative.

What did not happen. No new mid-term targets beyond FY27 were set before the offer; the post-FY27 framework (share of wallet, projects £2bn, compliance £1.5bn) has no margin or cash numbers attached. In a break case the market would need a new plan, which would take time under a new chief executive.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline?

C60

Question asked To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Judgement. Standards are improving clearly in cost discipline, systems and commercial measures, and are mixed in operational execution on large public mobilisations and in frontline workforce management. Net, standards are improving, but the failures are in exactly the area (public contracts) where political risk is rising.

Improving.

  • Cost discipline: margin enhancement savings of £25m (£25.1m) in FY26 through back-office automation, organisational design and the Coupa procurement rollout; transactions down 15% while the preferred supplier list grew from 590 to 900; Marlowe cost synergies of £7m in FY26 against initial expectations of about £5m.
  • Systems: 25 acquired entities migrated onto IBM Maximo; MAS 9.1 upgrade; Mozaic360; the Agentic Mesh due in 2026.
  • Commercial: renewal rate up from 59% (FY25) to 84% (FY26) and 91% (Q1 FY27); customer Net Promoter Score up from +39 (FY22) to +64 (FY26); telecoms turned from a £10.7m loss to break-even after exiting unprofitable frameworks; bidding templates and databases to avoid loss-making contracts; non-renewal of £70m of low-margin contracts.
  • Governance: Audit Committee became the Audit and Risk Committee (January 2026); externally validated testing of internal controls on the COSO model; fraud risk assessments aligned with the Economic Crime and Corporate Transparency Act; audit tender from FY28; seven of nine directors independent.

Not improving or deteriorating.

  • HMP Millsike opened "unsafe, understaffed and chaotic" (Independent Monitoring Board, September 2026); an interim director was appointed in February 2026 to fix it; Communities results had already carried £7m of mobilisation costs.
  • Payroll accuracy: UNISON says one in five payslips of transferred NHS cleaners were wrong; strikes followed.
  • Employee engagement 63% against a 75% benchmark for comparable UK service companies.
  • Other items rose from £73m to £113m; debtor days rose from 29 to 31.
  • Technical Services keeps losing contracts at renewal (Q1 FY27 revenue -5%).
C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units?

C54

Question asked To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

Judgement. Perimeter reduction is a minor current force. The large disposals were earlier (social housing to Mears for £22m, catering for £73m, pest control) and the current actions are smaller: exit of unprofitable telecoms frameworks (revenue -35% to £37m, losses eliminated), deliberate non-renewal of £70m of low-margin contracts, dissolution of the Communities division (three reporting divisions to two), and closure of Marlowe's Salford Quays office. Meanwhile the net perimeter is widening (Marlowe, Spain, Nordic electrical contractors, refrigeration, heat pumps, solar). The simplification is real in management structure, not in business lines.

Implication. Pruning removes loss-makers (telecoms adds back about £10m of profit in FY27 as the concluded loss-making contract drops out) but depresses organic growth by a point or so while it runs; no further material pruning is signalled.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly?

C60

Question asked To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

Judgement. Mitie is entering new product markets substantially and new geographies modestly, and the choices are mostly sensible because each extends self-delivery into adjacent, non-discretionary or growing niches that existing clients buy.

Product markets. Facilities compliance (fire, security systems, water, air, building safety) via Marlowe, Argus Fire and others; commercial refrigeration (Forest Group, £7m, November 2025); heat pumps (RMS); solar (Custom Solar); water network management (a £128m ten-year contract with AWE, March 2026); data-centre electrical and fire work. These follow client spend and regulation; the risk is execution breadth.

Geographies. Spain (security: Visegurity for €11m and the SPM portfolio for up to £4.3m, doubling Spanish security revenue to about £40m; Spain revenue £227m in FY26, +30% in Q1 FY27); Ireland (£63m); Nordic data-centre electrical contractors El Team Vest and ABC Elektro (an initial £8.1m, £12.7m including deferred consideration), which follow global clients such as Microsoft and Google. The UK remains 93.8% of revenue, so these are options, not a geographic strategy; OCS's international footprint would supersede them after completion.

Sensibility test. Strong where it deepens self-delivery for existing clients (compliance, refrigeration, water); weaker where the customer base or culture differs (Marlowe's small-business clients; a new Nordic country with no Mitie platform).

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible?

❌ D44

Question asked To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

Judgement. Price rises are merely nominal, and slightly below full cost recovery. Mitie recovered 97% of cost inflation in FY24, 95% in FY25 and 94% in FY26 on wages, but only 76% of the employer National Insurance rise; total recovery in FY26 was 89% (£151.4m of a £170m hurdle). The group margin was flat (4.7%, 4.6%, 4.7% across FY24-FY26), which is what pure pass-through produces. Real pricing power is latent in compliance (non-discretionary, evergreen contracts, 30% to 45% gross margins) and in self-delivered projects, and management talks of "hardening pricing power", but there is no evidence yet of real price increases being applied and held.

Why the structure prevents real pricing. Contracts are largely cost-plus or indexed: experts say price adjustments are "capped strictly in line with inflation" and clients demand continuing cost savings over 10 to 15 years; facilities management cost-plus margins run 1% to 5% (an expert from JLL); about 10% of contracts have no indexation, and in FY26 those suffered an £11.7m profit drag. Standard clauses cover wage inflation but not statutory changes such as employer National Insurance (the Sector and Geography Digests both make this point).

Comparison. ISS achieved about 4% pricing in Q1 2026, but it was driven by Turkish minimum-wage indexation, again pass-through; Securitas claims full pass-through of labour cost inflation. No peer shows real pricing in facilities services.

For the model. Price should be modelled as wage-inflation pass-through at about 94% for wages and about 75% for any statutory cost change; no real price contribution to margin.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they?

C62

Question asked To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

Judgement. M&A stepped up sharply with Marlowe, the largest deal since Interserve, and then stopped: the offer suspended the capital allocation framework, and even before it Jefferies reported that management had paused large-scale M&A until leverage returned toward the lower end of its 0.75x to 1.5x range. The acquisitions are strategically sensible, the integration of costs is ahead of plan, but discipline on price and on returns is less convincing than the integration record.

Strategic sense. Marlowe gives Mitie UK leadership in compliance testing and inspection, a business with higher margins, regulatory demand and route density benefits when combined with Mitie's engineers; bolt-ons (four deals for about £15m in FY26 including Argus Fire; Forest; El Team Vest and ABC Elektro; SPM; Visegurity) add self-delivery capability. Pre-offer guidance was about £75m a year of infill M&A.

Integration. Cost synergies £7m in FY26 (ahead of about £5m expected), £15m run-rate by FY27, £30m by FY28; Deutsche Bank calls the £30m target "highly conservative" and models Marlowe adding 60 basis points to group margin by FY28; about 300 Marlowe staff left in Q1 FY27; the Marlowe supply chain is moving onto Mitie's preferred supplier lists. The precedent supports delivery: Interserve (£190m, November 2020) produced about £55m of cost synergies against a £30m target. Revenue synergies are slower; the integration budget is £27m and Deutsche Bank warns it could be exceeded.

Discipline. Return on invested capital fell from 24.5% to 18.1% after £414m of capital deployment; Bentley defended the Marlowe price by saying "traditional valuation multiples are meaningless" for the structure; Marlowe had a weak historical free cash flow record as a roll-up. On my rough arithmetic (Marlowe's EBIT margin of about 6.6% on revenue of perhaps £350m, plus £30m of synergies, against about £390m of price and integration cost) the post-tax return on Marlowe reaches only about 10% to 12% by FY28, below the group's return but above its cost of capital. The Consumer should treat that figure as an estimate.

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet, or stepping up shareholder returns, or is management increasingly mooting either?

C55

Question asked To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

Judgement. Until July 2026 Mitie was stepping up shareholder returns; the offer then stopped them. Cumulative buybacks since FY23 reached £303m; FY26 bought £63m (38m shares at an average 166p) plus £29m (21m shares) for incentive schemes; a new and larger £100m buyback for FY27 was announced in June 2026 and suspended on 21-Jul-2026 after purchases (the Earnings Review cites 49m shares for £81m, which appears to include earlier tranches). The dividend rose 5% to 4.5p (a 33% payout of adjusted EPS). Leverage was 1.2x to 1.3x, inside the 0.75x to 1.5x range, with covenant leverage of 0.82x.

Value of past returns. Buybacks at 150p to 166p against the 218.5p offer were accretive for continuing holders; the record is that of a dynamic repurchaser at low multiples.

Going forward. No listed shareholder returns after completion. In a break case the £100m buyback could restart (Deutsche Bank earlier added 2% to outer-year EPS for the reinstated buyback), but consensus now assumes no buyback (share count flat at about 1.30bn), so a restart would be an upside to consensus EPS of about 2% a year.

C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present or plausible?

C62

Question asked What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

Judgement. Several positive internal forces are present and three are material for a standalone model.

  1. Technical Services recovery under new leadership. Sam White (from Costain, December 2025) has secured 70% of the FY27 sales target in Q1, 150% more than a year earlier, and retention has improved; Technical Services is 47% of revenue at a 5.1% margin, so stabilising it would lift group organic growth by about 1 to 2 points from FY28.
  2. Self-delivery ("Mitie First"). Targets insourcing £20m of third-party spend; Deutsche Bank estimates £170m of subcontractor cost historically sat inside legacy compliance work. Each pound moved in-house captures the subcontractor margin.
  3. Automation (Project PRIO). About 70 live agents across eight domains; a schedule optimiser in Q3 FY27; agentic recruitment in Q2 FY27. Plausibly worth tens of millions of back-office savings, partly shared with clients.
  4. Customer metrics. Net Promoter Score +64, renewals 91%, and named wins and extensions (Lloyds three-year extension; Department for Work and Pensions security, £1bn over seven years; AWE ten-year water contract; Heathrow five-year integrated extension; BBC security; Co-op; Westfield; Nationwide; AstraZeneca).
  5. Apprenticeships and people. 90% apprentice retention over three years; £5.7m invested in apprentices; largest accredited member of the Good Business Charter.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present or plausible?

❌ D38

Question asked What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

Judgement. The negative developments are real and cluster in three places: the leadership and capital-allocation vacuum that a deal break would expose; the recurring cost of "non-recurring" items; and execution and workforce problems on public contracts.

  1. Leadership vacuum if the deal breaks. Chief executive retiring, no successor, finance director leaving on completion, retention bonuses tied to completion, buyback suspended; Egon Zehnder's search would restart from scratch (C-11).
  2. Earnings quality. Other items rose to £113m; restructuring and transformation costs recur every year (Target Operating Model £70.7m cumulative to March 2026, due to finish March 2027; PRIO £20m to £25m in FY27; Marlowe integration budget £27m; FY27 will add deal costs and retention payments). The Industry Digest's rule applies: a restructuring charge that reappears every year is an operating cost.
  3. Working capital drift. FactSet net working capital moved from minus £102m (H2 FY24) to minus £19m (H2 FY26); debtor days 29 to 31; projects need about £25m a year; retail clients demanded longer terms (a £37m outflow in FY25); the Procurement Act cost £8m. Mitie has historically used invoice discounting facilities of up to £140m to manage intra-year cash, which can flatter year-end free cash flow; the files do not say how much is used now.
  4. Public contract execution. HMP Millsike; payroll errors; Technical Services contract leakage; profitable public contracts lost on price (Defra) or to rivals (the Department for Work and Pensions facilities contract).
  5. Workforce friction. Engagement 63%; redundancy costs up from £4.7m to £5.3m; GMB and UNISON disputes; Marlowe staff departures (about 300 in Q1 FY27) risk losing know-how and small clients.
  6. Integration overload. Marlowe integration, the Target Operating Model and PRIO run at the same time as the transfer to OCS ownership; the Earnings Review and Change Forces report both name service disruption on sensitive public accounts as the risk.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors indicated scope for change in strategy or approach?

C60

Question asked To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

Judgement. The controlling actors have gone beyond indicating scope for change: they have executed a change of control. Oasis Management (about 11%, an active shareholder with no board seat) and the directors signed irrevocable undertakings for the OCS offer in July 2026; shareholders approved the scheme overwhelmingly on 16-Sep-2026. The new controller, CD&R through OCS, has stated its approach: combine Mitie and OCS into an £8.5bn revenue group, install OCS's chief executive and finance director, and pursue international expansion. For listed shareholders the strategic question is closed unless a regulator stops the deal. Notably, OCS is itself highly leveraged and loss-making (a net loss after tax of £141.3m for 2025) and bought EMCOR's UK building services business in December 2025 for about $250m, so the combined private group will carry much more debt than Mitie does today.

In a break case. Oasis would remain a large active holder with a demonstrated preference for a sale; a renewed approach by another private buyer or pressure for higher returns would be plausible, but a long Competition and Markets Authority review would deter other bidders (Sector Digest).

C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

C60

Judgement. The positive corporate changes are strong, broad and well resourced: a value-chain pivot into compliance and projects backed by a £350m acquisition integrating ahead of plan; a cost programme that has delivered £25m a year; a technology programme with a ten-year record of investment and measurable automation; a commercial recovery (renewals 91%, order intake +33%, Technical Services sales up 150%); and a capital allocation record of accretive buybacks. Their weakness is that they have not yet shown up in margin (flat at 4.6% to 4.7% for three years because they were spent absorbing £170m of cost inflation in FY26 alone) or in clean earnings (other items £113m). The dominant corporate change, however, is the sale: it converts all of the above into a fixed 218.5p for listed holders and makes the standalone changes relevant only as the floor value in a break.

For the model. The positive changes justify a standalone path of margin rising from 4.7% to about 5.0% in FY27 and toward 5.3% in FY28 (Marlowe synergies and telecoms account for most of that), provided no new statutory cost shock arrives; they do not justify assuming the restructuring costs below the adjusted line disappear.
