Industry and Value Chain

C5516 questions
C-2472C-2555C-2655C-2766C-2855C-2954C-3155C-8462C-3260C-3355C-3458C-3558C-3642C-3742C-3852C-3958

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Mitie leads a moderately supportive, low-margin industry. It is number one in UK facilities management (about 14%, twice the next-largest) and in UK compliance, in a structure of long indexed contracts, dispersed customers, fragmented suppliers and procurement barriers that favour scale. The national competitors have become more disciplined over the last two to three years, the weakest (Capita, Sodexo in facilities) have retreated, and OCS is becoming the owner rather than a rival, so competitive intensity is not rising at the top. The structure caps margins near 5% (cost-plus contracts, clients claiming automation savings, local fragmentation), and its fragility is concentrated in one actor: the UK state as client and regulator, which is moving toward insourcing and higher statutory labour costs. Historical precedent in the sector says the largest share-price gains come from bids, recoveries and buybacks at low multiples, and that losses come from fixed-price contract failures, political insourcing and leveraged roll-ups; Mitie has collected the bid premium, is not exposed to leveraged contract risk, but carries an unusually large gap between adjusted and statutory earnings and a large public book. For Set 2: assume share gains continue at the pace of organic outgrowth of the market (about 1 to 2 points a year), no real price, a margin ceiling of about 5% to 5.5% for the mix, and a public renewal risk from FY28.

C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

C72

Judgement. Mitie is number one in UK facilities management with about a 14% share, roughly twice the size of the next-largest competitor (Deutsche Bank and the Business Foundations report). It is also, after Marlowe, the market leader in UK facilities compliance (testing, inspection and certification of fire, security and water systems), a market of over 2,000 local firms, and it is one of the largest UK security providers. It is the leader, not a chaser; the OCS combination would extend the lead (combined revenue £8.5bn), though Deutsche Bank estimates the combined UK share still below 20%.

What the ranking does and does not mean. Leadership by share gives bidding capacity for complex national contracts, overhead absorption (Deutsche Bank cites an overhead conversion ratio of 41% against a peer average of 21%) and technology funding. It does not translate into pricing power, because the market is fragmented locally, contracts are re-tendered and the relevant share is contract by contract. Globally Mitie is mid-sized (ISS about DKK 85bn of revenue with 325,000 staff; Compass $42bn).

C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution?

C55

Question asked How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

Judgement. Probably yes on the first test, no on the second, with limited evidence. If Mitie holds about 14% and the next-largest about half that, and the combined OCS-Mitie share is below 20% (implying OCS at roughly 5%), the top five plausibly hold 35% to 40% of UK facilities management. The files do not give a share table, so this is an estimate. The distribution is steep from first to second (about 2 to 1) but the top five do not appear to hold five times the share of numbers six to ten; below the national tier sit hundreds of regional cleaners, guards and engineers, and across Europe local operators hold over three quarters of the addressable market. Compliance is far less concentrated (over 2,000 players). In the Nordics, by contrast, ISS and Coor hold about 40% each of integrated facility management, which shows what concentration can look like in this industry.

Implication. A moderately concentrated national tier on top of a fragmented local base: the national tier competes for large multi-site contracts where scale matters, while local firms cap pricing on single-service and small contracts.

C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

C55

Judgement. Shares are stable at the industry level and unstable at the contract level. Consolidation toward national providers is slow (global top-provider share rises about 150 basis points a year, per the ISS material) and Mitie's share gains have come mostly in steps through acquisition (Interserve 2020, Marlowe 2025) plus organic outgrowth of the market by about 2 to 3 points a year. New entrants have taken little share in the last three to five years; the newcomers are private-equity-backed consolidators in compliance (PTSG, Andwis) and OCS buying EMCOR's UK building services. The largest "new entrant" by share taken is the state: insourcing of public services (Capita's lost contracts; the Transport for London cleaning pilot).

Contract-level instability. Mitie's renewal rate fell to 59% in FY25 before recovering to 84% and 91%, against a long-run average of about 90% and Compass at 96%. A 32-point swing in two years means individual large contracts move between providers often enough that headline share stability hides real churn; the files do not reconcile how much of the FY25 low was deliberate non-renewal (£70m of low-margin contracts) and how much was loss.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones, and is this company among them?

C66

Question asked To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

Judgement. Share consolidates slowly toward scaled, technology-enabled, self-delivering operators that can absorb compliance and statutory costs and fund automation; Mitie is among them and is the UK's clearest example. Smaller operators sold out after the pandemic for lack of balance sheet; rising compliance burdens and up-front technology costs favour scale (an expert: smaller firms are "unable to fund up-front technology"); the Sector Digest judges that artificial intelligence savings let the largest outsourcers bid below smaller rivals while holding margins; the OCS-Mitie deal is itself consolidation.

Qualification. The most advantaged businesses on returns in this sector are not the largest generalists but specialists (compliance testing, cash handling) and caterers with purchasing scale (Compass at a 7.4% margin). Consolidation toward Mitie therefore raises its share and stability more than its margin. In compliance, consolidation is contested by private-equity platforms, and the Wisdom Library's caution applies: fragmentation is usually structural rather than a roll-up opportunity, because local relationships and density are what customers value.

C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: margin and return first, or volume first; benign or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

C55

Question asked What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement. The national competitors are now mostly margin-first; the volume-first players are mid-market firms, construction generalists filling capacity, and leveraged private-equity platforms. Industry prices do not fall when volume falls, because contracts are cost-plus or indexed; instead, clients cut service scope (cleaning frequencies, guard hours) and re-tender for lower total cost.

Competitor by competitor. Compass: margin-first, exits uneconomic contracts. ISS: margin-selective, walks away from unfavourable terms, renegotiates scope rather than conceding price. Capita: prices "to deliver rather than to win", a 6% to 8% margin hurdle, withdrew from central government bidding. Serco: disciplined, indexed. Sodexo: historically "price to win" with margin dilution on mobilisation, now in a margin rebuild targeting above 5% only by FY2030. OCS: private-equity-owned, loss-making in 2025 and leveraged; it has bought capability (EMCOR UK) and is now the acquirer, which removes it as an independent bidder. Mid-market: un-leveraged regional players seeking to fill pipelines may start "localised price wars" (expert view); low bidders on contested contracts cost Mitie about 30 basis points.

Implication. The attitude of the national tier is benign enough to protect a margin of about 5%, but not benign enough to let anyone raise real prices.

C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last 18 months to three years; would competitors launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

C54

Question asked How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement. The attitude has become more disciplined over the last 18 months to three years, because the inflation and National Insurance shocks forced every national operator to prioritise pass-through and margin, and because the weakest bidders (Capita in central government; Sodexo in facilities management) retreated. Instability of the "Michael O'Leary" kind is low among the national operators and higher among private-equity-backed and mid-market bidders.

Shark-attack risk. Two windows of weakness matter. First, the ownership transition: while Mitie is integrating Marlowe and then being integrated into OCS, rivals (ISS, Sodexo, Serco, CBRE) have a reason to target Mitie's public and key-account contracts at renewal, arguing continuity risk; ISS says it monitors the combination but does not see it as a constraint on its UK expansion. Second, Sodexo's turnaround: the Sector Digest lists "a Sodexo turnaround bringing price competition" among the Sector's threats, and the Business Foundations report flags Sodexo's margin catch-up plan as a credible blueprint that could narrow Mitie's window. Neither is a price war today.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

C55

Judgement. The structure is moderately supportive of a stable, cash-generative, low-margin business and unsupportive of high margins. Support comes from: long contracts (5 to 10 years public, 3 to 5 private), indexation on about 90% of contracts, high renewal rates in normal years, low capital intensity (capex about 1% of revenue), customer dispersion (3,000 core clients, none above 10% of revenue), procurement barriers that favour scale (an 1,100-page central government contract; security vetting), and fragmented suppliers. Limits come from: cost-plus margins of 1% to 5% on core contracts, clients claiming automation savings, local fragmentation that caps price, and incomplete recovery of statutory costs.

Fragility. The support is moderately fragile in one direction: public-sector policy. A political decision to insource, a statutory cost not covered by indexation, or a high-profile failure that triggers penalties can each remove contracts or margin quickly; the private-sector core is much less fragile.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

C62

Judgement. In UK and European outsourcing, outperformance has come from five patterns and underperformance from five others. Mitie fits three of the outperformance patterns strongly (recovery from crisis with margin rebuild; a private bid at a large premium; buybacks at low multiples), fits a fourth partially (mix shift to higher-margin services), and is exposed to two underperformance patterns (a large gap between adjusted and statutory earnings; political insourcing), but not to the most destructive one (fixed-price contract losses with leverage).

Outperformance patterns.

  1. Recovery from crisis with a margin rebuild: Mitie 2017-2024 (net assets cut by £272m to £90m after a £60.5m write-down and accounting review; Project Helix £45m of savings; margin from 2.3% in FY21 to 4.7% in FY24); Babcock's slower recovery.
  2. Private bid at a premium where a private owner can finance the business: Mitie (47% premium), Apleona (1.0 times sales, February 2025), Edenred approaches. The Sector Digest rule: bids come for simple facility and voucher businesses, not for defence-heavy or family-controlled ones.
  3. Buybacks at low multiples: ISS (cash returns equal to 54% of market value), Mitie (£303m since FY23).
  4. Mix shift to higher-margin services: Securitas to an 8.0% margin via technology and solutions, but Morgan Stanley estimates over 50% of the gain came from perimeter pruning; Mitie's compliance and projects shift is earlier.
  5. Clean cash conversion with indexation: Serco (debtor days 16, cash conversion above 100%).

Underperformance patterns.

  1. Fixed-price contract losses with leverage: Carillion, Interserve, Capita's pension contract, Mitie itself in 2016-2017. Mitie's projects growth and percentage-of-completion accounting are the small residual exposure.
  2. Political or reputational insourcing: Capita, Serco in 2013.
  3. Roll-up leverage with weak cash (the "false friend"): Green Landscaping (28% cash conversion, 3.4x leverage, write-downs, a fraud investigation, shares -67% in a year). Mitie does not fit: leverage 1.2x, conversion high.
  4. Adjusted earnings far above statutory: Teleperformance, Hays, and Mitie (106% gap between adjusted and basic EPS against a 20% to 30% peer median).
  5. Organic stall hidden by acquisitions: a watch-point for Mitie (organic 4% inside 10% reported growth).

Rule of thumb that matters most now. In this sector the largest recent share-price gains have come from bids, not from earnings delivery: the market pays nothing for bid optionality in a listed outsourcer at the Sector median of 10.3 times earnings, then pays a 40% to 60% premium at once. Mitie has already received that payoff.

C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

C60

Judgement. Customer demand supports the standalone thesis of steady growth and high retention, with private customers the firmer support and public customers the more fragile. Evidence of support: order intake +33% in Q1 FY27; renewals 91%; Business Services revenue +23%; Spain +30%; data-centre and decarbonisation projects demand; compliance cross-sell pipeline about £700m; named private wins (Nationwide, AstraZeneca, Co-op, Westfield, BBC security). Evidence of fragility: Central Government revenue -14% after the Department for Work and Pensions completion; lower Landmarc training hours; project phasing delays in healthcare, local government and education; public clients pressed to deliver "more for less"; retail clients demanding longer payment terms; all clients claiming automation savings at renewal.

Fragility judgement. The support is robust for 12 to 18 months (order book and pipeline) and moderately fragile beyond, because public renewals from FY28 meet the Public Interest Test.

C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

C55

Judgement. The route to market is mostly direct bidding and public frameworks, and on balance it helps Mitie because procurement complexity is a barrier that favours scale. Intermediaries are a mixed force: property consultancies (CBRE, JLL) advise clients on tenders and also compete by acquiring technical maintenance capability; framework operators give access but impose terms (the Procurement Act's 30-day payment to subcontractors cost Mitie £8m of working capital). The Retinue joint venture supplied 6,511 workers in FY26, which helps labour supply.

What could hinder. The Public Interest Test adds a mandatory insourcing assessment before any re-tender above £1m; "Buy British" and social-value weightings (apprenticeships, regional employment) favour mid-market domestic firms on some lots. Fragility is moderate and concentrated in public procurement rules.

C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

C58

Judgement. Existing competitors' behaviour helps Mitie slightly on balance: the national peers are disciplined, Capita is weakened and retreating, Sodexo is mid-reset, and OCS is about to become Mitie's owner rather than its rival. It hurts at the margin: low bidders on contested public contracts, ISS's apparent capture of the Department for Work and Pensions facilities contract, and private-equity compliance platforms competing for Marlowe's small clients and for bolt-on targets. Mitie's position is not fragile against existing competitors: it is the largest, has the strongest technology platform among UK peers and has a record £16.3bn order book (Serco's comparable figure is £12.8bn), but its margin is below Serco's (about 6%) and Securitas's (8%), so it does not win by being the low-cost price leader in every segment.

C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

C58

Judgement. New entrants are a low-to-medium threat overall.

  • Adjacent entrants (medium threat): real estate advisers CBRE and JLL acquiring technical maintenance and energy management capability; electronic security firms such as Securitas integrating technology with guarding; equipment rental firms moving into data-centre maintenance. They target the higher-margin technical and projects work Mitie is moving into.
  • Standard entrants (low in large integrated contracts, medium in compliance): security vetting, 1,100-page contracts, national coverage and balance sheet requirements keep newcomers out of large public and multi-site contracts; in compliance, private-equity platforms (PTSG, Andwis) are active entrants by acquisition.
  • Orthogonal or innovative entrants (low to medium, rising): artificial intelligence, robotics and remote-monitoring firms act more as substitutes for labour hours than as rival service providers; Mitie is itself a leading adopter.
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives a threat, and how is that changing?

❌ D42

Question asked To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

Judgement. Substitutes are a meaningful and rising threat, led by the client doing the work itself. Insourcing by the state is the most material substitute and is strengthening (Public Interest Test from April 2027; the Prime Minister's proposed review of £400bn of contracts; Transport for London's cleaning pilot; Capita's insourced contracts). Technology substitutes (remote guarding, autonomous cleaning, artificial intelligence helpdesks) are rising and shrink the labour hours a client buys, though Mitie supplies many of them. Some private property owners self-perform (Vivion cited). Private-sector insourcing of labour-intensive maintenance is unlikely.

C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

❌ D42

Judgement. Regulators and political actors are doing both, and the balance has tilted toward hurting since the change of government: the political direction on public outsourcing (Public Interest Test, Two-Tier Workforce Code, Employment Rights Act, statutory employer cost rises) hurts; the regulatory direction on buildings (fire and building safety, Martyn's Law, EPC B, net zero, water investment) and selected capital budgets (prisons, NHS, education, defence) help. The Competition and Markets Authority review is the nearest event and is judged low-risk by Deutsche Bank. The position is fragile because political decisions can change public demand and costs within a single Budget or spending review; the helpful regulation is slower but more durable.

C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting, how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

C52

Question asked On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

Judgement.

  • Demand (clients): automation lets clients buy fewer labour hours and claim savings; hybrid working and, in a stagflation, estate downsizing reduce office facilities demand; decarbonisation, building safety and data centres increase technical and projects demand. Net: a shift of the demand mix toward technical and compliance work, favourable to Mitie's strategy.
  • Competition: private equity is consolidating compliance and buying listed outsourcers; scale and technology spend matter more. Favourable to Mitie as the largest.
  • Regulators and politicians: pushing insourcing and higher statutory labour standards; raising compliance requirements on buildings. Mixed, negative for public soft services.
  • Suppliers: labour scarcity in technical trades raises wages; subcontractors squeezed by 30-day payment rules and weak merchants' margins. Mixed.

Overall the forces leave Mitie's position moderately robust in private and technical work and moderately fragile in public cleaning and security.

C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

C58

Judgement. Suppliers are a support; labour is the risk. Product and subcontract suppliers are fragmented (about 8,800), have weak pricing (builders' merchants' margins contracting) and are being consolidated onto Mitie's preferred lists (900 vendors for 70% of spend; £2bn of spend on Coupa) and partly replaced by self-delivery (£20m insourcing target). Labour, the dominant input (about 60% of costs; 84,000 staff), is the source of risk: statutory wage floors, tight supply of engineers, union disputes, and modern-slavery exposure in lower supplier tiers (2,208 high-risk suppliers identified by desktop audit).

Company - Competitive advantage

C577 questions
C-4158C-4265C-4362C-4464C-4542C-4655C-4755

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Mitie's competitive advantage is real but narrow: scale (twice the next UK competitor), switching costs in integrated contracts, regional density and security vetting, and a technology lead together protect its ability to win and keep work, as shown by 91% renewals, a £16.3bn order book and record customer satisfaction. They do not confer pricing power: prices follow costs through indexation, and the margin has been flat at 4.6% to 4.7% for three years. The advantage is widening in technical and compliance work (Marlowe density, data, self-delivery) and narrowing in public soft services as policy turns toward insourcing. The total market allows mid-term organic growth of about 4% to 6% with mix shift; mechanical forces (synergies, telecoms, mix) justify about 40 to 60 basis points of margin gain over FY26-FY28; beyond that, margin gains require sustained self-help. For Set 2: no real price; share gains of 1 to 2 points a year above market growth; compliance pricing as an upside option only.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold, how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

C58

Question asked Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

Judgement. Mitie holds a narrow moat built from four moderate advantages; together they protect its right to win and keep contracts, not its price. The best test is in the numbers: a margin flat at 4.6% to 4.7% for three years and full but not more-than-full cost pass-through show no pricing moat, while renewal rates of 84% to 91%, a record order book and an asset-light return on invested capital of 18% show a durable franchise.

Advantage by advantage.

  • Scale (moderate; widening slightly). Twice the size of the next UK competitor; overhead conversion of 41% against a 21% peer average; capacity to bid complex national contracts and to fund £200m of technology since 2017; procurement leverage (Coupa, preferred suppliers). The OCS combination would widen it. Scale lowers Mitie's cost to serve; the contract structure passes much of that to clients.
  • Customer-related switching costs (moderate; widening slightly). Integrated multi-service contracts take 6 to 12 months to transfer and carry operational risk for the client; client data and workflows sit in Mozaic360 (140 clients) and IBM Maximo; Mitie delivers integrated services to only 40% of its largest customers, so integration can deepen. The FY25 renewal rate of 59% shows these costs are not decisive at every renewal.
  • Production and density (moderate; widening via Marlowe). Self-delivered engineering and compliance field forces with route density; dual security vetting separates public and private mobile workforces, which competitors find hard to replicate; Marlowe adds compliance visits to the same routes. The Wisdom Library's point applies: this is density, not a network effect, and it holds only inside each region.
  • Regulatory and political (moderate; mixed direction). Security vetting, accreditation and framework access protect public and compliance work (non-discretionary testing regulations stop clients leaving compliance entirely). But the political direction (insourcing) narrows the public moat.
  • Culture and knowledge (modest). Standardised bid templates and data, a technology lead the sell-side calls a differentiator; artificial intelligence claims are operating basics, not a moat, unless they rest on data competitors cannot obtain (Wisdom Library).

Overall direction. Slightly widening in private and technical work (density, data, compliance), narrowing in public soft services (policy).

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors, and what does that scale confer?

C65

Question asked How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

Judgement. Mitie is the largest UK facilities services company (£5.6bn of revenue; about 84,000 directly employed staff; 3,000 core clients; about 8,800 suppliers; over 70,000 data points processed every 15 minutes from 18,000 pieces of monitored equipment), about twice the next UK competitor, but mid-sized internationally (ISS about £9.8bn equivalent and 325,000 staff; Securitas SEK 155bn; Compass $42bn; Serco about £5bn). Scale confers: eligibility for complex multi-site and secure contracts; overhead leverage; the ability to absorb compliance and statutory costs better than small rivals; technology funding (Project PRIO, robotics fleets of over 1,000 units, a security operations centre); acquisition capacity; and procurement discounts. It does not confer pricing power over clients, and in the UK public market it raises political visibility (a large outsourcer is the natural target of insourcing rhetoric).

C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

C62

Judgement. The addressable market is large relative to Mitie and growing at about 2% to 3% a year in the core with faster niches, which allows organic growth of about 4% to 6% a year in the mid term if Mitie keeps gaining share and shifting mix; it does not support high single-digit organic growth for long.

Sizing. A 14% share implies a UK facilities management market of about £40bn; Mitie's revenue is therefore about a seventh of its core market. Niches growing faster: UK commercial heat decarbonisation (£1.1bn, about 30% a year); compliance (fragmented, over 2,000 firms; Mitie targets £1bn to £1.5bn of compliance revenue against Facilities Compliance running at roughly £500m a year on Q1 FY27's £127m); projects (target £2bn; pipeline £6.8bn); data centres and water (AMP8 £104bn). Within its own clients: a £1.5bn share-of-wallet opportunity in the top 50 accounts and about £700m of compliance cross-sell pipeline. Spain, Ireland and the Nordics are small options.

Constraint. About 45% to 50% of the addressable base is UK public spending, which is flat-to-down in real terms for soft services and exposed to insourcing.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior?

C64

Question asked How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

Judgement. Mitie's offering is demonstrably not inferior and probably leading in the UK on integration and technology, but not demonstrably superior on value for money. Evidence against inferiority: customer Net Promoter Score at a record +64 (from +39 in FY22); renewals 91% in Q1 FY27; long extensions with demanding clients (Lloyds, its largest private customer; Heathrow five years; AWE ten years; the Department for Work and Pensions security contract worth £1bn over seven years); Jefferies calls its technology a key differentiator; Mozaic360 is used by over 140 clients. Evidence of limits: contracts lost on price or scoring (Defra, Rolls-Royce, two notable public contracts), the 59% renewal rate in FY25, the failed HMP Millsike mobilisation and payroll errors on NHS cleaning contracts.

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

❌ D42

Judgement. Mitie does not have demonstrable pricing power; it has demonstrable cost pass-through. Recovery of 94% to 97% of wage inflation and 76% of the employer National Insurance rise, with a flat margin, is what indexation produces, not what pricing power produces. Pricing power is plausible but unproven in compliance (non-discretionary, evergreen contracts, 30% to 45% gross margins, small contract values that clients rarely re-tender) and in self-delivered projects (margins above 9%). The application test fails today: no evidence of price rises above cost held without loss of volume.

For the model. Price equals cost pass-through (about 94% for wages, about 75% for statutory costs); real pricing is an upside option confined to compliance.

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

C55

Judgement. De facto, substantially; stated, partially. The contract structure forces sharing: cost-plus contracts pass savings straight to the client, fixed-price contracts hand them back at re-tender, and clients demand continuing savings over 10 to 15 years. Mitie's messaging frames technology as improving client outcomes and moving to outcome-based contracts, which is a form of stated sharing. But the strategy is also about capturing margin (self-delivery, "Mitie First", compliance mix), not about lowering prices to grow volume in the way the Wisdom Library's "scale economies shared" pattern (Costco) describes. The sharing supports retention and share gain; it is why scale does not show up in margin.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

C55

Judgement. Partly mechanical, partly heroic. The mechanical forces over FY26-FY28 are: Marlowe cost synergies that are largely contracted actions (£7m to £30m, about 40 basis points of group margin on FY26 revenue), the mix shift toward higher-margin compliance and projects as they outgrow the core, the drop-out of telecoms losses, operating leverage on central costs of about £60m, and consolidation toward scaled operators. The heroic part is the core facilities management margin, which needs constant cost-out (about £25m a year of margin enhancement) merely to offset clients' savings demands and the share of statutory costs not recovered: a Red Queen race. With each period that passes, Mitie's competitive position improves slowly through density and data, but its margin improves only if the mechanical forces outrun the treadmill.

For the model. Roughly 40 to 60 basis points of margin improvement over FY26-FY28 can be treated as mechanical (synergies, telecoms, mix); anything beyond that requires management action that new private owners, not public shareholders, would deliver.

Company - Economic strength

C5611 questions
C-4952C-5060C-5166C-5260C-5352C-5435C-7555C-7666C-7756C-7862C-7963

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Mitie is economically sound but not strong: asset-light, cash-generative and moderately geared, with high returns on capital, but with a low and so far flat margin, EPS growth that has lagged revenue growth for two years, and an earnings base made unclean by about £113m of other items, part of which is a recurring operating cost. Return on invested capital of 18.1% (down from 24.5% after Marlowe) and free cash flow of £162m (about 100% of brokers'-basis net income) are the strengths; the eroding negative working capital, the 106% gap between adjusted and basic EPS, and the reliance of consensus EPS growth (+15% and +14% in FY27-FY28) on a margin step that has not yet happened are the weaknesses. Operating leverage on volume is modest; leverage on margin assumptions is large (50 basis points of margin is about 11% of EPS). For Set 2, the economic-strength evidence supports: margin 5.0% in FY27 rising to about 5.3% in FY28 (largely synergies and telecoms), organic growth of 4% to 5%, interest of about £30m to £34m, tax of about 25% plus minorities, free cash flow conversion of 80% to 85% of adjusted net income with explicit deductions for recurring restructuring and PRIO cash costs, and no buyback in the base case.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

C52

Judgement. For two years Mitie has been revenue-rich and earnings-poor: revenue grew 14.3% in FY25 and 10.5% in FY26, but EPS on the brokers' basis grew only 4% (11.3p to 11.8p) and 7% (to 12.6p); on FactSet's "Viewforth definition" EPS was flat to down (11.8p, 11.8p, 11.1p); reported basic EPS fell (9.8p, 8.2p, 6.6p). The company's own adjusted EPS shows a 13% CAGR since 2023, but on a basis that is flattered relative to the brokers' (13.6p against 12.6p in FY26). Consensus now expects acceleration: +15% in FY27, +14% in FY28, +8% in FY29.

Why EPS lagged revenue. (1) Margin was flat because savings absorbed the £170m FY26 cost hurdle; (2) acquisitions added revenue at low initial margins and added debt; (3) net interest rose from £13.6m (FY24, FactSet) to £31.0m (FY26); (4) the reported tax rate rose from 16.9% to 26.9%; (5) Marlowe added 86.6m shares, partly offset by buybacks; (6) other items and amortisation grew.

Durability of the expected acceleration. It rests on a margin step from 4.7% to 5.0% (FY27) and 5.3% (FY28), which is the thing that has not happened for two years, and on the absence of buybacks being offset by synergies. About half of the FY27 EBIT growth is identifiable and largely mechanical (Marlowe synergies and full-year consolidation, telecoms drop-out, lower net inflation drag), so FY27 is fairly durable; FY28-FY29 depend more on volume and on no new statutory cost shock. Interest (consensus implies about £34m in FY27) and tax (about 25% plus minorities) leave little room for below-the-line help.

For the model. Model EPS from EBIT down, on the brokers' basis, with interest, tax, minorities (about £10m) and a share count of about 1.30bn to 1.31bn stated explicitly; reconcile to the company's 13.6p only as a separate line.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

C60

Judgement. Moderate scope at good returns, but a small amount of capital: Mitie is asset-light (capital expenditure about 1% of revenue, about £35m a year in consensus), so organic growth consumes little capital and returns on organic growth are high. The main uses are working capital for projects (about £25m a year), bolt-on acquisitions in a fragmented compliance and technical market (pre-offer guide about £75m a year; deals such as Forest at £7m and SPM at £4.3m look high-return), technology operating spend (Project PRIO £20m to £25m) and large acquisitions, where returns are lower (Marlowe about 10% to 12% post-tax by FY28 on my estimate). With free cash flow of £150m to £200m a year and reinvestment needs of perhaps a third to a half of that, Mitie is a cash generator with surplus for returns rather than a compounder that can reinvest everything at high returns.

Fair multiple implication. Such a business justifies a market-average multiple, not a compounder multiple: its ten-year median 24-month-forward P/E is 9.1x and the five-year range 6.5x to 12.4x; the offer at about 12x 24-month-forward and 15x FY27 earnings is at the top of that range.

C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

C66

Judgement. Returns are high for the sector because the business is asset-light, and they have been diluted by acquisitions. Return on invested capital was 18.1% in FY26, down from 24.5% in FY25 after £414m of capital deployment; the target is above 20%. Peers: Sodexo 6.9%, ISS 9.2% to 11.0%, Compass about 18%, Green Landscaping 7.1%. Return on tangible capital is very high (tangible capital is small or negative: the balance sheet is mostly goodwill and intangibles, and FactSet consensus shows shareholders' equity of only about £438m at FY27). Return on new invested capital: organic growth needs little capital, so its incremental return is very high; acquisitions earn less (Interserve, at £190m with about £55m of synergies, earned well above 20%; Marlowe about 10% to 12% post-tax by FY28 on my estimate). Reported return on capital employed is not given in the files.

For the model. Group return on invested capital should recover toward 20% by FY28 as Marlowe synergies arrive, absent further large acquisitions.

C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

C60

Judgement. Conversion is high on a like-for-like basis (FY26 free cash flow of £162m is about 100% of brokers'-basis net income of about £158m and about 196% of reported net income of £82.6m), but the FY26 figure is flattered by timing and the trend in working capital is adverse. Consensus expects conversion of about 79% in FY27 (£150m of free cash flow against £190m of adjusted net income), 83% in FY28 and 85% in FY29, which is a reasonable and slightly conservative central case.

Working capital behaviour.

  • The structural negative working capital has eroded: FactSet net working capital moved from minus £102m (H2 FY24) to minus £29m (H2 FY25) to minus £19m (H2 FY26).
  • Debtor days rose from 29 to 31 (Marlowe); creditor days rose from 32 to 34; Serco runs 16 debtor days.
  • Specific drains: projects (about £25m a year; Jefferies reported adjusted working capital outflows of £35m to £37m); the Procurement Act (£8m, a one-off); retail clients' longer terms (£37m outflow in FY25).
  • Seasonality: large H1 outflows reverse in H2 (closing net debt £477m at June 2026 against £444m at March 2026).
  • Financing: Mitie has historically used invoice discounting of up to £140m; the files do not show current use. The Consumer should check, because factoring can lift year-end free cash flow.
  • Definitions: company free cash flow is after interest, tax and capital expenditure; it is not clear whether it is after lease payments and cash restructuring costs. FactSet's free cash flow (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capital expenditure only) was £161m in FY26.

For the model. Use about 80% to 85% conversion of adjusted net income, a projects working capital outflow of about £25m a year, cash restructuring and PRIO costs (£20m to £25m in FY27) deducted explicitly, and no repeat of the FY26 timing benefit.

C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

C52

Judgement. Margins are low and flat-to-slowly-rising. Gross margin (FactSet) has been 10.3% to 10.8% for five years (10.8% in FY26). Company operating margin before other items: 2.3% (FY21), 4.7% (FY24), 4.6% (FY25), 4.7% (FY26); guided to at least 5% in FY27; consensus 5.0%, 5.3%, 5.4% for FY27-FY29. Segments: Business Services 6.2% to 6.3%, Technical Services 5.1% to 5.2%. FactSet's adjusted EBIT margin is lower (3.6% in FY26) because it deducts items the company excludes; EBITDA margin on the brokers' basis was 6.4% in FY26, consensus 6.7% to 6.9%.

Against peers. Serco about 6%; ISS 5.0% (guided about 5.25% for 2026, targeting 5.5% to 6.0% by 2028); Securitas 8.0% (technology mix); Compass 7.4%; Sodexo 3.2% to 3.4%. Mitie sits in the lower half, consistent with its soft-services and public mix and 60% labour cost.

Trend drivers. Upward: Marlowe synergies (about +40 basis points by FY28), mix toward compliance and projects, telecoms drop-out, margin enhancement savings. Downward: unrecovered statutory costs, Department for Work and Pensions loss (-£15m in FY27), bid-pricing drag on renewals, Technical Services contract losses.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

❌ D35

Judgement. The earnings base is not clean; it is the weakest foundation in the case. In FY26 statutory operating profit was £151m against £264m before other items, a gap of £113m (43% of the adjusted figure); reported basic EPS was 6.6p against 13.6p adjusted, a 106% gap against a 20% to 30% peer median (the Industry Digest's lead example). Other items rose from £73m (FY25) to £113m and are recurring in substance.

Components and how to treat them.

  • Amortisation of acquired intangibles, £42m: non-cash; recurring while Mitie acquires; acceptable to exclude for valuation if acquisitions are charged as capital.
  • Restructuring and transformation, £27m to £29m a year (Target Operating Model £70.7m cumulative to March 2026; Project Helix before it; margin enhancement overhead £29m in FY26): recurring cash costs that buy the savings that support the adjusted margin. They should be treated as an operating cost.
  • Acquisition and disposal costs, £75m in FY26 (Marlowe transaction £22m, integration): genuinely tied to deals, but deals are part of the strategy.
  • FY27 additions: Project PRIO £20m to £25m (likely below the adjusted line, judging by management's reference to statutory margins), Marlowe integration (budget £27m), OCS deal costs and completion retention bonuses.

Tax and other leakage. The reported tax rate rose to 26.9% (UK statutory 25%); consensus implies about 25% plus about £10m of minorities; no hidden tax benefit. Interest is rising (£16.2m to £27.7m; consensus about £34m in FY27). Historical capitalisation of mobilisation costs was a feature of the 2016-2017 accounting problems; the files say new management cleaned the balance sheet ahead of IFRS 15 but do not show current capitalised mobilisation balances. Projects use percentage-of-completion accounting, a small but growing judgement area.

For the model. Carry a recurring "other items" cash cost of about £25m to £30m a year in free cash flow and in a "true" earnings line, separate from the brokers' adjusted EPS that the sell-side and the offer price use.

C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

C55

Judgement. Operating leverage on volume is modest; leverage on margin assumptions is large. Most costs are variable at contract level (labour transfers with contracts; subcontract and materials follow volume), so a lost contract removes most of its cost. The fixed or semi-fixed layer is central costs of about £60m, divisional overheads, technology and the self-delivered engineering field force. On a gross margin of about 10.8%, the incremental EBIT margin on extra volume is probably 7% to 10% against an average of 4.7%.

Sensitivities (my arithmetic on FY27 consensus). A 1% change in revenue (about £60m) moves EBIT by about £4m to £6m (about 1.5% to 2%). A 50 basis point change in margin moves EBIT by about £30m (about 10%) and EPS by about 1.7p (about 11%). The range of outcomes is therefore set by margin, which is set by cost recovery, synergies and contract pricing, not by volume. Jefferies' scenarios bracket FY27 EPS at 13.2p (bear: contract attrition, poor Marlowe integration, unmitigated labour inflation, margin 4.8%) to 16.8p (bull: 10% organic growth, margin 5.2%) around consensus of 14.5p, a range of minus 9% to plus 16%.

Downside asymmetry. Self-delivery (more employed engineers instead of subcontractors) raises the fixed share of cost and therefore operating leverage in a downturn, a trade-off the Business Foundations report flags.

C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

C66

Judgement. Financial leverage is modest and comfortable for the standalone business, and amplifies EPS only slightly. Average daily net debt was £440m in FY26 (1.2x EBITDA; 1.3x in some sources); FactSet period-end net debt including leases £444m (1.37x); closing net debt £477m at June 2026; covenant leverage 0.82x against a 3.0x limit; the target range is 0.75x to 1.5x. Deutsche Bank expected average leverage to reach 1.45x in FY27 (before the buyback was suspended). Debt structure: £180m of unsecured US private placement notes maturing to 2034 and a £250m revolving credit facility to October 2028. Net interest of £27.7m in FY26 (consensus about £34m in FY27) against EBIT of about £300m gives interest cover of about 9 times. A 50 basis point change in rates moves interest by about £1.2m a year (under 1% of EPS).

Peers. Serco 1.5x (FactSet; below 0.75x on its own measure), ISS 2.6x (2.3x on its own measure), Green Landscaping 3.4x to 3.9x, Sodexo and Aramark 2.9x.

After completion. OCS, loss-making and leveraged, will refinance the combined group with much higher debt; that is irrelevant to listed holders but means Mitie's balance sheet strength is part of what the buyer is paying for.

C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

C56

Judgement. Little of the cost base is cuttable in a way that stays with shareholders; Mitie's record suggests about £25m to £30m a year of savings (0.5% of revenue), most of which offsets inflation and client savings demands rather than lifting margin.

By cost line (revenue £5.6bn; costs about £5.35bn).

  • Front-line labour (about 60% of costs, about £3.2bn): contract-bound and largely transferred under employment law with contracts; reducible only through productivity (scheduling optimisation, robotics, artificial intelligence helpdesks), and clients claim most of that at once or at re-tender.
  • Subcontractors and materials (Coupa manages over £2bn of spend): reducible by a few per cent through preferred-supplier discounts (900 suppliers cover 70% of spend) and insourcing ("Mitie First" targets £20m of third-party spend; legacy compliance carried an estimated £170m of subcontract cost); these savings stay with Mitie on fixed-price and compliance work.
  • Overheads and back office (central costs about £60m plus divisional overheads, size not disclosed): the main source of retained savings; the Target Operating Model, margin enhancement (£25m in FY26) and Project PRIO target this layer; Marlowe duplication supplies the £30m synergy target (Salford Quays closure, about 300 staff departures).
  • Property and fleet: small (fleet 76% electrified; Marlowe added 1,900 fossil-fuel vehicles).

For the model. Assume continued gross savings of about £25m a year, with about £10m to £15m a year retained in margin after inflation and client sharing, plus Marlowe synergies to £30m by FY28.

C-78

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

C62

Judgement. There is plausible scope to lift the group margin from 4.7% to about 5.5% over three to four years and to grow organically at 4% to 6%, from a few big areas supported by many small ones, and it is congruent with the strategy. Peer precedents show margins of 5.5% to 6% are achievable for integrated facility service companies (ISS targets 5.5% to 6.0% by 2028 from 5.0%; Serco runs about 6%), but they also show such gains take years and are often driven by pruning (Securitas).

The big areas. (1) Mix: compliance (6% to 15% EBIT margins) rising toward £1bn to £1.5bn of revenue and projects toward £2bn at margins above 9%, against a core at about 4%. (2) Marlowe synergies, £30m by FY28 (Deutsche Bank: +60 basis points). (3) Cross-sell and share of wallet: over 80% of accounts do not buy compliance from Mitie; integrated services reach only 40% of the largest customers; a £1.5bn wallet opportunity in the top 50.

The many small ones. Automation, procurement, self-delivery, telecoms recovery, Spain, data centres, water.

Caution. Revenue synergies from compliance cross-sell have been slower than cost synergies; the Wisdom Library's test ("linked businesses are not a conglomerate") asks whether a client buys compliance from Mitie because of the bundle or simply because Mitie is already on site; that is not yet proven.

C-79

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

C63

Judgement. Geographically narrow, functionally broad, and widening slightly. The UK is 93.8% of revenue (Spain about 4%, Ireland about 1%, small United States dollar revenue from a Diego Garcia joint venture, a new Nordic foothold); currency effects are immaterial (a 10% euro move shifts revenue about £29m and EBITDA by less than £0.1m). Functionally there are two divisions but roughly ten business lines (security, hygiene and landscapes, facilities compliance, central government, immigration and justice, Spain, engineering, defence, healthcare, local government and education, projects), across 3,000 core clients with none above 10% of revenue. The perimeter has widened with Marlowe and the bolt-ons and would widen sharply under OCS.

Predictability. High at the aggregate level (UK only, dispersed clients, recurring contracts, no currency noise) and lower at the segment level, where contract wins and losses, project phasing and the public-private mix move results; the lack of contract-level disclosure (the Risks report notes contract-by-contract attrition is hidden in consolidated reporting) limits forecasting precision.

Company - Management

C6511 questions
C-5566C-5662C-5775C-57b60C-5860C-5950C-6062C-6174C-6258C-6368C-6470

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Management quality is the strongest foundation of the standalone case. Phil Bentley's record is one of action and delivery in difficult conditions: a turnaround, a large integration that nearly doubled its synergy target, every target of the previous plan met, the current revenue target met a year early, cash well ahead of guidance, and buybacks at low multiples, followed by a sale at the top of the valuation range. Governance is modern and shareholder-oriented, with strong executive ownership and pay discipline, marred only by a large retention award and completion bonuses. The weaknesses are a lean frontline culture (engagement 63%, payroll and mobilisation failures) and the absence of an internal successor, which led to the sale. For Set 2 the management record justifies leaning forecasts toward guidance being met or beaten in FY27 (a floor of 5% margin and £150m of free cash flow), but it also means that in a break case the forecasts lose their most reliable support: Bentley would be leaving and his team dispersing, so the delivery record cannot be extrapolated beyond FY27 without a discount.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision, particularly in relation to peers?

C66

Question asked How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

Judgement. Ambition under Phil Bentley has been large relative to UK peers and expressed in action: a turnaround from an accounting crisis (2016-2017) into the UK's largest facilities company, a strategic pivot from "facilities management" to "facilities transformation", the largest compliance acquisition in the UK (Marlowe), a £2bn projects ambition (raised from £1.5bn), a £1bn to £1.5bn compliance ambition, an enterprise-wide agentic artificial intelligence programme, and growth delivered ahead of plan (£1.2bn of revenue added in two years of a three-year plan). Peers' ambitions over the same period were defensive (Capita retreating; Sodexo resetting; Serco steady), with ISS the closest match. The final expression of ambition was to sell the company to a private buyer that intends to build an £8.5bn international group; for listed shareholders the vision now ends at 218.5p.

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities: widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

C62

Question asked How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

Judgement. The strategy is smart and broadly right, bold in its value-chain pivot, and only moderately dynamic. Right priorities: move into higher-margin, regulation-driven and technical work; raise self-delivery to capture subcontractor margin; use technology to lower cost to serve and to win tenders; deepen integration in key accounts. It widens the moat where the moat is real (density, data, compliance). It prioritises growth (high single-digit revenue CAGR target, delivered early). Its weakness is resilience to a plausible adverse environment: 45% to 50% of revenue remains UK public, and the strategy has no explicit answer to an insourcing turn beyond being the best-run provider; Marlowe's small-client base also tests a culture built for large accounts. A strategy that can capitalise on most environments would have reduced the public concentration; the sale to OCS, with its international ambitions, partly does that for the new owner.

C-57

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture?

B75

Question asked How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

Judgement. Phil Bentley is a very good chief executive judged by actions, close to the "animal chief executive" archetype in delivery and alignment, with a generalist's blind spots in frontline operations.

Actions. Took over in November 2016 after profit warnings and an accounting review (net assets later cut by £272m to £90m); delivered Project Helix (£45m of savings) and disposals (social housing, catering, pest control); bought Interserve facilities management for £190m in 2020 and delivered about £55m of synergies against a £30m target; raised the margin from 2.3% (FY21) to 4.7% (FY24) and met or beat every target of the FY22-FY24 plan; grew revenue from about £2.1bn (FY17) to £5.6bn (FY26); turned telecoms around within two years; bought Marlowe and is integrating it ahead of plan on costs; returned £303m by buybacks since FY23 at prices well below the offer; and negotiated a 47% premium cash exit. Prior record: British Gas (operating costs cut 9% in 2008; services profit +29%), Cable & Wireless Communications (sold to Liberty Global, 2016).

Alignment. Over 18 million shares (2,497% of salary); bought 195,000 shares in November 2025; exercised and held options rather than selling.

Blind spots. Engagement of 63%, payroll errors, union disputes and the HMP Millsike mobilisation failure suggest frontline execution received less attention than cost and technology; margin stalled in FY25-FY26 (an exceptional cost shock is the main reason); chief executive succession was not resolved before he chose to sell; a one-off 600% salary long-term incentive award was needed to defer his retirement.

C-57b

How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team the chief executive has built?

C60

Question asked How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team the chief executive has built (an enabler)?

Judgement. Hiring has been a moderate priority and the team is good but thin at the top. Simon Kirkpatrick (finance director since April 2021, previously Finance Director at Mitie and a Balfour Beatty finance executive) provides strong balance-sheet and cash discipline, though working-capital days are worse than peers and other items have risen. Jason Towse (Business Services) gives continuity; Sam White (Technical Services, from Costain, December 2025) has made an early commercial impact (70% of the FY27 sales target secured); Kathryn Dolan (Chief People Officer, from Bureau Veritas) joined in June 2025; a 70-strong digital team runs PRIO. Against that: Alice Woodwark left to run VP plc; no internal successor to the chief executive emerged, so the board hired Egon Zehnder; and the takeover will replace both the chief executive and the finance director with OCS executives. A chief executive who prioritised hiring well would have left an obvious successor.

C-58

How well resourced and invested is the whole business?

C60

Question asked How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

Judgement. Well resourced in technology, systems and balance sheet; less well resourced at the frontline. Investment: £200m in technology since 2017 (Mozaic360 on Microsoft Fabric, IBM Maximo MAS 9.1, Coupa, a security operations centre, over 1,000 cleaning robots), £20m to £25m for PRIO in FY27, £5.7m in apprentices with 90% apprentice retention over three years, comfortable leverage (covenant 0.82x) and long-dated debt. Under-resourced: frontline pay is compressed (median employee base salary £27,040; 25th percentile £24,905), engagement is 63%, payroll processes failed on transferred NHS contracts, HMP Millsike opened understaffed, and technical labour is scarce. The business is built for efficiency more than for resilience (in the Wisdom Library's "ant colony" sense, it is lean rather than buffered).

C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

C50

Question asked How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

Judgement. Partially congruent. Goals, strategy, incentives and capital allocation are well aligned at the top (long-term incentives weighted equally across EPS, return on invested capital and revenue; 80% of the annual bonus on financial measures; heavy executive ownership; a disciplined bidding process). The culture is cost-focused, commercial and technology-led. The congruence breaks at the frontline: a 63% engagement score against a 75% benchmark, payroll errors, a refusal to pay a £1,655 Covid recovery payment to transferred NHS cleaners (UNISON contrasted its £70,000 cost with ten hours of the chief executive's pay), redundancy disputes, and a mobilisation failure at a prison. A company that is the largest accredited member of the Good Business Charter but has union disputes over payslips is not the Lifco or Ryanair kind of total-system congruence.

C-60

How clear, straightforward and helpful is the company's communication, including on the strategy and the driver transmission mechanism?

C62

Judgement. Clear and helpful on the main transmission mechanisms, less so on contract-level detail and on adjusted measures. Strengths: Bentley's direct style ("shameful and awful" about the legacy sales model); explicit inflation bridges (gross inflation £121.5m, employer National Insurance £48.5m, recovery rates of 94% and 76%, net residuals); explicit FY27 building blocks (net inflation £10m to £12m, PRIO £20m to £25m, synergies £15m and £30m, the Department for Work and Pensions headwind £15m, telecoms £10m); clear targets. Weaknesses: heavy reliance on adjusted measures with a 106% gap to basic EPS; scripted remarks glossed over contract losses (Defra) that emerged only in questions; Technical Services' decline was underplayed in slides; contract-level attrition and gross margins by contract are not disclosed; the timing gap between wage rises and indexation is not stated.

C-61

How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, whatever the environment?

C74

Question asked How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

Judgement. Strong. Recent: the £1.2bn revenue target delivered a year early; free cash flow of £162m against a £120m guide; margin held at 4.7% through a £170m gross cost hurdle; Marlowe synergies ahead of plan; telecoms fixed; a £100m buyback launched (then suspended for the offer). Mid-term: every target of the FY22-FY24 plan met or exceeded, including the 4.7% margin; FY24 EBIT guidance of £190m beaten by £20m; seven consecutive consensus profit upgrades after the October 2023 Capital Markets Event; Interserve synergies nearly double the target. This was delivered through the pandemic, a high-inflation period and a large statutory cost shock, i.e. in a tougher-than-normal environment. The only blemishes are execution failures on individual public contracts and the margin stall in FY25-FY26.

C-62

To what extent does the company hold outlier standards, driving higher, faster and better, with a record of out-executing competitors?

C58

Judgement. Mitie out-executes UK peers on growth, cash and technology, but not on margin or frontline quality, so its standards are high but not outlier. It grew organically at 5.3% in FY26 against a UK market of 2% to 3%, Serco's 1% and Sodexo's 1.2% to 1.5%; it leads UK peers in automation; its free cash flow beat was large. But its margin (4.7%) trails Serco (about 6%) and ISS (5.0% to 5.25%), its debtor days (31) trail Serco (16), its renewal rate dipped to 59% in FY25 while Compass holds 96%, and its public mobilisation record includes a serious failure.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is: what does the evidence show, and why?

C68

Question asked Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

Judgement. The evidence shows a management that has largely put shareholders first and a board with prudent, modern governance, with two notable exceptions in pay.

Shareholders first.

  • Ownership: the chief executive holds over 18 million shares (2,497% of salary, far above a 200% norm); aggregate insider holdings 1.4% against a 0.8% sector median; open-market purchase of 195,000 shares in November 2025.
  • Pay restraint: the remuneration committee applied 7.5% negative discretion to the 2021 Enhanced Delivery Plan despite formulaic targets being met; management asked for the FY26 bonus to be cut to 31% of maximum (a 45 percentage point reduction) because of workforce cost pressures; malus and clawback (two years for bonus, five years for long-term incentives); post-employment shareholding of 100% of the guideline for one year.
  • Capital: buybacks of £303m since FY23 at prices well below 218.5p; a progressive dividend; the sale achieved a 47% premium and 15 times FY27 earnings, 27% above international blue-collar service peers on Deutsche Bank's measure.

Exceptions. A one-off 600% of salary long-term incentive award (£5.4m) in FY25 to defer the chief executive's retirement, against a 200% norm; cash retention bonuses of 150% of salary for top executives on completion of the offer. Both reward completion and continuity; neither is egregious relative to the value created, but both show the board paying to manage key-person risk it had not solved by succession.

Governance scaffolding. Single class of shares, one vote each; seven of nine directors independent; fully independent audit, remuneration and nomination committees; the Audit and Risk Committee strengthened in January 2026; COSO-based control testing; a competitive audit tender from FY28; independent whistleblowing; related-party transactions immaterial (£0.4m a year to the Mitie Foundation); past Financial Conduct Authority, Financial Reporting Council and Competition and Markets Authority investigations closed without action. One soft spot: a non-executive director staying beyond nine years on a comply-or-explain basis.

C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

C70

Judgement. Mitie under Bentley has been a good, opportunistic capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser at low multiples. Evidence: £303m of buybacks since FY23 when the shares traded at about 8 to 11 times earnings (FY26: 38 million shares at an average 166p, against the 218.5p offer); disposals of low-margin units (social housing, catering, pest control); Interserve at £190m with about £55m of synergies (a very high return); small bolt-ons at sensible prices; a 33% dividend payout; leverage kept inside 0.75x to 1.5x; and an exit at the top of the valuation range. The one large question is Marlowe: strategically sound, but bought at a price that cut group return on invested capital from 24.5% to 18.1% and, on my estimate, earns only about 10% to 12% post-tax by FY28 even with full synergies.

Risks

❌ D424 questions
C-8048C-8142C-8250C-8335

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The risk profile is dominated by one binary: completion of the OCS offer (about 3.7% upside to the 218.5p cash) against a break (about 25% to 30% downside to standalone fair value, about 40% to a trough). The probability of a break is low on the evidence (shareholder approval secured, combined UK share below 20%, committed financing) and the market prices it at roughly 6% to 12%. The fundamental risks of the standalone business are real but slow: an organic engine that has slowed to 4% (partly price, partly lapping one-offs), a public book exposed to insourcing from FY28, recurring other items and drifting working capital, and tail risks in public-contract execution, accounting judgement and cyber security. The Risks report's conclusion that the "false friend" profile (headline growth masking cash erosion) best fits the evidence should be discounted: the Wisdom Library warns that the vaguest hypothesis tends to win a fewest-inconsistencies contest, and Mitie's leverage (1.2x) and cash conversion (about 100% of brokers'-basis net income in FY26) do not resemble true false friends such as Green Landscaping (28% conversion, 3.4x leverage). For Set 2: in the downside case model a deal break with standalone forecasts on a lower multiple and a 12-month leadership gap; in the standalone base case, model the public renewal haircut from FY28 and a recurring cash cost for other items.

C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

❌ D48

Judgement. Yes, the core engine has decelerated: organic growth fell from about 8% in Q1 FY26 to 4% from Q2 FY26 onward, below Mitie's recent norm (FY24-FY25 were boosted by surge contracts; the FY25-FY27 plan targets high single-digit revenue CAGR including acquisitions) and below what the reported 10% growth suggests. The input forces are mixed: the price input is slowing (wage-led indexation weaker as the National Living Wage rise falls from 6.7% to 4.1%; Q1 FY27 price contribution about 2%), while the volume input is strengthening (order intake +33%, renewals 91%, the pipeline at a record £32.5bn, Technical Services sales +150%). So the deceleration is real but partly a price effect and partly lapping of one-off contracts (surge security, the Department for Work and Pensions completion, telecoms exits), rather than a collapse of demand. Technical Services (-5% in Q1 FY27) is the genuine weak spot.

For the model. Do not extrapolate the 10% reported growth; the engine runs at about 4% organic, with a plausible but unproven lift to 4.5% to 5% in FY28.

C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

❌ D42

Judgement. Five such risks matter; the first dominates because of the offer.

  1. Deal break (prohibition or onerous remedies by the Competition and Markets Authority, or another failure to complete). Plausibility low: shareholders approved; Deutsche Bank judges the combined UK share below 20% and intervention risk limited; financing is committed under UK Takeover Code rules. The market implies roughly 6% to 12%. Magnitude large: Deutsche Bank expects the shares to fall back toward the undisturbed 151p (about -28%); other sources give 140p to 160p. A break would also leave Mitie without a chief executive succession and with suspended buybacks.
  2. Operational failure on a sensitive public contract (prisons, immigration and justice, a nuclear site such as AWE, airport or rail security, NHS cleaning). Plausibility low to moderate (HMP Millsike shows it can happen). Magnitude: penalties, termination and reputational spillover, plausibly £10m to £30m of profit and a political catalyst for insourcing; the Capita pension case (£25m to £40m) is the precedent.
  3. Accounting or provisioning surprise. Plausibility low (balance sheet cleaned since 2017; audit and risk committee strengthened), but the growth of percentage-of-completion projects, £10.8m of contract dispute provisions and Mitie's own 2016-2017 history (a £60.5m write-down, net assets cut to £90m) mean it cannot be ruled out. Magnitude high for credibility.
  4. Cyber attack on operational technology and artificial intelligence platforms (700+ connected sites; 140 client estates). Plausibility low to moderate; the Earnings Review calls it the risk most absent from sell-side models; ISS's 2020 attack cost about DKK 300m to rebuild systems.
  5. Abrupt policy decision to insource building management or security across central government. Plausibility low within 12 months, higher over three years; magnitude potentially a material share of the public book.
C-82

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

C50

Judgement. While the offer stands, sentiment risk is capped: the price is anchored to 218.5p and moves only with perceived completion risk. The sentiment risks that could still hit the price are: (1) headlines from the Competition and Markets Authority (a Phase 2 referral or a remedies request would widen the spread sharply, perhaps by 5% to 10%, even without a break); (2) a peer failure or political announcement on insourcing that makes regulators or the government more hostile to a large combined public outsourcer; (3) in a break case, the full market regime: Fragility is rated Double Underweight, the discount-rate shock refuses re-ratings, UK small and mid caps are out of favour, and a broken bid typically trades below its undisturbed level for a time; (4) tighter leveraged-finance markets (the first credit crack in the artificial intelligence chain) reducing the chance of an alternative bidder after a break.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

❌ D35

Judgement. If the deal completes, the shares do not fall: holders receive 218.5p (3.7% above 210.8p). If it breaks, the fair standalone value is about 150p to 160p and a fair trough about 125p to 130p, i.e. falls of about 25% to 30% to fair value and about 40% to a trough. Weighted by a low break probability the expected downside is a few per cent, but the payoff is asymmetric: about 4% to 5% of upside against about 25% to 40% of downside.

Arithmetic (24-month-forward consensus EPS 17.45p).

  • At the ten-year median 24-month-forward P/E of 9.1x: 159p (-25%).
  • At the 35th percentile ("conservative", 8.5x): 148p (-30%).
  • At the 10th percentile ("crash", 7.3x): 127p (-40%).
  • Jefferies' stressed case (zero organic growth, margin 4.8%, 8x P/E, the low of its ten-year range): 115p (-45%).
  • The undisturbed price was about 151p after a 16.6% fall in the three months before the bid.

Why the trough would sit low in a break. A break would combine a leadership vacuum, suspended buybacks, a regime that refuses re-ratings, the Public Interest Test approaching, and the overhang of arbitrage holders selling; the sector has re-rated (facility outsourcers +21.9% in 12 months; ISS +44%), which argues for the upper end (150p to 160p) rather than the trough.
