The Investment Analyst's closing notes for this layer

Closing steer for the Consumer (not a question)

  1. Build two cases. The completion case needs no forecasts beyond confirming that nothing undermines regulatory clearance before calendar Q1 2027. The standalone case (needed for the break downside and to judge the offer) should be built from the FY26 base on the brokers' basis.
  2. Standalone base-case anchors from these answers: organic growth about 4% in FY27 and 4.5% to 5% in FY28-FY29 (price about 2%, volume the rest); reported FY27 growth about 7% with acquisitions; operating margin 5.0% to 5.1% in FY27, about 5.3% in FY28 (Marlowe synergies £30m, telecoms) and about 5.4% to 5.5% in FY29 only if no new statutory cost and public renewals hold; interest £30m to £34m; tax about 25% plus about £10m of minorities; shares about 1.30bn to 1.31bn with no buyback; free cash flow conversion 80% to 85% of adjusted net income, with recurring other items of about £25m to £30m a year in cash and PRIO £20m to £25m in FY27 deducted explicitly; capital expenditure about £35m to £40m; projects working capital about £25m a year.
  3. Downside case: deal break with a 12-month leadership gap, a valuation of 8.5x to 9.1x 24-month-forward EPS (about 148p to 159p) and a trough at about 7.3x (about 127p); from FY28 a renewal-rate haircut on public cleaning and security contracts.
  4. The weakest foundations to test in Set 2 are the cleanliness of earnings (a 106% gap between adjusted and basic EPS), the working-capital drift, and the true incremental return on Marlowe; the strongest are management's delivery record and the asset-light cash generation.
Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: MITIE Group PLC (MTO-GB), the UK's largest facilities services company (FY26 revenue £5,619m, year to 31 March 2026), reporting two divisions: Business Services (security, hygiene and landscapes, facilities compliance including the Marlowe business acquired in August 2025, central government, immigration and justice, Spain) and Technical Services (engineering maintenance, Facilities Transformation projects, defence, healthcare, local government and education); subject to a recommended cash offer from CD&R-backed OCS Group announced on 21 July 2026.

Request: T-P1

Peers: ISS A/S (largest listed integrated facility services peer, self-delivery model, recent cost and margin programme); Serco Group (UK public services outsourcer with indexed contracts and a strong cash record); Sodexo (facilities and catering group in a multi-year margin rebuild); Compass Group (margin-first contract caterer with disclosed volume-to-profit sensitivity); Securitas (guarding and technology mix shift with portfolio pruning and a European transformation programme); Capita (UK public outsourcer with repeated restructuring programmes); Coor Service Management (Nordic integrated facility services, subcontractor-based model); OCS Group (private UK facilities services competitor and the acquirer of Mitie); Babcock International (UK public-sector support services turnaround precedent); Interserve and Carillion (historical UK precedents of cost and contract failure). Inputs: What share of Mitie's cost base is variable at contract level (front-line staff transferring with contracts, subcontract and materials) and what share is fixed or semi-fixed (central costs, divisional overheads, technology, the self-delivered engineering field force), and how has the shift to self-delivery under the "Mitie First" initiative changed that split? How did Mitie's operating margin move with volume in past swings: the pandemic surge and its unwinding (FY21 to FY23), the Interserve integration (from November 2020), and the inflation and employer National Insurance shock of FY25 and FY26, with dated figures? For each Mitie cost programme, what was promised, what was delivered, at what cost to achieve and over what time: Project Helix (from 2017, £45m of savings per the company), the Interserve integration (target £30m of synergies; about £55m delivered per the company), the Target Operating Model (cumulative cost £70.7m to March 2026, due to complete by March 2027), the margin enhancement programme (£25m of savings in FY26), and the Marlowe integration (cost synergies £7m in FY26, £15m run-rate targeted for FY27, £30m targeted for FY28; integration budget £27m)? How much of each programme's savings stayed in operating margin rather than being passed to clients through cost-plus contracts, re-tenders or price concessions, or being absorbed by wage inflation? What does Project PRIO (agentic artificial intelligence with McKinsey and QuantumBlack; £20m to £25m of upfront cost in FY27 per the company) target, on which cost lines, and what have peers retained from similar automation (ISS's reported working-hour savings; Serco's live artificial intelligence applications)? Which cost lines proved cuttable at Mitie and peers (back office, procurement through Coupa and a preferred supplier list, property such as the Salford Quays office closure, fleet, subcontractor insourcing) and which did not (front-line labour), and why? Do restructuring and transformation costs reported below adjusted operating profit recur year after year at Mitie (other items of £73m in FY25 and £113m in FY26 per the company) and at peers, and should they be treated as operating costs? Focus: How much of Mitie's past and planned cost savings (margin enhancement, Marlowe synergies, Project PRIO) has stayed, and will stay, in operating margin rather than being shared with clients or absorbed by inflation, and how fixed the cost base really is, so that the margin path from FY27 to FY30 can be modelled under rising and falling volumes.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Serco Group (UK public services, highly indexed contracts, absorbed employer National Insurance costs in 2026); ISS A/S (indexation on most contracts, scope reductions instead of price concessions); Securitas (guarding pass-through of labour cost inflation, technology pricing); Sodexo (price-to-win history and reset); Compass Group (dynamic contract repricing structures); Capita (pass-through clauses on about two-thirds of contracts per its annual report); Coor Service Management (Nordic indexation); OCS Group (private competitor and acquirer); G4S/Allied Universal and Corps Security (UK manned guarding competitors); PTSG and Churches Fire (private UK fire and compliance competitors to Marlowe). Operating conditions: Statutory employer cost increases that standard indexation clauses may not cover (the employer National Insurance rise from April 2025; National Living Wage rises of 6.7% in April 2025 and 4.1% in April 2026 to £12.71; the Employment Rights Act 2025; the Two-Tier Workforce Code returning from October 2026). Wage inflation running above headline CPI, and the reverse. UK public-sector budget pressure and the insourcing assessment required by the Public Interest Test for public contracts above £1m from April 2027. Contract renewal and re-tender points, as against mid-term indexation. Energy and fuel price spikes. A private-sector downturn with office estate downsizing. Automation and artificial intelligence savings that clients may claim. A change of ownership to private equity. Inputs: How fully and how quickly does Mitie recover cost increases by contract type (cost-plus, indexed fixed price, unindexed fixed price, gain-share), given the company's disclosed recovery of 97% of wage inflation in FY24, 95% in FY25 and 94% in FY26, and 76% (£36.8m of £48.5m) of the FY26 employer National Insurance rise (FY26 results, 4 June 2026)? What is the lag between a wage rise and the indexation reset, and what share of contracts lacks indexation (Jefferies has cited about 10%)? Were recovery rates achieved partly through scope reductions rather than price? Is there evidence of real price increases, above cost inflation, being achieved and held in facilities compliance (Marlowe's evergreen, small-value testing and inspection contracts) or in self-delivered projects, and what churn followed? What evidence is there of price given back at renewal: the contracts Mitie lost on price (Defra and Rolls-Royce are cited in research), the central government facilities contract completed in H1 FY26, and the swing in Mitie's renewal rate from 59% in FY25 to 84% in FY26 and 91% in Q1 FY27 (company disclosures); how much of the FY25 low was deliberate non-renewal of low-margin contracts? How are statutory cost changes handled in UK central government frameworks, and how did peers recover the April 2025 National Insurance rise? How do clients share automation savings at renewal under each contract type? How might the Public Interest Test change pricing leverage on public cleaning and security contracts? What do clients, competitors, industry experts and the sell-side say about facilities services pricing now? Focus: Whether Mitie has any pricing power beyond inflation pass-through, particularly in compliance and projects, and how completely and quickly it recovers wage and statutory cost increases under each contract type, including how this changes at renewal and under the Public Interest Test.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Mitie's margin and renewal outlook for FY27 to FY30 depends on competing loops (scale, technology and route density reinforcing its position; client savings claims, statutory costs and public insourcing balancing it), and their relative strength is not yet clear from the evidence. Peers: ISS A/S (scale and technology-led self-delivery); Serco Group (UK public services exposed to the same political cycle); Capita (precedent of delivery failures leading to insourcing); Sodexo and Compass Group (scale and purchasing loops in contract services); Securitas (technology substitution for manned guarding); Coor Service Management (Nordic density); OCS Group (private consolidator and acquirer); PTSG and Andwis (private-equity-backed compliance consolidators). Inputs: Scale funds technology (Mitie reports £200m of technology investment since 2017, Mozaic360 used by over 140 clients, IBM Maximo, Project PRIO), which lowers cost to serve, wins tenders and adds scale and data. Route density: adding compliance visits from Marlowe to existing engineering routes lowers cost per visit and supports more competitive bids; over 80% of Mitie accounts did not buy compliance services from it at the time of the acquisition (company). Self-delivery replaces subcontractors, captures margin and funds more engineers. Integrated contracts deepen data and workflow lock-in, raising renewals and cross-sell (Mitie reports integrated services to about 40% of its largest customers and a £1.5bn share-of-wallet opportunity in its top 50 clients). Balancing: clients claim automation savings at once on cost-plus contracts and at re-tender on fixed-price contracts. Balancing: outsourcer delivery failures (Capita's Civil Service Pension Scheme contract; the HMP Millsike inspection findings of September 2026) feed insourcing rhetoric and the Public Interest Test, reducing public contracts. Balancing: statutory labour costs not covered by indexation squeeze margins and bid prices. Labour loop: frontline pay errors and disputes (UNISON, GMB) leading to service failures and contract losses. Consolidation loop: low listed multiples attracting private equity bids, reducing listed competitors and changing bidding behaviour. Focus: Which loop is strongest for Mitie over FY27 to FY30, the scale, technology and density reinforcing loop or the balancing loops of client savings claims, statutory labour costs and public-sector insourcing, and what dated evidence shows each operating now.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Mitie's earnings per share depend heavily on items between adjusted operating profit and EPS (recurring other items, interest, tax, minorities, share count and differences in EPS definitions) and on the timing of Marlowe synergies and Project PRIO costs, which current disclosures leave unclear. Peers: Serco Group (UK outsourcer with low adjustments and strong cash conversion); ISS A/S (EPS growth from buybacks and margin, factoring use); Capita (UK outsourcer with large restructuring charges); Securitas (items affecting comparability from transformation programmes); Sodexo (adjusted versus reported earnings in a reset); Babcock International (UK support services earnings quality). Inputs: The reconciliation of EPS definitions: Mitie's FY26 adjusted basic EPS of 13.6p (company) against Deutsche Bank's adjusted EPS of 12.6p and basic EPS of 6.6p, and what each excludes. The composition and likely path of other items (£73m in FY25, £113m in FY26 per the company, including £42m of amortisation of acquired intangibles, £27m of restructuring and £22m of Marlowe transaction costs) over FY27 to FY30, including Project PRIO (£20m to £25m in FY27), the remaining Marlowe integration cost (budget £27m), the completion of the Target Operating Model by March 2027, and OCS transaction costs. The phasing of Marlowe cost synergies (£7m in FY26; £15m run-rate targeted for FY27; £30m targeted for FY28), revenue dis-synergies from churn of small compliance clients, and the effect of about 300 Marlowe staff leaving in Q1 FY27. Net finance costs (£16.2m in FY25 and £27.7m in FY26 per the company), the debt structure (£180m of US private placement notes maturing to 2034; a £250m revolving credit facility maturing in October 2028), lease interest, the current use of invoice discounting (historically up to £140m), and the disclosed sensitivity of £1.2m per 50 basis points. The adjusted effective tax rate against the reported 26.9% in FY26, and minority interests. The share count (1,301,692,145 voting rights in September 2026) and dilution from share schemes vesting on a change of control, given the buyback suspended in July 2026. The working capital needs of the projects business (Jefferies has cited about £25m a year), the Procurement Act effect (£8m in FY26 per the company), debtor days (29 in FY25, 31 in FY26), and the definition of Mitie's free cash flow (treatment of lease payments and cash restructuring costs). Margin inputs management has guided for FY27: the central government contract demobilisation (£15m headwind), the telecoms recovery (about £10m) and net inflation of £10m to £12m. How far apart management guidance and the assumptions of Jefferies, Deutsche Bank, Investec and Berenberg are on each of these. Focus: What Mitie's clean, recurring earnings per share and free cash flow are once recurring other items, interest, tax, minorities and share dilution are fully counted, and how they are likely to evolve from FY27 to FY30 compared with the brokers' adjusted EPS.

Layer 2

Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

9 questions in 1 group · 13,909 words · written 24 October 2026, 02:05 to 02:31 UK, Fable, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
MITIE Group PLC (MTO-GB): Set 2 answers, the operating and financial model for the next 18 months to four years

Written 24-Oct-2026 by the Investment Analyst for Set 2, for the Investment Analyst answering Sets 3 and 4 (predictability of the forecasts; guidance, consensus and narrative).

Preliminary: the frame, the bases, and where I differ from the first set

The frame. The standalone model below is the value a holder falls back to if the recommended cash offer from OCS Group (218.5p plus the 3.1p FY26 final dividend) breaks, and it is the yardstick for judging whether 218.5p is fair. The market prices roughly a 6% to 12% chance of a break (price 210.8p on 21-Oct-2026 against 218.5p cash; completion expected in calendar Q1 2027 after Competition and Markets Authority clearance). I build the standalone case honestly, as the first set asked, and I do not let the offer price anchor it. Every figure is on the company's basis unless stated: revenue as reported; operating profit before other items (which I call EBIT); EBITDA before other items on the brokers' basis (FY26 £360m); adjusted EPS before other items, after minorities, on basic weighted shares (FY26 13.6p). Money is in pounds sterling millions.

The three bases the Consumer must keep apart.

  1. EBIT and EBITDA. Company FY26 EBIT before other items £264.1m (4.7%); brokers' basis EBITDA £360m (6.4%); FactSet's history lines (operating EBITDA £337m, adjusted EBIT £200m) deduct items the company excludes and must not be compared with consensus. Depreciation and amortisation on the brokers' basis is £96m (360 less 264), excluding £41.5m of amortisation of acquired intangibles, which sits in other items.
  2. EPS. The company's 13.6p (FY26) is adjusted attributable profit of about £171m (EBIT £264.1m, less net finance costs £27.7m, less tax at the 24.5% effective rate on profit before other items, less minorities £7.7m) over about 1,256m weighted basic shares. FactSet's and Deutsche Bank's FY26 "actual" of 12.6p is, on my reconstruction, the same profit over a fully diluted count of about 1,350m to 1,370m shares (FactSet shows 1,372m diluted shares). This matters for Set 4: the FY27 consensus of 14.5p (median of three brokers; Jefferies 14.6p, Investec 14.4p, Deutsche Bank 14.2p) rests on about 1,310m to 1,340m shares, because the share schemes that were only potential dilution in FY26 have since been exercised (voting rights 1,301.7m in September 2026 against FactSet's 1,255m at March 2026). The headline "+15% consensus EPS growth" therefore compares a diluted FY26 base with a post-dilution FY27 count; on the company's basic basis the like-for-like growth to consensus is about +7% to +10%, and the roughly 7% dilution from the vesting of about 80m shares is a real FY27 EPS headwind that the brokers' basis had already absorbed. This is my inference from the share counts, not a stated fact; I treat it as probable.
  3. Free cash flow. I use the company's definition throughout: after interest, cash tax, capital spending including intangibles, lease capital repayments and the cash cost of other items (restructuring, integration, deal costs), before dividends, acquisitions and buybacks. FY26 on this definition was £162m. The Financial Analyst's model computes cash flow from the adjusted EBITDA I give it and has no line for the cash cost of other items, so its free cash flow will sit above mine by that cost (about £60m in FY27, £25m to £30m a year after); I explain this in the forecast-inputs form so the flag the one-page summary raises is understood.

Where I differ from the first set.

  • Project Helix. The first set records £45m of savings delivered under Project Helix (2017 onward). Jefferies (2-Aug-2020, cited in the operating-leverage report) records £45m promised, £30m delivered, and none of it retained in margin because it was reinvested in customer rate-card concessions and frontline incentives. I use Jefferies' version: the earliest programme was handed to clients, which is the pattern the Sector Digest describes, and the retention record only improved with Interserve (about £55m delivered against a £30m target, largely retained because it was supplier renegotiation and property and IT consolidation).
  • FY27 free cash flow. The first set leaned to £150m to £165m. I lean to about £135m after the cash costs of the OCS transaction (my estimate about £13m) and Project PRIO (£20m to £25m, reported below the adjusted line but cash), with about £150m excluding the transaction costs. The £150m guidance is attainable on the company's own definition only if working capital is neutral; the first set itself documented an adverse working-capital trend and a timing benefit in FY26.
  • Buybacks. The first set modelled no buyback, to match consensus. The standalone model only matters in a break, and in a break a company with 1.1x leverage, a 0.75x to 1.5x target range, an authorised but suspended £100m programme and an 11% active shareholder would very probably resume repurchases within a year. I include £50m in FY28 and £75m a year from FY29, half to three-quarters of the pre-offer pace, and I state the EPS effect separately so the Consumer can remove it.
  • The EPS base for comparison with consensus (point 2 above): the first set advised modelling from 12.6p; I advise modelling from 13.6p on the company's basic basis and converting to the brokers' diluted basis only when comparing with the FactSet median.
  • The FY27 bridge. The first set's indicative bridge reached £290m to £305m. Mine reaches £304m, because I treat the £22m of margin-enhancement savings that Deutsche Bank models as falling through in full: FY27 is the first year since FY24 in which no new statutory cost shock arrives to consume the annual self-help. That single observation is the reason the margin can step up in FY27 after two flat years, and the Autumn Budget of November 2026 is the event that could re-impose a shock on FY28.

Financial forecasts

C569 questions
P-0156P-0266P-0354P-0444P-0550P-0662P-0858P-0957P-1058

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The forecasts describe a business whose profit lines improve steadily and whose growth line slows: an operating margin rising from 4.7% to about 5.5% over four years, EBIT compounding at about 8%, adjusted EPS at about 10% (8.5% without buybacks), statutory EPS converging toward adjusted, free cash flow rising from a FY27 trough of about £135m to over £200m, and leverage falling below 1x while dividends, bolt-ons and buybacks are all funded. Set against that, reported revenue growth halves from 10% to under 5% as Marlowe annualises, organic growth drifts from about 4.2% toward 3.3% as the price element fades and public renewals meet the Public Interest Test, and the margin gain is modest in absolute terms and half mechanical.

Four judgements tie the answers together. First, the margin can rise in FY27 for one simple reason: it is the first year since FY24 in which the roughly £20m to £25m of annual self-help is not consumed by a statutory cost shock, and the Marlowe synergies and the telecoms drop-out add about £16m on top. That makes the FY27 floor of 5% attainable with modest headroom (my £304m, 5.05%), makes the Autumn Budget of November 2026 the most important external event for the FY28 forecast, and means the margin step is a release of effort already being made rather than a new capability. Second, the business converts efficiency into share and renewals, not into margin: the contract structure passes most savings to clients, so the reinforcing loops of scale, technology and density set a floor under volume and the balancing loops of client claims, statutory costs and insourcing set a ceiling near 5.5%; the forecasts should not extrapolate the FY27 to FY28 margin pace into FY29 to FY31. Third, the below-the-line picture turns favourable from FY28 (interest falling, other items halving, the share count falling), so the quality of earnings improves markedly and the 106% gap between adjusted and statutory EPS, the Industry Digest's named example, narrows into the peer range by FY29; but the recurring cash cost of restructuring (about £25m a year) should be treated as an operating cost, which takes about 1.5p a year off clean EPS. Fourth, the dilution from the vesting of about 80m share options is already in the FY27 share count and explains why the FactSet median's "+15%" EPS growth is about +7% to +12% on the company's basic basis; consensus and my base case agree on the operating path to FY29 and differ on composition, cash and capital allocation.

For the Consumer's purposes in Set 3: the cone is narrow for 18 months (FY27 EBIT £292m to £312m; margin 4.9% to 5.2%; free cash flow £120m to £155m) and widens from FY29 (EBIT plus or minus 10%), with the skew slightly positive on FY27 profit, negative on FY27 cash, symmetric on FY28, and negative on FY29 to FY31 revenue. The drivers of the difference between the next four years and the last six are concentrated: two cost programmes (Marlowe, PRIO), one date (the end of the Target Operating Model in March 2027), one policy (the Public Interest Test) and one calendar (the statutory cost cycle). For Set 4: guidance is a floor that will be met; consensus is a fair anchor on profit, a high anchor on FY27 free cash flow and a low anchor on EPS from FY29 if buybacks resume; and the whole standalone case is the downside of a recommended cash offer at 218.5p that sits about 35% above my standalone fair value of 160p to 165p, so the forecasts matter to a listed holder only if the Competition and Markets Authority breaks the deal.

P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

C56

Judgement. Mitie's operating leverage on volume is low and is rising slowly; its leverage to margin assumptions is high. Over the next 18 months to four years operating leverage will add about 20 to 30 basis points to the operating margin (roughly a third of the roughly 80 basis points of improvement I forecast from 4.7% to 5.5%), will make operating profit grow at about one and a half to two times the rate of revenue (EBIT compound growth of about 8% a year on revenue growth of 4% to 5%), and will do so with a narrow cone of outcomes, because most of the cost base moves with the contracts. Against the last four to six years the shape is reversed: FY21 to FY26 was a period in which revenue grew from £2.5bn to £5.6bn by acquisition and surge work while the margin was built by synergies and pruning (2.3% to 4.7%) and then held flat for three years by cost shocks; FY27 to FY31 will be a period of slower, organic revenue growth in which the margin finally moves because the shocks have passed and the semi-fixed layer is spread over a larger base.

Why the leverage on volume is low. About 60% to 65% of the cost base is front-line labour that transfers with contracts under TUPE and is priced into them; subcontract and materials (over £2bn of spend) follow volume. The fixed or semi-fixed layer is central costs (£55m to £60m), divisional overheads (not disclosed), the technology stack (about £200m invested since 2017; Project PRIO £20m to £25m in FY27) and the self-delivered field force (3,235 mobile engineers plus Marlowe's compliance operatives). On a gross margin of 10.8%, the incremental EBIT margin on an extra pound of contracted revenue is about 7% to 10%, against an average of 4.7%. The caterers show what high leverage looks like: Compass's 1% like-for-like volume change moves its margin by about 20 basis points through a 25% drop-through; Sodexo's by 20 to 30 basis points. Mitie's equivalent is nearer 5 basis points, because a lost contract takes most of its cost with it. Serco, with indexed public contracts, absorbed the 2021 to 2023 inflation spike with no margin degradation, which is the same mechanism in reverse: contract structures that pass cost through also pass volume through.

Why the leverage is rising, and why that is two-sided. Three changes raise the fixed share of costs. First, "Mitie First" self-delivery replaces subcontractors with employed engineers, so a pound of lost project revenue now strands more cost; Coor's analysts (DNB Carnegie, 19-Jan-2026) make the same point about Coor's 75% self-delivery. Second, Marlowe adds a dense regional field force whose economics depend on visits per route; Deutsche Bank's arithmetic that a 10% loss of Marlowe revenue would cost £7.5m of profit at a 25% drop-through shows how much higher the leverage is in compliance than in the group (25% against 7% to 10%). Third, the technology layer is a fixed cost whose return depends on volume through it. The consequence for the model is an asymmetry: in the base case (revenue up 4% to 5% a year) the rising fixed share lifts the margin; in a bear case of flat organic revenue it would remove about 20 to 30 basis points of margin that the FY21 to FY26 structure would not have lost, which is the "downside asymmetry" the Business Foundations report flagged and which I carry into the bear case in P-10.

How it shapes each line item, FY27 to FY31 against FY21 to FY26.

  • Revenue. Operating leverage does not drive revenue, but the cost of carrying the semi-fixed layer is what makes management chase volume: the record pipeline (£32.5bn) and order intake (+33% in Q1 FY27) are partly the field force being kept busy. I model revenue growth of 6.8% in FY27 (organic about 4%, acquisition carry-over about 3 points), then 4.8%, 4.3%, 4.0% and 3.8%, against +15%, +56%, +1%, +13%, +14% and +11% in FY21 to FY26.
  • Gross margin. Flat at about 10.8% to 11.0%: contract-level pricing is pass-through (P-04), so the mix shift to compliance and projects is the only source of gross-margin gain and it is worth about 10 basis points a year.
  • Operating costs below gross profit. This is where the leverage acts. Central costs of about £55m to £60m grow at perhaps 2% a year against revenue at 4% to 5%; divisional overheads grow more slowly than revenue as Marlowe's duplicated functions close (£16.5m of the £30m synergy target is support functions); technology spend stays roughly flat in pounds after PRIO's FY27 peak. I estimate the combined effect at about 8 basis points of margin a year, about 30 basis points over FY27 to FY30.
  • Operating profit and margin. 4.7% (FY26) to 5.05% (FY27), 5.3% (FY28), 5.4% (FY29), 5.5% (FY30) and 5.5% (FY31), i.e. EBIT of about £304m, £333m, £352m, £374m and £390m. Of the roughly 80 basis points of gain, I attribute about 30 to operating leverage on the semi-fixed layer, about 40 to Marlowe synergies and the telecoms drop-out (mechanical), and the balance to mix, net of the inflation and bid-pricing drags that take back about 15 to 20 basis points a year.
  • EBITDA. Brokers' basis margin from 6.4% to 6.75% (FY27), 6.95%, 7.0%, 7.1% and 7.1%.
  • Sensitivity for the Consumer. On FY27 revenue of about £6,000m, a 1% revenue shortfall costs about £5m of EBIT (1.6%) if it is contract attrition and about £8m to £10m (3%) if it is project volume, because projects carry the self-delivered field force. A 50 basis point margin miss costs £30m of EBIT (10%) and about 1.7p of EPS (11%). The range of outcomes is set by margin, not volume, which is why P-03 and P-04 matter more than this question for predictability.

Against peers. ISS's capital markets day (14-Sep-2026) stated that margins "automatically decline over time" from wage and input inflation and that productivity must be generated continuously just to hold them: that is the treadmill on which Mitie's operating leverage runs. ISS targets 5.5% to 6.0% by 2028 from about 5%; Securitas reached 8.0% through technology mix and pruning; Serco runs about 6% on indexed public contracts with a capital-light model (£150m of operating invested capital on a £5bn business). Mitie's 5.5% by FY30 sits within what integrated facility services peers have shown is achievable, and below the specialists.

Persistence and predictability. Structural and persistent while revenue grows; the leverage reverses on a fall in project volume or a cluster of public contract losses. Predictability is high for FY27 (the semi-fixed layer and the synergy actions are known), moderate for FY28, and lower from FY29 when public renewals meet the Public Interest Test.

P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

C66

Judgement. Financial leverage is low, comfortable, fixed-rate and largely unused as an EPS lever in consensus; over the next four years it is a positive, not a risk. Net finance costs peak in FY27 at about £33m (FY26 £27.7m; FY25 £16.2m) because of a full year of Marlowe debt and the first-half buyback, then fall to about £28m by FY31 as net debt drifts down even after the dividends, bolt-ons and buybacks I model. Leverage falls from about 1.2x average net debt to EBITDA (1.37x period-end including leases on FactSet's definition, £444m at March 2026; covenant leverage 0.82x against a 3.0x limit) to about 0.9x by FY31 in my base case, which leaves roughly £100m a year of unused capacity inside the 0.75x to 1.5x target range. The balance sheet's weakness is cosmetic rather than financial: shareholders' equity is thin (consensus £438m at FY27 on £5.6bn of revenue) and tangible equity is negative because the assets are goodwill and intangibles, but covenants are EBITDA-based and the debt is long-dated.

The debt and its cost. £360m of US private placement notes with maturities to 2034 at a weighted average fixed rate of 4.65% (including £180m issued in November 2025 at 5.44% to refinance the Marlowe bridge); a £250m revolving credit facility to October 2028, undrawn at March 2026 with average utilisation of £22.5m; lease liabilities £195.5m carrying £10.2m of lease interest and £67.5m of capital repayments in FY26. Company interest cover was 17.8x in FY26 on its covenant measure; on my simpler measure (EBIT over net finance costs) about 9.5x in FY26 and 9x in FY27, rising to 14x by FY31. Rate sensitivity is £1.2m a year per 50 basis points, under 1% of EPS, because most structural debt is fixed. The invoice discounting facility (historically up to £140m) was closed in FY23, so reported cash is not flattered by factoring, unlike ISS (DKK 1.59bn of factoring at December 2025); this is a point in favour of the quality of Mitie's free cash flow that the first set left open.

Effect on EPS, FY27 to FY31. Three channels.

  1. Interest. Net finance costs of about £33m, £31m, £30m, £29m and £28m take about 11% of EBIT in FY27 falling to 7% in FY31; the fall is worth about 1 percentage point of EPS growth a year from FY28.
  2. Buybacks funded by the balance sheet. I model £60m in FY27 (the tranche executed before suspension on 21-Jul-2026; the circular cites 49m shares for £81m, which appears to include FY26 tranches), nothing further in FY27, £50m in FY28 and £75m a year in FY29 to FY31, at assumed prices of 160p to 190p. That removes about 30m to 45m shares a year, about 2.5% to 3.5% of the count, and adds about 2 to 3 percentage points to EPS growth a year from FY29. Consensus assumes none, so this is the main reason my FY29 EPS (18.6p) sits above the FactSet median (17.9p). Removing it gives about 18.1p.
  3. Dilution from share schemes. The vesting and exercise of nil-cost options (a maximum of 120.45m shares under option; Phil Bentley exercised 8.8m in August 2026) has already lifted the share count from about 1,255m (FactSet, March 2026) to 1,301.7m voting rights in September 2026 despite the buyback. This is a one-off dilution of about 6% to 7% that falls in FY27 on the basic basis; I carry +80m shares in FY27 and +4m a year after. It is the single largest below-the-line item in FY27 and is invisible in the brokers' diluted basis.

Balance sheet path in my base case (period-end net debt including leases, FactSet definition). About £454m at March 2027 (1.1x), £436m (1.0x) at March 2028, £450m (1.0x), £453m (0.9x) and £450m (0.9x) at March 2031, after dividends of about £60m rising to £86m a year, bolt-on acquisitions of £25m rising to £50m a year, and the buybacks above. Return on invested capital, 18.1% in FY26 after Marlowe, recovers above the 20% target by FY28 as synergies arrive and no large acquisition is made. The debt structure has one date inside the horizon: the revolving credit facility matures in October 2028 and would need renewal in FY28 or FY29 under a new chief executive in a break case; I expect that to be routine given covenant leverage below 1x.

What would change the picture. (i) Completion of the offer: irrelevant to listed holders, but the DBRS review with negative implications (6-Aug-2026) confirms that the combined private group will carry much more debt; Mitie's clean balance sheet is part of what the buyer is paying for. (ii) A large acquisition in a break case (the pre-offer plan allowed about £75m a year of infill; a Marlowe-sized deal would take leverage back to 1.5x and suspend buybacks). (iii) A working-capital reversal (P-08) or an Autumn Budget cost shock, each worth perhaps 0.1x of leverage; neither threatens covenants.

Peers. Serco about 0.75x on its own measure; ISS 2.3x to 2.6x; Securitas near 2x after Stanley; Sodexo and Aramark about 2.9x; Green Landscaping 3.4x to 3.9x. Mitie is the least geared of the European facility services peers after Serco, which is why its EPS can be helped, not hurt, by the balance sheet over the horizon.

P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

C54

Judgement. Mitie generates gross cost savings of about £25m to £30m a year (0.5% of revenue) through its permanent programmes, and over FY27 to FY31 the identifiable savings reaching operating profit are about £20m in FY27, £30m in FY28, £25m in FY29 and £20m a year after; but about half of each year's saving is consumed by the net inflation drag (£10m to £12m a year after pass-through) and by clients' savings claims and bid-pricing drag at renewal (about £5m to £10m a year), so the net amount that lifts the margin is about £5m to £15m a year, 10 to 25 basis points. Cumulatively that is about 40 to 50 basis points over four years, half of the margin gain I forecast. The timing is front-loaded: FY27 and FY28 carry the contracted Marlowe synergies and the first retained PRIO savings; FY29 to FY31 revert to the treadmill rate. The cost to achieve is recurring and roughly equal to the annual retained saving (restructuring and transformation cash costs of £20m to £30m a year below the adjusted line), which is why the adjusted margin can rise while statutory profit lags.

The record, programme by programme (promised, delivered, retained).

  • Project Helix (2017 onward): £45m promised, £30m delivered, none retained (reinvested in client rate-card concessions and frontline incentives, per Jefferies). The lesson: savings made while rebuilding client relationships are given away.
  • Interserve integration (November 2020 to December 2021): £30m target, raised to £35m and £42m, about £55m delivered; largely retained because the sources were supplier renegotiation (£11.5m) and IT and property consolidation (£7.6m), i.e. costs the client never saw. Cost to achieve £17.1m.
  • Target Operating Model (to March 2027): cumulative cost £70.7m (£66.9m cash); it modernised back-office processes and partly absorbed central inflation; retention partial. Its completion removes about £15m a year of below-the-line cash cost from FY28, which is the one genuinely non-recurring improvement in the horizon.
  • Margin enhancement programme (FY26): £25m delivered and fully retained, but entirely consumed by the unrecovered employer National Insurance (£11.7m) and wage inflation (£6.9m) and fuel; the margin rose 10 basis points.
  • Marlowe integration (August 2025 to FY28): £7m delivered in FY26 (ahead of about £5m expected); £15m run-rate targeted for FY27 and £30m for FY28; £16.5m of the £30m is support functions, the rest field-force optimisation, IT migration (25 acquisitions already moved onto IBM Maximo) and property (15 properties exited; Salford Quays closes by December 2026). Integration budget £27m (£14.8m spent in FY26, about £10m in FY27). Retention should be high (70% to 80% per the operating-leverage report) because these are overhead and route-density savings. Deutsche Bank calls the £30m "highly conservative" (0.5% of the combined cost base) and the Interserve precedent supports £35m to £45m; I model £30m, with the excess as upside.
  • Project PRIO (agentic artificial intelligence, from April 2026): £20m to £25m of cost in FY27 below the adjusted line; about 70 live agents across eight domains; a schedule optimiser in Q3 FY27 and agentic recruitment in Q2 FY27; no savings target disclosed. Peer evidence sets the plausible size: ISS has saved about 28 million working hours since 2023 (about 9,500 full-time equivalents on the Sector Digest's arithmetic) but its margin rose only from 4.2% to 4.6% in H1 2026; Capita's £250m cost programme was delivered in full and mostly handed back through volume decline and contract pricing deflation of 26% to 32% on business-process renewals; Compass's chatbots save 10 to 20 hours per role per month. For Mitie I assume gross savings of £20m by FY28 and £30m by FY29, of which 60% to 70% is retained in self-delivered engineering and compliance (output-priced) and under half in guarding and cleaning (headcount-priced, shared at re-tender): retained about £10m in FY28, £15m in FY29 and £18m a year after. This is the least certain input in the model.
  • "Mitie First" self-delivery: targets insourcing £20m of third-party spend; Deutsche Bank estimates Marlowe subcontracted about 75% of its compliance projects (about £170m historically) with about £13m of potential saving if internalised. I model £5m in FY27 and a further £5m in FY28, inside the Marlowe synergy figure.

What is cuttable and what is not. Cuttable: central support functions, property, procurement (Coupa; 900 preferred suppliers for 70% of spend; transactions down 15%), subcontractor mark-ups, duplicated technology. Not cuttable: front-line labour (statutory wage floors, TUPE, union resistance: the GMB dispute over 300 Asda security roles in July 2026 shows the friction), contract remediation and penalties (Capita's £25m to £40m pension failure; Mitie's HMP Millsike mobilisation), and the client's share of any productivity gain. The structural point is that the cuttable layer is perhaps 8% to 10% of the cost base (£400m to £500m), so even a 5% efficiency gain on it each year is only £20m to £25m, which is exactly the run-rate Mitie reports. There is no large one-off cut available; the scope is incremental, permanent and roughly offsets the treadmill.

When it reaches the line items (my base case, operating profit effect against the prior year).

  • FY27: Marlowe synergies +£6m (in-year about £13m against £7m), telecoms loss drop-out +£10m, margin-enhancement savings retained +£22m (not consumed by a new statutory shock), self-delivery +£5m; against net inflation -£11m, the Department for Work and Pensions demobilisation -£15m and bid drag -£5m. Net about +£12m of margin, before volume and consolidation effects. PRIO adds cost, not saving.
  • FY28: Marlowe +£12m (in-year about £25m), PRIO retained +£10m, continuing savings +£10m; against inflation -£10m and bid and client sharing -£10m. Net about +£12m.
  • FY29: Marlowe +£5m (to the full £30m), PRIO +£5m, savings +£10m; against inflation -£10m, sharing -£10m and the first Public Interest Test renewals -£5m. Net about -£5m to +£5m: the treadmill year.
  • FY30 to FY31: savings +£10m to £13m a year against drags of the same size: net roughly nil, with the margin held by mix and operating leverage rather than by cost-out.

Below the line. Restructuring and transformation cash costs of about £60m in FY27 (Target Operating Model about £15m, PRIO £22m, Marlowe integration £10m, OCS transaction costs about £13m), about £30m in FY28 (PRIO tail and restructuring) and about £25m a year after. The operating-leverage report's conclusion, which I adopt, is that at least 30% to 50% of reported below-the-line restructuring must be modelled as a recurring cash operating cost in facilities management; Securitas (about 0.5% of sales a year since 2018), Capita (eight years of annual charges) and Carillion (every year from 2009) are the precedents, and only ISS and Babcock have shown such charges fading once a programme genuinely ends. Mitie has run a named programme in every year since 2017 (Helix, Forte, Interserve, Target Operating Model, Marlowe, PRIO).

For the Consumer. The identifiable savings are concentrated in two programmes (Marlowe and PRIO) and one date (the end of the Target Operating Model in March 2027); monitor the H1 FY27 results on 19-Nov-2026 for the in-year Marlowe synergy figure (expect about £6m in the half), the Facilities Compliance margin, and whether management attaches a savings number to PRIO.

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

❌ D44

Judgement. Price will contribute about 2 percentage points of revenue growth in FY27 and about 1.5 to 2 points a year in FY28 to FY31, and it will contribute nothing to margin: the net of what indexation recovers and what it misses is a drag of about £10m to £12m a year (15 to 20 basis points), which the cost programmes in P-03 have to earn back before the margin can rise. Real pricing power, price above cost, exists in a narrow part of the business (facilities compliance, about £500m of revenue; self-delivered projects), is worth at most £5m to £10m a year at group level if achieved, and is not yet evidenced in the numbers, so I give it no weight in the base case and treat it as the main upside to the FY29 to FY31 margin. The direction of travel is slightly worse for public work from FY28 (Public Interest Test, Two-Tier Workforce Code) and slightly better for the pricing environment as a whole (disciplined national peers; private equity ownership of OCS; falling wage inflation reducing the unrecovered residue).

The mechanics, and what the recovery rates really show. Mitie recovered 97% of wage inflation in FY24, 95% in FY25 and 94% in FY26 (£114.6m of £121.5m), but only 76% of the FY26 employer National Insurance rise (£36.8m of £48.5m; £11.7m unrecovered), a total recovery of 89% of a £170m hurdle. The pattern is structural, not a performance failure: about 90% of contracts carry indexation, about 10% do not (Jefferies, November 2023), indexation resets annually so there is a lag of up to a year behind an April wage rise, standard clauses cover wage indices but not statutory employer costs, central government frameworks received no Budget funding for outsourced suppliers' National Insurance (Institute for Fiscal Studies, February 2025), and most contracts contain change-of-law clauses that recover statutory costs only after negotiation. Peers show the same split: Serco absorbed about £5m of National Insurance in H1 2026 and faced a £20m headwind without contractual recovery; Capita, with pass-through on two-thirds of contracts, still took a £9m hit; Securitas and Compass, with price-wage balancing and dynamic repricing, recover more fully. The slow decline in Mitie's wage recovery rate (97%, 95%, 94%) is itself informative: the unindexed and lagging tail grows as the contract book ages and as acquired contracts (Interserve, Marlowe) with weaker clauses are added.

Price as a revenue driver. Price enters organic growth as indexation of about 60% of the cost base (labour) plus materials. With the National Living Wage up 4.1% in April 2026 (against 6.7% in April 2025), public-sector pay settlements of 3% to 5.5% and private wage growth slowing to about 3%, the price element of organic growth is about 2% in FY27 (management cited about 2% in Q1 FY27 against 3% in FY26). For FY28 to FY31 I assume a National Living Wage rise of 3% to 4% a year (the Low Pay Commission's two-thirds-of-median-earnings formula; the November 2026 Autumn Budget sets the April 2027 rate) and a price contribution of 1.5% to 2%. The implication the Consumer must carry: an unchanged 4% to 4.5% organic growth rate now contains more volume than it did in FY26, so the underlying demand picture is slightly stronger than the headline suggests, and if wage inflation falls further the reported organic rate falls with it without any loss of volume or profit.

Price as a margin driver: the net drag. Management guides FY27 net inflation after pass-through at £10m to £12m, including about £2m of fuel; Deutsche Bank models £11m. This is the structural residue: 6% of wage inflation on a £3.3bn wage bill rising 3.5% (about £7m), the lag on the 10% unindexed book (about £2m to £3m), and fuel. It recurs every year at roughly this size unless a new statutory measure arrives, in which case the FY26 precedent says about a quarter of it is unrecovered. The Autumn Budget of November 2026 is the live risk for FY28: a further employer National Insurance or wage-floor measure of the FY26 scale would add a one-year drag of £10m to £15m (about 20 basis points) and would push my FY28 margin from 5.3% toward 5.1%. I do not include it in the base case because there is no evidence of one in the files, but it is the most probable single source of a downgrade to FY28.

Renewal pricing: both directions. At renewal, prices move in both directions and the evidence is mixed. Negative: Defra lost on price and Rolls-Royce lost to a global bid (both FY25), the Department for Work and Pensions facilities contract completed in H1 FY26 and apparently moved to ISS, low bidders on contested public contracts cost about 30 basis points of margin, and the renewal rate fell to 59% in FY25 (two public completions alone cost 29 percentage points of the metric). Positive: renewals in FY26 and Q1 FY27 ran at 84% and 91% with extensions at GSK, the Home Office, Lloyds, Heathrow, Sellafield and Transport for London; a former OCS category manager (15-Oct-2026) says renewals have frequently carried double-digit price resets to recover inflation that mid-term indexation missed; Jefferies notes Mitie bids new work at margins above the portfolio average because technology lets it. My reading is that the renewal point is where Mitie recovers the lag on unindexed contracts, and also where it loses the contracts that were only ever held on price; the net is neutral for margin at group level and about 1 percentage point of revenue a year is lost to non-renewal (some deliberate: £70m of low-margin contracts were not renewed).

Where real pricing power could exist. Facilities compliance: non-discretionary, regulation-driven (fire, water, air, building safety), evergreen small-ticket contracts (Marlowe's average customer spend about £11,000) that clients rarely re-tender; private specialists earn 30% to 45% gross margins and 8% to 10% EBIT margins (PTSG 39.6% and 8.4%; Andwis 30.3% and 9.5%) against Mitie's 10.8% group gross margin. A 1% real price rise on about £500m of compliance revenue is £5m, about 8 basis points of group margin; a 3% rise over three years would be worth about £15m. Against that: competitors withdrew work from Marlowe after the acquisition (Deutsche Bank, June 2026), about 300 staff left in Q1 FY27, and the cross-sell pipeline of about £700m of annual contract value has not yet produced disclosed compliance organic growth. Projects: pricing power is exercised through selectivity (as SPIE does), not through list prices, and shows up as a stable 9%-plus margin rather than as price inflation. I therefore model compliance pricing as an upside of up to 10 basis points a year from FY29, not in the base case.

The Public Interest Test and public pricing from FY28. From April 2027 central government contracts above £1m (cleaning and security named first) must be assessed for insourcing before re-tender, and a central Insourcing Unit was launched on 29-Sep-2026. The pricing effect precedes the volume effect: incumbents bidding against an in-house option will concede price to keep the work, so I assume the bid-pricing drag on public renewals rises from about 30 basis points of the affected contracts' margin to perhaps 50 basis points from FY28, which is inside my £5m to £10m a year "sharing" drag. Defence, justice and secure sites (Landmarc, AWE, immigration) are largely exempt or impractical to insource, which limits the exposure to perhaps £800m to £1bn of revenue.

Automation savings and price. Clients claim automation savings at once on cost-plus contracts and at re-tender on fixed-price ones (Deutsche Bank, January 2026; Sector Digest). Mitie's own deployment (about 40% of service requests handled without human intervention; 43% of engineering requests automated) is therefore partly deflationary for revenue per contract in guarding and helpdesk work, and I carry a slight negative in the volume line (about 0.3 points a year of organic growth) rather than in price.

Private equity ownership and sector pricing. Consolidation under CD&R removes OCS, which a former Sodexo commercial manager (16-Oct-2026) describes as highly price-competitive and willing to accept lower margins, as an independent low bidder; a former Mitie project lead (5-Oct-2026) expects fewer bidders for national integrated contracts and higher prices. This is a mild positive for sector pricing from 2027, with the counter-risk that a highly leveraged owner bids for cash flow. For the standalone Mitie model it matters only in a break case, where OCS would remain an aggressive rival.

For the model. Price contributes about 2.0 points of revenue growth in FY27, 1.8 in FY28, 1.6 in FY29 and 1.5 in FY30 to FY31; the net inflation drag is £11m in FY27 and £10m a year after; no real price in the base case; a Budget shock of £10m to £15m is the bear-case item for FY28.

P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

C50

Judgement. Two reinforcing loops and three balancing loops are operating now, and their relative strength changes across the horizon. In FY27 and FY28 the reinforcing loops of scale, technology and route density are the stronger: they are what lift the margin from 4.7% toward 5.3% and keep renewals above 85%. From FY29 the balancing loops of public-sector insourcing and clients' savings claims become the stronger: they cap revenue growth near 4% and the margin near 5.5%. The decisive judgement for the model is that the reinforcing loops in this business model do not compound into accelerating returns, as they would in a network business, because the contract structure (cost-plus, indexed, re-tendered) converts most of each efficiency gain into client savings or share gain rather than margin. The loops therefore shape the forecasts as a ceiling and a floor: a floor under renewals and share (the scale loop), and a ceiling on margin and public revenue (the balancing loops). Net, they are mildly positive for the line items to FY28 and neutral to mildly negative after.

Reinforcing loop 1: scale funds technology, which wins tenders and adds scale and data. Mechanism: £200m of technology investment since 2017 (Mozaic360 on over 140 strategic clients; IBM Maximo; the Agentic Mesh; over 1,000 cleaning robots) lowers cost to serve and is cited by Jefferies as a differentiator in tenders; wins add volume over which the fixed technology layer is spread. Evidence it operates now: order intake £1.6bn (+33%) and a record pipeline of £32.5bn in Q1 FY27; renewals 91%; customer Net Promoter Score +64 from +39 in FY22; new wins and extensions at Sellafield (600 staff), Transport for London (five years), the Metropolitan Police and HMP Millsike; Technical Services secured 70% of its FY27 sales target by Q1. Precedent: ISS's technology-led integrated contracts (Deutsche Telekom extended to 2035). What would stall it: a client claiming the savings (loop 4), or a cyber or systems failure (ISS's 2020 attack cost about DKK 300m). Effect on the line items: it supports organic volume growth of 2.5% to 3% a year above a market growing 2% to 3% and supports the renewal rate; it adds little to margin directly.

Reinforcing loop 2: route density and self-delivery (Marlowe). Mechanism: compliance visits added to existing engineering routes lower cost per visit; self-delivery captures the subcontractor's margin (Marlowe subcontracted about 75% of compliance projects); captured margin funds more engineers (3,235 mobile engineers), which tightens routes further; the schedule optimiser due in Q3 FY27 accelerates it. Evidence now: £7m of synergies in FY26 ahead of plan; Alarm Receiving Centres consolidated; 15 properties exited; a compliance cross-sell pipeline of about £700m of annual contract value after a launch to 200 clients; over 80% of Mitie's accounts did not buy compliance from it at acquisition and under 20% of its 3,000 clients do. Precedents: Rentokil's branch profitability converging toward 25% with density (Bernstein, June 2026); Coor's 75% self-delivery supporting margins; Interserve's £55m. What would reverse it: churn of Marlowe's small clients (Deutsche Bank: a 10% revenue loss at a 25% drop-through costs £7.5m), which the 300 Q1 FY27 departures and competitors' withdrawal of work make a live risk; the first set's Wisdom Library caution that this is density, not a network effect, and holds only region by region. Effect on the line items: this loop is the engine of the FY27 and FY28 margin step (Marlowe synergies to £30m, about 50 basis points of group margin on FY26 revenue) and of the Business Services margin rising from 6.25% to about 6.9% by FY30 in my model; it is also the source of the upside case (synergies of £40m-plus; compliance organic growth in high single digits).

Reinforcing loop 3 (weaker): integrated contracts deepen data and workflow lock-in. Integrated services reach only about 40% of the largest customers and a £1.5bn share-of-wallet opportunity is identified in the top 50 clients. The lock-in is real but not decisive: the renewal rate fell to 59% in FY25 and recovered to 91%, a 32-point swing that a strong lock-in would not permit. I treat it as supporting the 85% to 90% renewal assumption, not as a growth engine.

Balancing loop 4: clients claim automation savings. Mechanism: on cost-plus contracts the saving passes to the client at once; on fixed-price contracts at the next tender; incumbents cannot "buy" renewals because they know the true cost (former Sodexo manager, 16-Oct-2026). Evidence now: management expects no PRIO savings before FY28 and has no savings target; Capita's business-process pricing deflating 26% to 32% at renewal; Securitas's guarding (64% of sales) billed at cost plus a fee; ISS's margin up only 40 basis points despite 28 million hours saved. Effect: it caps the retained share of PRIO and the margin-enhancement programme at perhaps half, which is why P-03's net retained saving is £5m to £15m a year rather than £25m to £30m, and it deflates revenue per contract in security and helpdesk work by perhaps 0.3 points of growth a year.

Balancing loop 5: statutory labour costs not covered by indexation. Mechanism as in P-04. Evidence now: £11.7m of unrecovered National Insurance and £6.9m of unrecovered wage inflation in FY26; a guided £10m to £12m net drag in FY27; the Two-Tier Workforce Code returning from October 2026 and the Employment Rights Act 2025 raising wage baselines on public contracts; the Autumn Budget pending. Peers: Sodexo's margin fell 130 basis points to 3.4% in FY26 under contract reviews and reinvestment; Coor's margin dipped to 4.4% on the indexation lag and recovered to 5.0% as inflation fell. The loop is weakening because wage inflation is falling and because no new statutory step has been legislated for FY27; it is the loop most likely to re-strengthen abruptly (one Budget). Effect: a recurring -15 to -20 basis points a year on margin, inside my inputs.

Balancing loop 6: delivery failures feed insourcing, which removes public contracts. Mechanism, step by step: an outsourcer fails visibly (Capita's Civil Service Pension Scheme, a £25m to £40m hit and a backlog of 120,000 cases; HMP Millsike found "unready, understaffed and unsafe" by the Independent Monitoring Board on 3-Sep-2026; one in five NHS cleaners' payslips wrong, with strikes); unions campaign (UNISON and the RMT, 1-Sep-2026, calling for cleaners and security staff to be brought in-house); government builds machinery (the Public Interest Test from April 2027; the Insourcing Unit launched 29-Sep-2026; the Department for Work and Pensions insourcing Universal Credit checking from Teleperformance, 2-Oct-2026); re-tenders are lost or priced down; the outsourcer cuts cost to compensate, raising the chance of the next failure. This is the one loop in the model that is genuinely self-reinforcing in the negative direction, and Mitie's own labour loop (payroll errors, redundancy disputes, 63% engagement) feeds it. What limits it: the practical barriers to insourcing (termination costs, redundancy and pension transfer liabilities, lack of in-house capacity: Stephenson Harwood, July 2026; the NHS continuing to lean on contractors), the exemption of defence and the difficulty of insourcing vetted security and custodial work, and 5-to-10-year contract lengths that defer the effect. Effect on the line items: nil in FY27, a small pricing effect in FY28, and from FY29 a revenue haircut that I size at about 0.5 points of growth a year (roughly £30m to £40m of revenue and £3m to £5m of profit a year) in the base case, with a bear case of £150m of revenue a year lost from FY29.

Balancing-to-resolving loop 7: low listed multiples draw private equity bids. This loop has already run to completion for Mitie: listed European business services at about 9.1x EBITDA against 12.0x paid for private ones drew the OCS offer at a 45% to 47% premium, 15x FY27 earnings. Its remaining relevance is to the competitive structure (one fewer independent national bidder; two or three players able to bid for national integrated contracts, per a former Mitie project lead) and to the break case, where the loop would run again from a lower price, with a long Competition and Markets Authority review deterring other bidders for a time.

Which loop is strongest, by period. FY27 to FY28: loop 2 (density and synergies), because its actions are contracted and ahead of plan; it is worth about +50 basis points of margin against about -35 from loops 4 and 5, which is how the margin reaches 5.3%. FY29 to FY31: loops 4, 5 and 6 together, which hold organic growth near 4% and the margin gain to about 10 basis points a year; loop 1 keeps Mitie gaining share within that. The net shape for the forecasts: EBIT growth of about 10% in FY27 and 9% in FY28 slowing to 6% in FY29 and 4% to 6% after, which is a deceleration profile the Consumer should test against consensus (which shows EBIT growth of 14%, 10% and 7% for FY27 to FY29 on the FactSet median).

P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

C62

Judgement. The forecasts for FY27 deserve high weight and a slight upward lean on profit and margin, a downward lean on revenue and free cash flow, and a neutral stance on EPS once the share-count basis is corrected; the forecasts for FY28 deserve moderate weight and a neutral-to-slightly-upward lean on margin; the forecasts for FY29 to FY31 deserve low weight and a downward lean on revenue and a neutral lean on margin, because by then the record belongs to a management team that, in the standalone case, will have left. The track record is excellent but it is Phil Bentley's and Simon Kirkpatrick's, both of whom leave on completion and one of whom (Bentley) retires in spring 2027 in any case; in a break case the record cannot be extrapolated beyond FY27 without a discount for a leadership gap of about 12 months.

The record by line, and the lean it justifies.

  • Operating profit and margin: beat. Seven consecutive consensus upgrades after the October 2023 Capital Markets Event; FY24 EBIT £20m above the £190m guide; FY26 EBIT £264m in line with Jefferies and £2m above Deutsche Bank, margin 4.7% against 4.6% expected; every FY22 to FY24 target met in a high-inflation environment. Lean FY27 EBIT slightly above the "at least 5%" floor: my £304m (5.05%) against guidance of at least £300m and the FactSet median of £301m. The lean is small because the first set's bridge and mine both show the Department for Work and Pensions loss and continuing inflation absorbing most of the synergy and telecoms gains; the floor is attainable, not deeply sandbagged.
  • Free cash flow: beat, but flattered. FY26 £162m against a guide of at least £120m (+35%), after £70m of cash other items and helped by about £20m of working-capital inflow and a cash tax rate held down by Marlowe's tax losses (Deutsche Bank assumes about 15%). The record says management sets cash floors it clears; the composition says FY26 borrowed from FY27 (working capital) and that FY27 carries PRIO and transaction cash costs. Lean FY27 to about £135m on the company definition, £150m excluding transaction costs, i.e. at guidance rather than above it; lean FY28 and after to about 80% conversion of adjusted net income.
  • Revenue: slight miss. FY26 £5,619m against about £5,650m guided and £5,653m consensus; Q1 FY27 organic 4% against Jefferies' 5%; Technical Services -5% in Q1 FY27. Lean revenue slightly below consensus from FY29 (public renewals) and in line for FY27 and FY28 (my £6,000m and £6,288m against £6,000m and £6,288m).
  • Interest: miss. FY26 £27.7m against Jefferies' £20m (a 38% miss); the pattern is that acquisition debt costs more than brokers model. Lean net finance costs to the higher end (£33m in FY27 against the £34m consensus implies; the risk is still upward if the revolving facility is used more heavily for working capital).
  • Guidance behaviour. Floors on margin and cash, reiterated not raised; adjacent ambitions raised instead (projects to £2bn, compliance to £1.5bn, savings target to £25m). Headline margin target never raised in three years. That is the "sandbagging precision" archetype for profit and cash, and the honest-tracker archetype for revenue; it argues for leaning above on margin and cash floors and in line on revenue.

How the record transfers across the horizon.

  • FY27 (high weight). About half of the FY27 EBIT growth is mechanical and already visible (Marlowe consolidation and synergies, telecoms drop-out, the absence of a new statutory shock); the first-half print on 19-Nov-2026 will show most of it; the executives are retained through completion (cash retention awards of 150% of salary); the only unknowns are the Autumn Budget (which affects FY28 more than FY27) and working capital. Cone: EBIT £292m to £312m; margin 4.9% to 5.2%.
  • FY28 (moderate weight). The contracted synergies (£30m) and the end of the Target Operating Model are known; PRIO's retained saving, the Budget and the first Public Interest Test renewals are not; in a break case the new chief executive would be in post for perhaps six to nine months of the year with an incentive to rebase. Cone: EBIT £312m to £350m; margin 5.0% to 5.55%.
  • FY29 to FY31 (low weight). No guidance exists beyond FY27; the public renewal cycle begins under the Public Interest Test; a new plan would be set by new management; the record of beating floors says nothing about floors that have not been set. Cone widens to roughly plus or minus 10% on EBIT.

Where the record argues against the forecasts. Three blemishes matter for Sets 3 and 4. First, the margin stalled at 4.6% to 4.7% for three years while revenue rose 41%: the record of beating guidance coexists with a record of not raising margin, so a forecast of margin expansion is new ground even if guidance is met. Second, execution on public mobilisations (HMP Millsike; NHS payroll) and contract retention in Technical Services (Q1 FY27 -5%) show that the operational record is weaker than the financial record. Third, the "other items" line has risen every year (£73m to £113m), so the record of delivering adjusted profit has been bought with recurring below-the-line cash; a clean-earnings forecast should be leaned below the adjusted one by about 2p to 3p of EPS in FY27 and about 1.5p a year after (P-08).

Direction of lean, summarised for the model. FY27: EBIT +1% against consensus, revenue in line, free cash flow -10% against the £150m guide on the company definition after transaction costs, EPS in line with consensus on the diluted basis (14.6p) and 15.2p on the basic company basis. FY28: EBIT in line to +1%, margin 5.3%. FY29 onward: revenue -0.5% to -1% a year against consensus trend, margin in line, EPS above consensus only through buybacks.

P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

C58

Judgement. Below the operating line, the next four years are kinder to Mitie than the last four to six: interest peaks in FY27 and falls; the tax rate is stable at about 25% with cash tax below it for two years; minorities are a steady £8m to £10m; the share count stops rising (after one last step of dilution in FY27) and starts falling if buybacks resume; and other items, which rose every year from FY22 to FY26, fall by about half from FY28 as the Target Operating Model ends and the Marlowe and PRIO costs roll off. The result is that adjusted EPS grows at about 10% a year (13.6p in FY26 to about 22p in FY31), statutory EPS grows much faster from a depressed base (6.6p to about 18p), and the gap between the two narrows from 106% to about 27% by FY29, at the top of the 20% to 30% peer range. Free cash flow conversion dips to about 70% of adjusted net income in FY27 and recovers to about 80% from FY28. The single largest below-the-line change against FY22 to FY26 is the end of acquisition-driven growth in other items and share count; the single largest risk is that a new programme replaces the old ones, as has happened every year since 2017.

Interest. FY22 to FY26: £19.1m, £13.4m, £13.6m, £19.5m, £31.0m on FactSet's basis (£16.2m and £27.7m in FY25 and FY26 on the company's), rising with Marlowe and buybacks. FY27 to FY31 in my model: £33m, £31m, £30m, £29m, £28m, including about £10m to £11m a year of lease interest. The fall is slow because I spend most of the free cash flow on dividends, bolt-ons and buybacks; without buybacks it would fall to about £22m by FY31.

Tax. The effective rate on profit before other items was 24.5% in FY26 (statutory 27.0% because acquisition costs are not deductible); UK corporation tax is 25%. I use 25% throughout. Cash tax runs below the charge in FY27 and FY28 because Marlowe brought tax losses (Deutsche Bank assumes a 15% cash rate for the combined group and about £15m of cash saving in FY26); I assume cash tax of about £50m in FY27 against a £68m charge, converging by FY29. Because the Financial Analyst's form has no minorities line, I enter a "tax rate" of 28.0% falling to 27.5% that includes the minority interest; the true rate is 25%.

Minorities. Landmarc's 49% partner: £7.0m in FY25, £7.7m in FY26; I assume £8m in FY27 rising to £9.5m in FY31 with Landmarc's defence work. Not a lever; a steady deduction.

Other items: composition and path. FY25 £72.5m; FY26 £112.7m (amortisation of acquired intangibles £41.5m; restructuring £27.2m; acquisition and disposal costs £74.9m including £22m of Marlowe transaction costs and £14.8m of integration; other £10.6m). My path: FY27 about £120m (amortisation £42m; Target Operating Model about £15m; PRIO £22m; Marlowe integration £10m; OCS transaction costs about £13m; other restructuring about £15m; plus, only if the deal completes, retention awards); FY28 about £75m (amortisation £42m; PRIO tail £10m; restructuring £20m); FY29 to FY31 about £65m a year (amortisation about £40m; restructuring £25m). Cash component: about £60m in FY27, £30m in FY28, £25m a year after. The recurring restructuring of about £20m to £25m a year is, on the operating-leverage report's test and the Industry Digest's rule, an operating cost; my "clean" EPS below deducts it.

Share count. FactSet 1,255m at March 2026; voting rights 1,301.7m in September 2026 after the FY27 buyback tranche (about £60m, my estimate of the FY27 portion of the £81m cited) and the exercise of roughly 80m nil-cost options under the Long-Term Incentive Plan and Enhanced Delivery Plan (the offer document caps options at 120.45m shares). Weighted average about 1,285m in FY27 (a 2.3% rise on FY26 despite the buyback), about 1,286m in FY28, then falling to about 1,252m, 1,213m and 1,177m with £50m to £75m a year of buybacks at 160p to 190p. Against FY20 to FY26, when the count went from 686m to 1,408m (the 2020 rights issue and Interserve shares) and back to 1,255m through £303m of buybacks and then up again with Marlowe's 86.6m shares, the next four years are the first in which the count falls steadily without a corresponding acquisition.

Clean EPS deducts the restructuring, transformation and PRIO cash costs that recur in substance (about £52m in FY27 excluding the OCS transaction costs and the Marlowe integration budget, £30m in FY28 and £25m a year after), taxed at 25%; statutory EPS deducts all other items including amortisation of acquired intangibles.

FactSet consensus (median, three brokers) adjusted EPS: 14.5p, 16.6p, 17.9p for FY27 to FY29, on a diluted share basis of about 1,310m to 1,340m. My FY27 figure on that basis is about 14.6p (in line); my FY28 and FY29 figures without buybacks are 16.8p and 17.9p (in line); with buybacks 17.0p and 18.6p (+2% and +4%). The Consumer should read my base case as agreeing with consensus on the operating path to FY29 and differing on capital allocation and on the share-count basis.

Net income. Adjusted attributable profit grows from £171m to £195m (+14%), £218m (+12%), £232m, £250m and £262m; statutory net income from £83m to about £105m, £162m, £184m, £203m and £217m. The acceleration in statutory profit from FY28 is the most visible change against FY22 to FY26 (statutory net income £31m, £91m, £126m, £101m, £83m: falling for two years while adjusted profit rose) and is the point at which the Industry Digest's "106% differential" criticism stops applying.

Free cash flow (company definition). My path: £135m in FY27, £178m in FY28, £184m in FY29, £204m in FY30 and £215m in FY31 (FY26 £162m), after cash other items of £60m, £30m, £25m, £25m, £25m, working-capital outflows of £20m to £27m a year (projects about £25m; no repeat of the FY26 inflow), capital spending of £40m rising to £48m, lease capital repayments of £68m rising to £76m, interest paid of £31m falling to £27m and cash tax of £50m rising to £88m. Conversion of adjusted attributable profit: 69%, 82%, 79%, 81%, 82%. Against FY22 to FY26 (FactSet free cash flow £213m, £54m, £157m, £144m, £161m: volatile with working capital and acquisitions), the next four years are steadier and rising, with FY27 the trough. The FactSet consensus free cash flow of £150m, £178m and £198m for FY27 to FY29 is on an unstated definition; mine is below it in FY27 (transaction and PRIO cash) and in line in FY28.

What would move these materially. Upward: faster use of Marlowe's tax losses; completion of the £100m buyback pace (+1p of EPS by FY29); a one-off working-capital release if debtor days return from 31 to 29 (about £30m). Downward: a new restructuring programme under new management in a break case (each £10m of recurring cash cost is 0.6p of clean EPS); the revolving credit facility drawn for working capital at a floating rate; a contract dispute or remediation charge (provisions of £10.8m exist); retention and transaction costs if the deal completes late.

P-09

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

C57

Judgement. The next four years invert the shape of the last six. FY21 to FY26 was revenue-rich and earnings-poor: revenue grew from £2.5bn to £5.6bn (a 17.6% compound rate, most of it Interserve, Marlowe, bolt-ons and surge work), the operating margin was rebuilt from 2.3% to 4.7% by FY24 and then held flat for three years by cost shocks, adjusted EPS on the brokers' basis grew only 4% and 7% in FY25 and FY26, statutory EPS fell for two years, other items doubled, the share count rose with every acquisition and net debt went from a small net cash position (FY22) to £444m. FY27 to FY31 will be revenue-modest and earnings-rich: revenue grows at 4% to 5% a year (6.8% in FY27 with the Marlowe carry-over), the margin rises about 80 basis points to 5.5%, EBIT compounds at about 8%, adjusted EPS at about 10% (with buybacks; about 8.5% without), statutory EPS at about 22%, free cash flow rises from £135m to about £215m, leverage falls below 1x and return on invested capital returns above 20%. The net impact is positive on every profit and cash line and negative on the growth line; the quality of earnings improves markedly from FY28; and the dispersion of outcomes narrows for FY27 and widens again from FY29. The two caveats that temper the positive reading are that the margin gain is modest in absolute terms (80 basis points over four years, half of it mechanical and already in consensus) and that the standalone forecasts beyond FY28 depend on a management team that does not yet exist.
