The connections that matter most.

  1. The margin step and the statutory-cost calendar. The margin was flat for three years because each year's £20m to £25m of self-help was consumed by a statutory shock (National Living Wage +9.8% in April 2024, +6.7% in April 2025 plus employer National Insurance). FY27 has no such shock, so the same self-help drops through. Every forecast of the FY28 margin is therefore a forecast about the Autumn Budget of November 2026 and the April 2027 National Living Wage; the Consumer should treat that date as the single most important external event for the 18-month forecasts.
  2. Dilution and the EPS narrative. The exercise of about 80m options lifts the FY27 share count by about 6% to 7% on the basic basis. It explains why Jefferies, on the company's basis, forecasts only +7% EPS growth in FY27 (13.6p to 14.6p) while the FactSet median shows +15% from a 12.6p diluted base. Both are internally consistent; the Consumer should quote the company-basis growth (about +7% to +12%) when describing the FY27 earnings story and the diluted-basis figures when comparing with consensus.
  3. Cash other items and free cash flow. The adjusted figures and the free cash flow move in opposite directions in FY27: adjusted EBIT rises 15% while free cash flow falls about 17% (£162m to about £135m), because PRIO, the Marlowe integration and the transaction costs are cash and below the line, and FY26 had a working-capital inflow. A free cash flow print below £150m at the FY27 results would not contradict the operating story but would contradict the guidance, and would be the first visible evidence that the "false friend" concern the first set discounted has some substance. I agree with the first set that the concern is overstated (leverage 1.1x; conversion recovering to 80%), but FY27 is the year in which it will be tested.
  4. Growth deceleration and the mechanical slowdown. Reported growth falls from 10% in Q1 FY27 to about 7.5% in Q2, about 5% in H2 FY27 and under 5% from FY28 as Marlowe annualises in August 2026. This is arithmetic, not deterioration; but it coincides with the Technical Services decline (-5% in Q1 FY27) and with the Public Interest Test, so the market, in a break case, would see a company whose reported growth has halved, which matters for the multiple.

Magnitude in pounds over the four years to FY30. Revenue up about £1.2bn (£5.6bn to £6.8bn); EBIT up about £110m (£264m to £374m), of which about £55m is holding the 4.7% margin on the extra revenue and about £55m is margin improvement; adjusted attributable profit up about £80m; free cash flow up about £40m against FY26 and about £70m against the FY27 trough; cumulative free cash flow FY27 to FY30 about £700m, of which dividends take about £280m, bolt-ons about £165m and buybacks about £260m.

Predictability and skew. FY27: narrow, slightly positive skew on profit (guidance is a floor), negative skew on cash. FY28: moderate cone, symmetric, with the Budget the main downside and synergy outperformance (Interserve precedent) the main upside. FY29 to FY31: wide cone, negative skew on revenue (insourcing), positive skew on EPS from capital allocation, and in a break case a leadership discount for at least 12 months.

P-10

Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

C58

Judgement. My base case is a standalone Mitie that grows organically at about 4% to 4.5% a year, falling toward 3.5% by FY31 as public renewals meet the Public Interest Test; raises its operating margin from 4.7% to 5.05% in FY27, 5.3% in FY28, 5.4% in FY29 and 5.5% in FY30 and FY31, with about half of the gain mechanical (Marlowe synergies to £30m, the telecoms drop-out) and half earned (operating leverage on the semi-fixed layer, mix toward compliance and projects, net of a recurring £10m to £20m a year of inflation and bid-pricing drag); converts about 80% of adjusted profit to free cash flow after a 69% trough in FY27; and uses a balance sheet that falls below 1x leverage to pay a dividend at a third of earnings, buy bolt-ons at £25m to £50m a year and, from FY28, resume buybacks at half to three-quarters of the pre-offer £100m pace. Adjusted EPS on the company's basic basis is 15.2p, 17.0p, 18.6p, 20.7p and 22.3p for FY27 to FY31 (14.6p, 16.8p, 17.9p, 19.4p and 20.4p on the brokers' diluted basis without buybacks, which is in line with the FactSet median of 14.5p, 16.6p and 17.9p). This base case is close to consensus on the operating path to FY29, which I regard as a fair reading of the foundations rather than as a weakness in the analysis: the identifiable drivers are few and visible, the management record argues for meeting the FY27 floor, and the balancing loops argue against anything faster. Where I differ from consensus is in the composition (clean earnings, cash, share count, buybacks) rather than the headline. For the deal, the base case implies a standalone fair value of about 160p to 165p (9.1x my 24-month-forward EPS of about 17.8p) and a trough of about 130p; the 218.5p offer is about 14.4x my FY27 EPS and 12.3x my FY28 to FY29 average, about 35% above standalone fair value: full for Mitie's own history, fair against private-market precedent, and a price a holder should be glad to receive.

Phasing over the next 18 months. H1 FY27 (to 30-Sep-2026, reported 19-Nov-2026): revenue about £2,905m (+8.5%: Q1 +10%, Q2 about +7.5% as Marlowe annualises from August), EBIT about £132m (margin about 4.55% against about 4.3% in H1 FY26, which I estimate from FactSet's half-year split), EBITDA margin about 6.3%; organic growth about 4%; Technical Services still slightly down; Marlowe synergies about £6m in the half; guidance of at least 5% and £150m of free cash flow reiterated; net debt seasonally higher (about £480m to £500m at September after the buyback). H2 FY27 (to 31-Mar-2027): revenue about £3,095m (+5.2%), EBIT about £172m (margin about 5.55% against about 5.0%), EBITDA margin about 7.2%; the full-year margin lands at about 5.05%. H1 FY28 (to 30-Sep-2027): revenue growth about 5.0%, EBITDA margin about 6.5% (+20 basis points), the first half under a new chief executive in a break case and the first half after the Public Interest Test takes effect.

Why this base case and not a higher or lower one. Higher is constrained by the pricing structure (P-04: no real price in the core), the balancing loops (P-05: clients and the state claim most efficiency gains) and the thin cuttable layer (P-03: about £25m a year gross). Lower is constrained by the record (P-06: floors are met), by the mechanical content of FY27 and FY28 (P-03: Marlowe synergies are contracted actions), by the balance sheet (P-02: no financial stress) and by the demand indicators (order intake and pipeline at records). The base case therefore sits near the centre of a cone that is narrow for 18 months and widens after.

Scenarios around the base case (standalone; FY29 adjusted EPS, company basis).

  • Bull (probability about 25%): organic growth 5.5% to 6% as the £700m compliance pipeline converts and Technical Services recovers; Marlowe synergies £40m; PRIO retained £25m; margin 5.3% in FY27 and 5.9% in FY29; buyback at £100m a year; no Budget shock. FY29 EPS about 21.5p; standalone value at 10.2x about 220p, i.e. the offer price. This is the case in which a holder would regret the sale.
  • Base (about 50%): as tabled. FY29 EPS 18.6p; value about 160p to 170p at 9.1x to 9.5x.
  • Bear (about 25%): the deal breaks and a 12-month leadership gap follows; the Autumn Budget adds £12m of unrecovered cost in FY28; organic growth 2.5% as Technical Services keeps leaking and public insourcing takes £150m of revenue a year from FY29; margin 4.85% in FY27 and 5.0% flat; no buyback; a new restructuring programme of £30m a year. FY29 EPS about 15p; value about 115p to 130p at 7.3x to 8.5x, matching Jefferies' stressed 115p and the first set's trough.

The deal, read against this base case. Completion remains the central case (the market implies about 88% to 94%); the standalone model is the downside. At 218.5p the buyer pays about 14.4x my FY27 EPS, 12.9x FY28 and about 8.5x FY27 EBITDA including leases on an enterprise value of about £3.45bn; against private-market precedent (12.0x EBITDA for unlisted business services since 2020; 13.5x in deals since 2021) the price is not generous, which is why CD&R can pay it and still earn a return with leverage and OCS synergies; against Mitie's own ten-year median multiple of 9.1x earnings it is about 35% above fair standalone value. The arithmetic the Consumer needs for Set 4 is therefore: expected value of holding at 210.8p is about 218.5p times 0.9 plus about 155p times 0.1, roughly 212p, so the spread is fairly priced; the asymmetry is 3.7% up against 25% to 30% down.

What would make me change the base case within 18 months, in order of importance. (1) The Competition and Markets Authority's Phase 1 decision (a Phase 2 reference would both widen the spread and delay the standalone restart of buybacks); (2) the Autumn Budget of November 2026 (the files do not give the day) and the April 2027 National Living Wage (a statutory step of the FY26 scale takes 20 basis points off FY28); (3) the H1 FY27 print on 19-Nov-2026: organic growth below 3.5% or an H1 margin below 4.4% would cut my FY27 EBIT toward £295m; free cash flow guidance withdrawn or restated would cut my FY27 free cash flow toward £120m; (4) any disclosure of Marlowe revenue attrition beyond the competitor withdrawals already noted (each £35m of lost Marlowe revenue is about £9m of profit at a 25% drop-through); (5) in a break case, the identity and first plan of the new chief executive, which would set FY28 onward.

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

Mitie reports two divisions (Business Services; Technical Services) with central costs; FY26 company figures: Business Services £2,985m (+17.6%) at a 6.2% to 6.3% margin before other items (about £186m); Technical Services £2,634m (+3.5%) at 5.1% to 5.2% (about £136m); central costs about £58m; group £264.1m, 4.7%.

Business Services carries Marlowe (Facilities Compliance £127m in Q1 FY27, +144%), security, hygiene and landscapes, central government, immigration and justice and Spain (+30% in Q1 FY27); its FY27 growth is about 7% organic plus about five points of acquisition carry-over; its margin rises with the Marlowe synergies (£7m in FY26, about £13m in FY27, about £25m in FY28, £30m from FY29), route density and self-delivery, net of the Department for Work and Pensions demobilisation (-£15m in FY27) and the Public Interest Test from FY29. Technical Services fell 5% in Q1 FY27 (engineering maintenance -7% on prior-year contract losses); it has secured 70% of its FY27 sales target, so I model a flat FY27 (H1 down about 3%, H2 up about 4%) and 3.5% to 4% growth after, led by Facilities Transformation projects (decarbonisation, data centres, water) at margins above 9%; the telecoms business, a £10.7m loss in FY25, is at break-even. FactSet's segment consensus (Business Services £3,220m, Technical Services £2,780m for FY27) looks stale on Technical Services given the Q1 decline; my split reaches the same group total by a different route.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Organic revenue growth of 4.2% in FY27 (price about 2.0 points on a 4.1% National Living Wage rise and about 2.2 points of volume), 4.3% in FY28, 3.8% in FY29, 3.5% in FY30 and 3.3% in FY31; acquisitions add 2.6 points in FY27 (Marlowe to August 2026; bolt-ons) and about 0.5 points a year after. Volume rests on order intake of £1.6bn (+33%) and a record £32.5bn pipeline (over 70% to be awarded within 18 months) in Q1 FY27, renewals at 91% (84% in FY26; 59% in FY25), and a market growing 2% to 3%; from FY29 I take about 0.5 points a year off for public renewals under the Public Interest Test (central government contracts above £1m assessed for insourcing from April 2027; Insourcing Unit launched September 2026) and about 0.3 points a year for automation deflating revenue per contract in security and helpdesk work. The price element falls with wage inflation, so a steady organic rate contains more volume than in FY26.
  • Margin: FY27 EBIT bridge from £264m: Marlowe full-year consolidation +£9m; incremental Marlowe synergies +£6m; telecoms loss not recurring +£10m; Department for Work and Pensions demobilisation -£15m; net contract wins and volume at an incremental margin of about 8% +£17m; net inflation after pass-through -£11m (guided £10m to £12m); margin-enhancement savings retained +£22m (the FY26 programme's £25m run-rate falling through because no new statutory shock arrives in FY27); self-delivery of Marlowe subcontract +£5m; bolt-ons +£2m; bid-pricing drag and Marlowe revenue dis-synergies -£5m: total about £304m (5.05%). Project PRIO's £20m to £25m sits below the adjusted line in FY27. FY28: Marlowe +£12m, PRIO savings retained +£10m, continuing savings +£10m, operating leverage on the semi-fixed layer (central costs £55m to £60m, divisional overheads, technology, 3,235 mobile engineers) +£8m, against inflation -£10m and client sharing and bid drag -£10m: margin 5.3%. FY29 to FY31: the treadmill, with mix (compliance and projects) and operating leverage adding about 10 basis points a year net of the drags; margin 5.4%, 5.5%, 5.5%. The incremental margin on volume is about 7% to 10% (a 1% revenue change moves EBIT by about £5m), and the leverage to margin assumptions is high (50 basis points is £30m of EBIT and about 1.7p of EPS).
  • Pricing: cost pass-through only (94% of wage inflation and 76% of statutory costs recovered in FY26; about 10% of contracts unindexed; annual reset lag); no real price in the base case; compliance pricing power is an upside of up to 10 basis points a year from FY29.
  • Below the line: net finance costs peak at £33m in FY27 (£360m of US private placement notes at a 4.65% weighted average to 2034; £250m revolving facility to October 2028; lease interest about £10m) and fall as net debt drifts down; tax 25%; minorities £8m to £9.5m; the share count rises by about 80m in FY27 from option exercises and falls from FY28 with buybacks of £50m to £75m a year at assumed prices of 160p to 190p (30m to 45m shares a year).
  • Cash: working-capital outflows of £20m to £27m a year (projects); capital spending about 0.65% of revenue; lease capital repayments £68m rising to £76m; cash tax below the charge in FY27 and FY28 (Marlowe's tax losses; Deutsche Bank assumes a 15% cash rate) converging by FY29; cash cost of other items £60m in FY27 (Target Operating Model about £15m to its completion in March 2027, PRIO about £22m, Marlowe integration about £10m, OCS transaction costs about £13m), £30m in FY28 and £25m a year after.
  • Leverage: net debt including leases (FactSet definition) about £450m throughout, 1.12x EBITDA at March 2027 falling to 0.89x at March 2031, inside the 0.75x to 1.5x target range; return on invested capital back above 20% by FY28.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

Guidance (FY25 to FY27 plan and FY26 results of 4-Jun-2026, reiterated at the Q1 FY27 update of 21-Jul-2026): FY27 operating margin of at least 5%; free cash flow of £150m a year; FY27 net inflation headwind £10m to £12m including about £2m of fuel; Project PRIO upfront cost £20m to £25m in FY27 (other items); Department for Work and Pensions demobilisation a £15m profit headwind in FY27; Marlowe cost synergies £15m run-rate by FY27 and £30m by FY28; leverage range 0.75x to 1.5x; return on invested capital above 20%; high single-digit revenue CAGR over the plan (delivered a year early). No guidance exists beyond FY27, and formal guidance is constrained during the offer period.

My inputs against it: FY27 margin 5.05% (EBIT about £304m) against the floor of at least 5% (about £300m), a small lean above, because management's record is to set floors and beat them (seven consecutive consensus upgrades after October 2023; FY24 EBIT £20m above guidance; FY26 free cash flow 35% above guidance) but the Department for Work and Pensions loss and continuing inflation absorb most of the synergy and telecoms gains. Free cash flow about £135m in FY27 on the company's definition (after the OCS transaction costs and PRIO cash) and about £150m excluding the transaction costs: at guidance, not above it, because FY26's £162m carried a working-capital inflow of about £20m and a low cash tax charge that do not repeat. Net inflation £11m, PRIO £22m, Department for Work and Pensions -£15m and Marlowe synergies at the guided run-rates: as guided. FY28 to FY31 have no guidance; my margin of 5.3% in FY28 matches the FactSet consensus and my 5.4% to 5.5% thereafter is in line with ISS's 5.5% to 6.0% target for 2028 and below Serco's about 6%.

The FactSet consensus (median of three brokers, 22-Oct-2026): revenue £6,000m, £6,288m, £6,586m; EBITDA £402m, £436m, £455m; EBIT £301m, £331m, £353m; EPS 14.5p, 16.6p, 17.9p; free cash flow £150m, £178m, £198m; dividend 4.8p, 5.4p, 5.7p for FY27 to FY29. My revenue is in line to FY28 and about 0.4% below in FY29; EBIT about 1% above in FY27 and in line after; EPS in line on the brokers' diluted basis without buybacks (14.6p, 16.8p, 17.9p) and above with buybacks and on the company's basic basis (15.2p, 17.0p, 18.6p); free cash flow about 10% below in FY27 and in line in FY28.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Operating profit: the company's "operating profit before other items" (£264.1m, FY26) is the brokers' EBIT; FactSet's history line "EBIT (adjusted)" (£200m) deducts items the company excludes and is not comparable. Other items in FY26 were £112.7m: amortisation of acquired intangibles £41.5m, restructuring £27.2m, acquisition and disposal costs £74.9m (including £22m of Marlowe transaction costs and £14.8m of integration), other £10.6m, less items netted within those lines; statutory operating profit £151m.
  • EBITDA: brokers' basis £360m in FY26 (EBIT £264m plus £96m of depreciation and amortisation excluding acquired intangibles); FactSet's operating EBITDA £337m is lower and not the base. The model's EBITDA margin inputs are on the brokers' basis.
  • EPS: the company's adjusted basic EPS of 13.6p (FY26) is adjusted attributable profit of about £171m (EBIT £264.1m, net finance costs £27.7m, tax at 24.5%, minorities £7.7m) over about 1,256m weighted basic shares. FactSet's and Deutsche Bank's FY26 "actual" of 12.6p is, on my reconstruction, the same profit over a fully diluted count of about 1,350m to 1,370m shares (FactSet shows 1,372m diluted). The FY27 consensus of 14.5p rests on about 1,310m to 1,340m shares because the options have since been exercised (voting rights 1,301.7m, September 2026), so the consensus "+15%" growth compares a diluted base with a post-dilution count; on the company's basic basis the like-for-like growth to consensus is about +7% (Jefferies, 14.6p) to +12% (my 15.2p). The model's EPS, built from FactSet's 1,255m shares plus my +80m change and the buybacks, will be on the company's basic basis.
  • Free cash flow: the company's definition (£162m in FY26) is after interest, cash tax, capital spending including intangibles, lease capital repayments (£67.5m) and the cash cost of other items, before dividends, acquisitions and buybacks. FactSet's history line (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capital spending, £161m) is a different definition that happens to be close in FY26; the consensus free cash flow line's definition is not stated. The Financial Analyst's model will compute free cash flow from adjusted EBITDA without the cash cost of other items, so its figure will exceed my cross-check by about £60m in FY27, £30m in FY28 and £25m a year after; that difference is the reconciling item, not a disagreement on operations.
  • Net debt: FactSet's £444m at March 2026 includes lease liabilities of £195.5m; the company's average daily net debt of £440m (1.2x) and covenant leverage of 0.82x are on different bases. I use the FactSet definition in Part 4.
  • Tax: the input of 28.0% falling to 27.5% is 25% tax plus minorities expressed as a rate on adjusted profit before tax.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: MITIE Group PLC (MTO-GB), the UK's largest facilities services company (FY26 revenue £5,619m, year to 31 March 2026), reporting two divisions: Business Services (security, hygiene and landscapes, facilities compliance including the Marlowe business acquired in August 2025, central government, immigration and justice, Spain) and Technical Services (engineering maintenance, Facilities Transformation projects, defence, healthcare, local government and education); subject to a recommended cash offer from CD&R-backed OCS Group of 218.5p per share plus the 3.1p FY26 final dividend, announced 21 July 2026, approved by shareholders on 16 September 2026, with completion expected in calendar Q1 2027 subject to Competition and Markets Authority clearance.

Request: T-D1

Peers: ISS A/S (largest listed integrated facility services peer; margin rebuilt from about 0% in 2020 to about 5% under the OneISS programme; targets 5.5% to 6.0% by 2028); Serco Group (UK public services outsourcer; about 6% margin on indexed contracts; strong cash conversion); Sodexo (facilities and catering; FY26 margin fell to 3.4%; targets 5% by FY2030); Compass Group (margin-first caterer; disclosed volume-to-profit sensitivity); Securitas (margin raised to 8.0% by 2025 through technology mix and portfolio pruning); Capita (UK public outsourcer; £250m cost programme delivered; Civil Service Pension Scheme failure); Coor Service Management (Nordic integrated facility services; 75% self-delivery; margin recovered from 4.4% to 5.0%); Babcock International (UK support services turnaround; margin from 7.0% to 7.4%); OCS Group (private UK facilities competitor and the acquirer); PTSG, Churches Fire and Andwis (private-equity-backed UK compliance consolidators competing with Marlowe); Interserve and Carillion (historical UK precedents of margin promises that failed). Operating conditions: a disinflating UK labour market after statutory cost shocks (employer National Insurance rise April 2025; National Living Wage +6.7% April 2025 and +4.1% April 2026; Employment Rights Act 2025; Two-Tier Workforce Code from October 2026; the Autumn Budget of November 2026 and the April 2027 National Living Wage); the integration of a large acquisition into a labour-intensive services group (Marlowe, £350m, August 2025); a UK public-sector turn toward insourcing (Public Interest Test for central government contracts above £1m from April 2027; the Insourcing Unit launched September 2026); an offer period and change of control under private equity ownership; a chief executive retirement (Phil Bentley, announced for spring 2027) with no named successor; decarbonisation and data-centre capital programmes driving project demand. Inputs: The expected change to test is the step in Mitie's operating margin before other items from 4.7% in FY26 (flat at 4.6% to 4.7% in FY24 to FY26) to management's guided "at least 5%" in FY27 and to the FactSet consensus of 5.3% in FY28 and 5.4% in FY29 (median of Jefferies, Deutsche Bank and Investec, 22 October 2026), alongside the consensus of revenue growth slowing to 4.8% and 4.7% in FY28 and FY29 as acquisitions stop. Test the record: seven consecutive consensus profit upgrades after the October 2023 Capital Markets Event; FY24 EBIT £20m above guidance; FY26 free cash flow £162m against guidance of at least £120m; FY26 revenue £5,619m slightly below guidance; FY26 net interest £27.7m against Jefferies' £20m; a renewal rate of 59% in FY25, 84% in FY26 and 91% in Q1 FY27; Technical Services revenue down 5% in Q1 FY27. Test the strengths the business demonstrably has: cost pass-through (94% of wage inflation and 76% of employer National Insurance recovered in FY26); integration (Interserve synergies of about £55m against a £30m target; Marlowe synergies of £7m in FY26 against a £15m FY27 and £30m FY28 target); cost programmes (£25m of margin enhancement savings in FY26; Project Helix; the Target Operating Model, £70.7m cumulative cost to March 2026, due to complete March 2027); technology (£200m invested since 2017; Project PRIO costing £20m to £25m in FY27 with no disclosed savings target); scale (about twice the next UK competitor). Test the strengths it would need to acquire: margin expansion while growing organically (the margin has not risen in three years while revenue rose 41%); retention of automation savings against clients who claim them at renewal; real pricing in facilities compliance (private specialists earn 30% to 45% gross margins against Mitie's 10.8%); retention of Marlowe's small clients (about 300 Marlowe staff left in Q1 FY27; competitors withdrew work after the acquisition); public contract execution (HMP Millsike found "unready, understaffed and unsafe" by the Independent Monitoring Board, September 2026; NHS cleaning payroll disputes); and delivery under a new chief executive and finance director if the offer lapses. For each peer: how many facility services companies that announced a margin step of 50 to 100 basis points from a flat base achieved it within two years, how long it took, and whether acquisitions, pruning or organic efficiency delivered it. Focus: Whether the FY27 to FY29 margin step rests on actions already contracted and demonstrated (Marlowe synergies, the lapse of statutory cost shocks, the telecoms recovery) or on capabilities Mitie has not yet shown (retaining efficiency gains against clients, real pricing in compliance, margin expansion without acquisitions), and how often integrated facility services peers starting from a flat margin near 5% have reached the 5.5% to 6.0% level that ISS targets for 2028 within three years.

Request: T-D2

Peers: ISS A/S (self-delivery and technology scale; 28 million working hours saved since 2023; margin outcomes versus guidance 2021 to 2026); Serco Group (indexed public contracts; dispersion of outcomes around guidance, including the 2024 Australian immigration contract loss); Sodexo (margin dispersion in a reset); Compass Group (volume sensitivity, 25% drop-through); Securitas (items affecting comparability about 0.5% of sales a year); Capita (volume decline and contract remediation outcomes); Coor Service Management (indexation lag and margin recovery 2023 to 2026); Babcock International (turnaround dispersion and the Type 31 provisions); Green Landscaping (a leveraged roll-up whose outcomes fell far outside forecasts); Rentokil Initial (route-density economics and the dispersion when density falls); OCS Group (private acquirer; outcomes of CD&R's OCS and Atalian combination). Operating conditions: disinflation after statutory wage and employer-tax shocks in the UK; an offer period with Competition and Markets Authority review and the possibility of a lapsed offer (precedents of UK recommended cash offers that lapsed or were blocked, and the operating and share-price outcomes afterward); a chief executive succession during a strategic plan's final year; a UK public-sector insourcing policy (Public Interest Test from April 2027) acting on 5-to-10-year public contracts; a large acquisition integration (Marlowe) with small-client churn risk; growth of a projects business (Facilities Transformation) with percentage-of-completion accounting and working capital needs (Jefferies has cited about £25m a year). Inputs: The differences to test between FY27 to FY30 and FY21 to FY26 are: revenue growth slowing from double digits (acquisition-driven: Interserve 2020, Marlowe 2025) to mid-single digits (organic, with Marlowe annualising in August 2026); an operating margin rising from a three-year plateau of 4.6% to 4.7% to the guided at least 5% in FY27 and the FactSet consensus of 5.3% and 5.4% in FY28 and FY29; consensus adjusted EPS of 14.5p, 16.6p and 17.9p for FY27 to FY29 against a FactSet FY26 actual of 12.6p and a company-reported adjusted basic EPS of 13.6p; consensus free cash flow of £150m, £178m and £198m against £162m in FY26; other items that rose from £73m in FY25 to £113m in FY26; a share count that rose from 1,255m (FactSet, March 2026) to 1,301.7m voting rights (September 2026) through option exercises despite a buyback suspended on 21 July 2026 after £81m of a £100m programme. How concentrated are the drivers: the Marlowe synergy target (£15m FY27, £30m FY28), the absence of a new statutory cost shock in FY27 (net inflation guided at £10m to £12m against £18.6m unrecovered before savings in FY26), the telecoms recovery (about £10m), the Department for Work and Pensions demobilisation (-£15m), Project PRIO savings (undisclosed), Technical Services' recovery (70% of the FY27 sales target secured by Q1), and the public renewal cycle from FY29. For the dispersion: the range of margin outcomes for facility services companies in the two years after a large acquisition; the frequency with which guided margin floors in this sector were met, beaten or missed in disinflationary years; the frequency and size of downgrades when a chief executive left without a successor; the share-price and estimate paths of UK companies whose recommended offers lapsed on competition grounds; and how often forecasts resting on one integration and one cost programme were met. Focus: How much of the forecast change from the last six years rests on two programmes (Marlowe synergies and Project PRIO) and one calendar effect (no statutory cost shock in FY27), what the historical dispersion of outcomes has been for integrated facility services companies in the two years after a large acquisition and in the year a chief executive departs, and how the dispersion would differ between completion of the offer and a lapse.

Request: T-I1

Peers: ISS A/S (raised 2026 guidance; capital markets day 14 September 2026; Q3 2026 results due); Serco Group (H1 2026 results 6 August 2026; guidance record under UK National Insurance absorption); Sodexo (FY26 results 23 October 2026; FY27 outlook confirmed); Compass Group (FY25 results November 2025; H1 FY26 May 2026); Securitas (Q3 2026 results October 2026); Capita (FY26 margin softness; pension contract remediation); Coor Service Management (Q3 2026 results 22 October 2026); Babcock International (FY26 results June 2026); Rentokil Initial (recent downgrades); OCS Group (private acquirer; latest filed accounts). Operating conditions: an offer period in which three brokers (Jefferies, Deutsche Bank, Investec) rate the shares Hold with targets of 218.5p to 221p anchored on the offer and the buy share has fallen from 89% to 0%; a thin consensus (three brokers in FactSet; consensus EPS revisions +1% over one month and +2% over three, six and twelve months to 22 October 2026); the H1 FY27 results due 19 November 2026, probably the last major standalone print before completion; the Autumn Budget of November 2026 and the April 2027 National Living Wage decision; the Competition and Markets Authority's Phase 1 decision (not yet published as of 22 October 2026); a UK public-sector insourcing policy from April 2027; disinflation reducing the price element of organic growth (management cited about 2% price in Q1 FY27 against 3% in FY26). Consensus to test (FactSet median, 22 October 2026): revenue £6,000m, £6,288m, £6,586m; EBIT £301m, £331m, £353m; EPS 14.5p, 16.6p, 17.9p; free cash flow £150m, £178m, £198m for FY27 to FY29; and the individual brokers' FY27 EPS (Jefferies 14.6p, Investec 14.4p, Deutsche Bank 14.2p) against the company's FY26 adjusted basic EPS of 13.6p and FactSet's FY26 actual of 12.6p, including each broker's share-count and dilution basis. The run-rate to test: Q1 FY27 revenue £1,406m (+10%; organic +4%; Business Services +23%; Technical Services -5%; Facilities Compliance £127m, +144%); order intake £1.6bn (+33%); pipeline £32.5bn; renewal rate 91%; FY26 H1 and H2 margins and the seasonal pattern. The record to test: Mitie's habit of setting floors and beating them (FY24 EBIT £190m guided, £210m delivered; FY26 free cash flow at least £120m guided, £162m delivered) against a FY26 revenue shortfall and the FY26 interest miss; whether formal guidance can be changed during an offer period under the Takeover Code; what peers' latest results (ISS raising guidance; Sodexo's margin fall; Serco's National Insurance absorption; Coor's margin recovery) imply for UK facilities services in FY27; and what the Autumn Budget could add to statutory costs. For each outcome at the 19 November 2026 results and at any later standalone events: what a raise, a confirmation and a cut would each require, and how the three brokers have revised after each of the last six Mitie results and trading updates. Focus: Whether the H1 FY27 results on 19 November 2026 are likely to confirm, raise or cut the FY27 margin floor and the £150m free cash flow guidance, how the three covering brokers would revise FY27 and FY28 estimates in each case given that their ratings and targets are anchored to the offer, and what would happen to guidance, estimates and coverage breadth if the offer lapsed.

Request: T-I2

Peers: ISS A/S (the most discussed listed facility services story: buybacks, technology, margin targets); Serco Group (UK public outsourcing and insourcing narrative); Capita (insourcing and delivery-failure narrative); Sodexo and Compass Group (margin and volume narratives in contract services); Securitas (technology substitution for guarding); Coor Service Management (Nordic density and indexation narrative); Rentokil Initial (route density); Teleperformance (artificial intelligence deflation narrative); OCS Group (private acquirer; CD&R); PTSG and Andwis (private compliance consolidators). For each: which analysts (Jefferies, Deutsche Bank, Investec, Peel Hunt, Stifel, Berenberg, Goldman Sachs) and which commentators discuss it, how prominently, since when, with what view; which dynamics are widely discussed, which by one or two, and which by no one. Focus: Whether sell-side and investor commentary on Mitie is now almost entirely about the offer spread and the Competition and Markets Authority timetable, and how much of the standalone operating story (the statutory cost calendar behind the margin step, Marlowe churn, Project PRIO retention, share-option dilution, the recurrence of other items, and the Public Interest Test) is articulated by anyone, so that it is clear what a break would surprise investors with.
