Layer 3 and Layer 4

Layer 3: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months
and Layer 4: Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

10 questions in 4 groups · 15,124 words · written 24 October 2026, 22:51 to 23:12 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
MITIE Group PLC (MTO-GB): Sets 3 and 4 answers, the predictability of the forecasts and what guidance, consensus and the narrative already reflect

Written 24-Oct-2026 by the Investment Analyst for Sets 3 and 4, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo for Richard.

Preliminary: the frame, what is new since the second set, where I differ, and the model's cross-check gaps

The frame. Everything below is written for a listed holder at 210.8p (21-Oct-2026) who will receive 218.5p in cash if the OCS offer completes (expected calendar Q1 2027, subject only to Competition and Markets Authority clearance) and who falls back to a standalone Mitie if it lapses. The standalone forecasts of record (model update 1) are the break case: revenue £6,001m, £6,289m and £6,559m, EBIT £304m, £333m and £352m (margins 5.05%, 5.3%, 5.4%), adjusted EPS 15.2p, 17.0p and 18.6p on the company's basic basis for FY27 to FY29, free cash flow on the model's own basis £169m, £185m and £197m. The predictability questions in Set 3 are therefore about two different things, and I keep them apart throughout: the predictability of the financial forecasts (high for 18 months, as I show) and the predictability of the equity outcome (binary, and almost entirely a regulatory question).

New facts since the second set, from the AlphaSense reports, that change or sharpen the picture.

  1. The deal's mechanics. The European Commission cleared the acquisition unconditionally (19 to 21-Oct-2026) and the UK Investment Security Unit cleared it under the National Security and Investment Act (21-Oct-2026); only the Competition and Markets Authority remains. The Authority's invitation to comment ran from 15 to 29-Sep-2026 and, as of 22-Oct-2026, no formal Phase 1 inquiry had been launched, so the 40-working-day Phase 1 clock had not started; the parties still expect completion in calendar Q1 2027. The long-stop date is 21-Jul-2027, extendable by agreement with Panel consent. There is no break fee or reverse break fee. OCS may seek Panel consent to lapse the offer if the Authority refers it to Phase 2 without mutual consent, or if remedies are "adverse to a material extent" (undisclosed threshold). Jefferies puts the combined UK share at about 15.8% of security and 11.7% of cleaning; Deutsche Bank's combined facilities management share is below 20%. The Interserve precedent (Phase 1 clearance in seven weeks, 29-Sep to 17-Nov-2020, no remedies) is the closest template. If the scheme is not effective by 19-Nov-2026, Mitie may declare an interim dividend of 1.3p, payable in addition to the 218.5p. Since completion is expected after that date, a holder today should expect about 219.8p, not 218.5p; the spread is 4.3%, not 3.7%. (This assumes the 3.1p FY26 final dividend, approved at the AGM of 21-Jul-2026, has already been paid in the normal August window; the AlphaSense coverage report quotes an "11p spread to 221.6p", which double-counts it if so. The memo should verify the payment date.)
  2. The Autumn Budget is on 28-Oct-2026, four days from now. The second set said the files did not give the day. The Low Pay Commission's indicative National Living Wage for April 2027 is £13.18, a 3.7% rise (Morgan Stanley, 6-Oct-2026), against a risk of 5.5% after the ONS hourly-earnings survey showed 5.8% growth (Morgan Stanley, 22-Oct-2026). Deutsche Bank's preview (21 and 22-Oct-2026) is titled "Do no harm" and flags a possible employer National Insurance holiday for workers aged 20 to 24, which would be a tailwind for a labour-intensive employer. The Budget is therefore two-sided and its central case is benign for FY28; a shock of the April 2025 kind is the tail, not the centre.
  3. Guidance is locked by the Takeover Code. The company confirmed on 21-Jul-2026 that its FY27 targets (an operating margin of at least 5%; free cash flow of £150m a year) are profit forecasts under Rule 28.1. Any revision during the offer period needs reports from the reporting accountants and financial advisers. This makes a formal raise or cut at the H1 results on 19-Nov-2026 very unlikely for procedural reasons, independent of trading; it is the single most important fact for I-01.
  4. The working-capital record is the opposite of what the second set assumed. The second set said FY26 free cash flow of £162m was "helped by about £20m of working-capital inflow" that would reverse in FY27. The FY26 annual report (4-Jun-2026) records a working-capital cash outflow of £35.7m (projects and extended retail payment terms), and the Q1 FY27 update records a free cash outflow of £13m against £21m a year earlier. FY26 cash was therefore earned against an adverse working-capital movement, not helped by a favourable one, and the quality of the £162m is better than the second set credited. The second set's FY27 free cash flow of £135m (company definition) is too low on this count; I lean it to about £145m to £155m (about £150m to £165m before the OCS transaction costs). The cash-tax step the second set assumed (£50m in FY27 against a very low FY26 cash tax) is the other reason its FY27 figure is depressed and is itself uncertain.
  5. Net debt is running higher than the second set's path. Average daily net debt was £523m in Q1 FY27 (against £238m a year earlier, before Marlowe), and closing net debt was £477m at June 2026. The second set's FY27 net finance cost of £33m assumed roughly £450m to £470m of average net debt. On the disclosed debt costs (£360m of US private placement notes at a 4.65% weighted average, lease interest of about £10m, the balance on a revolving facility at about 5.5%), a £500m average implies about £36m. I change this input (section 6).
  6. The share count and EPS bases are now confirmed. The company's FY26 diluted adjusted EPS is 12.6p (basic 13.6p) because 102.1m dilutive potential shares sat behind 1,245.6m weighted basic shares; FactSet's and Deutsche Bank's 12.6p "actual" is that diluted figure, as the second set inferred. Options outstanding were capped at 120.45m in the offer document; voting rights reached 1,301.7m on 1-Oct-2026 (issued 1,302.1m less 8.0m in the employee benefit trust and 0.4m in treasury) after executives exercised and partly sold in August and September. Deutsche Bank's standalone model left its share count at 1,255m after the buyback suspension (per the AlphaSense reconciliation), so its standalone EPS is overstated by about 3% to 4% on that count alone.
  7. Facilities Compliance revenue was £421m in FY26 (+115%), Marlowe's legacy FY26 EBITA margin 6.7% (Jefferies), and Marlowe's own integration history shows customer attrition spiking to about 20% after acquisitions before settling below the 10% industry average within two years (former Marlowe integration manager, 27-Nov-2024). Some Marlowe churn is therefore normal, and the second set's base case (dis-synergies inside a £5m bid-drag line) is consistent with the precedent rather than optimistic.
  8. Leadership in a break is less empty than the first set implied. The annual report states that Phil Bentley intends to retire "once a successor is found" and only at the end of the plan; the finance director's departure is tied to completion. In a break, Mitie would have a retiring but present chief executive running a search that Jefferies says includes internal and external candidates, and a finance director with no stated reason to leave. A £12m retention programme for critical staff exists but is deal-conditional. The 12-month leadership discount in the break case stands, but it is a discount for an announced succession, not for a vacancy.

Where I differ from the first and second sets.

  • FY27 free cash flow (company definition): about £145m to £155m, not the second set's £135m, for the working-capital and cash-tax reasons above; the first set's £150m to £165m was closer. The guidance of £150m is more likely to be met than missed.
  • FY27 net finance costs: £36m, not £33m (input changed).
  • The break-case multiple: 9.5x to 10x 24-month-forward EPS rather than the ten-year median of 9.1x, because the listed peers have re-rated during 2026 (ISS at 11.8x and Serco at 11.7x on the dashboard's measures; facility outsourcers up about 22% over twelve months) and the pre-bid co-movement data show Mitie had become more sector-linked in H1 2026 (D-07). Against that stands the leadership discount and arbitrage selling. Net: a break-case fair value of about 160p to 175p (central 168p) and a trough of about 130p, slightly above the second set's 160p to 165p.
  • The break probability: about 9% to 11%, which is also what the price implies once the 1.3p interim dividend is included (section D-04). The first set's 6% to 12% and the second set's "about 10%" stand; the point is that the spread is fairly priced, so there is no arbitrage edge either way.
  • The Budget: the second set treated it as the main downside to FY28; I agree it is the most important dated external event but judge its central case benign (National Living Wage +3.7%, possible National Insurance relief), with a 15% to 20% probability of a shock of the FY26 kind.
  • The second set's statement that no "statutory cost shock" arrives in FY27 is right for employer National Insurance but should be qualified: the Employment Rights Act's day-one sick pay (from 6-Apr-2026) and the Two-Tier Workforce Code (from October 2026) are statutory cost steps inside FY27, small individually and inside the guided £10m to £12m net inflation, but they are why the net drag does not fall further.

The model's cross-check gaps (required explanation). The one-page summary flags EPS in FY29 and FY30 (the analyst's 0.19 and 0.21 against the model's 0.18 and 0.20) and free cash flow in every year (135 against 169; 178 against 185; 184 against 197; 204 against 218; 215 against 227).

  • EPS. The adjusted attributable profits agree to the pound (195, 218, 233, 250, 262). The gap is the share count: the model retires shares at its own price path (about the current 211p, or its fair-value path), while the second set assumed 160p to 190p, the break-case prices at which a standalone Mitie would actually be buying. At 211p, £75m retires about 36m shares; at 170p, about 44m. Over FY28 to FY30 the difference accumulates to roughly 25m to 30m shares, 2% to 2.5% of the count, which is the 0.5p to 1p gap. No input is wrong: the buyback is a cash input and the price the model uses is its own convention. The memo should quote the model's EPS and note that a standalone Mitie buying at break-case prices would do about 2% better by FY30.
  • Free cash flow. The two figures are on different definitions and both are internally consistent. The model's figure is EBITDA less tax at the input rate on pre-tax profit, less net financial items, capital spending, lease payments and the working-capital movement; it has no line for the cash cost of other items (restructuring, Project PRIO, Marlowe integration, OCS transaction costs: about £60m in FY27, £30m in FY28, £25m a year after), which raises it above the company definition. Pulling the other way, the input tax rate of 28% falling to 27.5% deducts the Landmarc minority as if it were cash tax (about £8m to £9.5m a year) and ignores the Marlowe tax losses that hold FY27 and FY28 cash tax below the charge (the second set assumed £50m of cash tax in FY27 against a £68m charge), which lowers it. The net of plus £60m and minus about £26m is the £34m FY27 gap; plus £30m less about £23m is the £7m FY28 gap; plus £25m less about £12m (minority plus small tax timing) is the £12m to £14m gap thereafter. No input is wrong; the form has no line for either item. For the memo: use the model's free cash flow for leverage and the return calculation, and the company-definition figure (my £145m to £155m for FY27, then roughly the model's figure less £25m a year) when comparing with the £150m guidance.

The third set: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Layer 3: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Risks

C607 questions
D-0172D-0264D-0357D-0463D-0554D-0660D-0755

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The financial forecasts for the next 18 months are highly predictable; the equity outcome is a weighted coin; and the two must not be conflated in the memo. On the forecasts: the first and second sets, three brokers and this set all land within £10m on FY27 EBIT (about £300m to £306m) because the year's change is generated by a handful of disclosed, dated, mostly contracted items (Marlowe consolidation and synergies, the telecoms drop-out, the lapse of the FY26 statutory shock, the Department for Work and Pensions demobilisation, a guided net inflation drag) rather than by judgement; the step asks nothing of the business that it has not demonstrated (pass-through at 93% to 95%; integration at 1.8 times target; floors cleared seven times running) and nothing that integrated facility services peers starting from a flat margin near 5% have not done within two years (ISS, Coor, Capita, Babcock, Securitas; Sodexo the exception, for pricing reasons Mitie does not share); the cone around FY27 EBIT is about minus 4% to plus 3% with a slight positive skew, and the H1 print on 19-Nov-2026 will resolve most of it before the window is a quarter old. FY28 is less predictable because two of its three drivers are either undisclosed (Project PRIO's savings) or external (the Budget of 28-Oct-2026), and because in a break case it would be the first year under a successor chief executive; the forecasts of record are rightly cautious about it (5.3%, in line with consensus) and the Budget's central case is benign. FY29 onward depends on strengths not yet shown (organic margin expansion, compliance pricing, Marlowe client retention, delivery under new leadership) and on the Public Interest Test, and deserves the low weight the second set gave it.

Three corrections to the earlier sets improve the picture rather than worsen it: FY26 free cash flow was earned against a £35.7m working-capital outflow, not helped by an inflow, so FY27 cash is more likely to meet the £150m guidance than the second set allowed; the break-case leadership gap is an announced succession with the chief executive staying until a successor is named, not a vacancy; and the sector's 2026 re-rating puts the break-case multiple nearer 9.5x to 10x than 9.1x. One correction worsens it slightly: net debt is running higher than modelled and FY27 net finance costs are nearer £36m than £33m (input changed). On the equity: the price of 210.8p against an expected receipt of about 219.8p (the 1.3p interim dividend becomes payable if the scheme is not effective by 19-Nov-2026) and a break value of 160p to 175p implies a break probability of about 9%, which is also my estimate; the spread is fairly priced and the holder earns about 10% annualised for bearing a roughly one-in-ten chance of a 20% to 30% loss. The multiple is company-driven only because a bidder has fixed it; a break would hand it back to the sector within weeks. For the memo's risk topic: the case is simple (two programmes, one calendar, one policy, one regulator), its fragilities are known and dated, the trackability of its drivers is good for FY27 and poor for the items that decide FY28 and the break case, and the one risk that is sudden, material and outside the forecasts is the Competition and Markets Authority's decision.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

C72

Judgement. The two sets are highly congruent on the architecture of the case and on every forecast that matters for the next 18 months; they jar in four places, three of which are matters of record that I resolve above, and one of which is a difference of lean that the Consumer should carry as a range rather than a disagreement. The architecture both sets share is this: a low-margin, asset-light, cash-generative market leader whose margin has been flat for three years because an exceptional statutory cost shock consumed its self-help; a FY27 margin step that is largely mechanical (Marlowe synergies, telecoms, the lapse of the shock) and therefore attainable at the 5% floor with modest headroom; a growth engine that has slowed to about 4% organic and will show a halving of reported growth as Marlowe annualises; a public book that meets the Public Interest Test from FY29; an unclean earnings base (other items £113m, a 106% gap between adjusted and basic EPS) that both sets treat as partly recurring; strong management delivery that leaves with the deal; and a recommended cash offer at about 35% above standalone fair value that converts all of this into a binary regulatory outcome. The second set's base case (margin 5.05%, 5.3%, 5.4%; EBIT £304m, £333m, £352m) sits inside the first set's steer (5.0% to 5.1%, about 5.3%, 5.4% to 5.5%) and inside consensus (5.0%, 5.3%, 5.4%). On the figures that drive the 18-month forecast there is no contradiction at all.

The four places where the sets jar, and how I resolve them.

  1. FY26 working capital and FY27 free cash flow (a contradiction of record, now resolved). The first set documented an adverse working-capital trend and leaned FY27 free cash flow to £150m to £165m; the second set asserted a FY26 working-capital inflow of about £20m and leaned FY27 to £135m. The annual report's £35.7m FY26 outflow settles it in the first set's favour on the base, though not on the lean: with projects consuming about £25m a year and cash tax rising, £145m to £155m is the right central figure. The memo should not carry the second set's "FY27 is the year the false-friend concern is tested" with its original force; a print of £140m-plus would be consistent with a sound cash business carrying one-off deal and transformation cash costs.
  2. The buyback (a difference of modelling choice, not of judgement). The first set modelled none, to match consensus; the second set included £50m to £75m a year from FY28 because the standalone case only matters in a break, where a 1.1x-levered company with an 11% active shareholder would resume repurchases. Both are right for their purpose. The second set states the EPS effect separately (about 2% to 4% by FY29), so the Consumer can remove it. I keep the second set's choice.
  3. Project Helix (a factual difference). The first set credited £45m of savings; the second set, citing Jefferies (23-Nov-2020), recorded £30m delivered and none retained. The AlphaSense plausibility report quotes both the 2018 annual report's "£45m run-rate savings target met" and Jefferies' warning that the savings were reinvested in customers. Both are therefore literally true: £45m of run-rate savings were generated and handed to clients. The second set's lesson (savings made while rebuilding client relationships are given away) is the one that matters for Project PRIO and I adopt it.
  4. The lean on FY28 to FY31 revenue (a difference of judgement to carry as a range). The first set allowed organic growth to lift to 4.5% to 5% in FY28 "if the Q1 order intake converts"; the second set modelled 4.3% in FY28 and a drift to 3.3% by FY31 with an explicit public renewal haircut from FY29. The second set is the more conservative and I agree with it as the base, because the first set's lift was conditional and because the price element of organic growth is falling (2% in FY27 against 3% in FY26) so that an unchanged volume rate produces a lower headline. But the Consumer should carry the first set's conditional upside: a 1-point lift in FY28 organic growth is worth about £5m of EBIT and 0.3p of EPS, and the Q1 FY27 order intake (+33%, book-to-bill 1.14x, 70% of the Technical Services sales target secured) is the evidence for it.

Smaller inconsistencies the memo should not repeat. The first set's "no statutory cost shock in FY27" should be read as "no employer National Insurance step" (point 5 of the preliminary). The first set's break-case leadership "vacuum" is a succession in progress (point 8). The AlphaSense guidance report misattributes the FY24 beat (£210m against £190m guided) to FY26; the FY26 beats were margin (4.7% against 4.6%), pre-tax profit and free cash flow (£162m against at least £120m), with revenue a slight miss. Several AlphaSense reports quote the pipeline as £31.7bn (Deutsche Bank's rounding) against the company's £32.5bn and treat the 3.1p dividend as unpaid; the memo should use the company's figures.

What the congruence means for predictability. When two analysts working in sequence from the same evidence arrive at the same FY27 EBIT within £10m, the same margin path within 10 basis points and the same conclusion about what is mechanical and what is earned, and when three brokers independently land on £301m, the forecast is being driven by a small number of visible, disclosed items (D-03) rather than by analyst judgement. That is the strongest evidence in the file that the 18-month forecasts are predictable. The incongruities sit in cash and capital allocation, which matter for the break case and the fair multiple, not for the operating forecast.

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

C64

Judgement. The expected change over the next 18 months (margin from 4.7% to about 5.05% in FY27 and toward 5.3% in FY28; EBIT from £264m to about £304m and £333m) is highly plausible on demonstrated strengths alone: the FY27 step requires nothing Mitie has not already shown, and the FY28 step requires one thing it has shown in a weaker form (retaining efficiency gains) and one thing outside its control (no new statutory shock). The change expected beyond 18 months (margin to 5.5% by FY30, organic growth of 4% to 4.5% a year with Facilities Compliance growing faster, real pricing in compliance, margin expansion without acquisitions) relies increasingly on strengths the business has not demonstrated, and in a break case on a management team that has not been named. I therefore weight the FY27 forecast very highly, the FY28 forecast highly, and the FY29 to FY31 forecasts moderately, with the decisive observation being that the forecasts of record do not ask the business to do anything in the next 18 months that peers starting from a flat margin near 5% have not done within two years.

The FY27 step rests on four demonstrated strengths and one calendar fact.

  1. Cost pass-through. Recovery of 97%, 95% and 94% of wage inflation in FY24 to FY26 and 76% of the FY26 employer National Insurance rise (£36.8m of £48.5m), a total of £151.4m of a £170m hurdle. This is the capability that kept the margin at 4.6% to 4.7% through the worst statutory cost shock in the sector's recent history. The FY27 guide of £10m to £12m of net inflation (gross £135m, recovery £125m, a 93% rate) asks for the same rate on a smaller hurdle.
  2. Integration. Interserve delivered about £55m of synergies against a £30m target (raised to £35m and £42m along the way) at a cost to achieve of £17.1m; Marlowe delivered £7m in FY26 against about £5m expected, with Alarm Receiving Centres consolidated into Craigavon, 15 properties exited, 300 roles removed, Salford Quays closing by December 2026, and 25 acquired entities already on IBM Maximo. The FY27 requirement (a £15m run-rate; about £13m in-year) is the easiest part of the Marlowe curve because it is overhead and property.
  3. Cost programmes. Margin enhancement delivered £25.1m in FY26 and fully offset the £18.6m unrecovered shock; the Target Operating Model delivered £20m of overhead savings in FY26 and completes in March 2027; Coupa delivered £5.1m. The FY27 requirement is that the same programmes deliver about £22m and that, for once, nothing consumes it.
  4. The record of meeting floors. Seven consecutive consensus upgrades after the October 2023 Capital Markets Event (consensus operating profit moved from £207m to over £260m, per the finance director in November 2025); FY24 EBIT £210m against a £190m floor; FY26 free cash flow £162m against at least £120m; every target of the FY22 to FY24 plan met. Management's incentive scheme paid between target and maximum on FY26 operating profit and above maximum on cash, which is the behaviour of a team that sets floors it expects to clear.
  5. The calendar. The Department for Work and Pensions demobilisation (-£15m) and the telecoms loss drop-out (+£10m) are both known, both largely in H1, and the employer National Insurance step of April 2025 has annualised. The margin can rise in FY27 because, for the first time since FY24, the £20m to £25m of annual self-help is not spent absorbing a new statutory step.

The FY28 step adds two requirements that are only partly demonstrated. First, retention of efficiency gains: the second set assumes £10m of retained Project PRIO savings and £10m of continuing savings in FY28 against £10m of inflation and £10m of client sharing. Mitie has retained savings before (Interserve; the FY26 margin enhancement), but it has also handed them back (Helix), management has set no PRIO savings target, and the peer evidence is sobering (ISS saved 28 million hours since 2023 and lifted its margin only 40 basis points; Capita delivered its £250m programme and gave most of it back through contract deflation of 26% to 32%). I would describe this strength as demonstrated at half strength: the second set's 60% to 70% retention in output-priced engineering and compliance, and under half in headcount-priced guarding and cleaning, is the right calibration. Second, the Budget: no demonstrated strength covers it, only the pass-through machinery, which recovers about three-quarters of a statutory cost in the year it arrives.

The strengths the thesis would need to acquire, and when they bite.

  • Margin expansion while growing organically without acquisitions. Never shown: the margin went from 2.3% to 4.7% (FY21 to FY24) on Interserve synergies and pruning, then held flat through 41% revenue growth. The FY27 and FY28 steps are again synergy-led. The first test of organic margin expansion is FY29 (the "treadmill year" in the second set's phrase), which is why the forecasts of record add only 10 basis points a year from then.
  • Real pricing in Facilities Compliance. Not shown; private specialists earn 30% to 45% gross margins and 8% to 11% EBIT margins (PTSG 39.6% and 8.4%; Churches Fire 14.6% EBITDA) against Marlowe's legacy 6.7% EBITA; the forecasts of record give it no weight before FY29 and then only as upside.
  • Retention of Marlowe's small clients. Partly shown: group renewals 91% in Q1 FY27 and a ten-fold rise in the cross-sell pipeline to about £700m of annual contract value, against unquantified dis-synergies from competitors withdrawing subcontracted work. Marlowe's own history (attrition spiking to about 20% then falling below 10% within two years) says the base case should assume some churn, which the second set's £5m bid-drag and dis-synergy line does; a 10% loss of Marlowe revenue would cost about £7.5m at a 25% drop-through, which is the size of one year's synergy increment.
  • Public mobilisation and workforce execution. Not shown at a consistent standard (HMP Millsike; NHS payroll), but these affect the political risk to renewals from FY29 more than the FY27 to FY28 numbers.
  • Delivery under new leadership. Relevant only in a break; the precedents are mixed (Serco held guidance through a regional chief executive's departure; Green Landscaping fell 70% and Rentokil's North American turnaround stalled after unplanned departures; Sodexo guided a flat transition year under its new chief executive).

What the peers say about the step itself. Of the named facility services companies that attempted a 50 to 100 basis point margin step from a flat base, the plausibility report finds five that achieved it within two years and two that failed. The successes were cost- and mix-led: ISS (about 0% in 2020 to about 5% by 2025 under OneISS; now targeting 5.5% to 6.0% by 2028), Coor (adjusted EBITA margin 4.5% to 5.0% within a year to Q3 2026, on cost control after the indexation lag), Capita (3.8% to 5.2% in 2025 on a £250m cost programme, with revenue falling), Babcock (to 7.4% on bidding discipline and dropping low-margin contracts), Securitas (to 8.2% on technology mix and pruning), and the acquirer itself (OCS and Atalian: adjusted EBITDA margin 6.4% to 7.2% in 2025 on back-office synergies). The failures were pricing-led: Sodexo (margin down 130 basis points to 3.4% in FY26 under contract reviews and reinvestment, a guided floor missed in a reset) and the historical collapses of Interserve and Carillion (fixed-price mispricing). Mitie's step is of the successful type (overhead synergies, a lapsed shock, a loss-maker exited) and carries none of the failure markers (leverage 1.1x; 90% of contracts indexed; no large fixed-price exposure).

The one blemish the Consumer should weigh. The record of beating guidance coexists with a record of never raising the margin target in three years and of the margin not moving; the step is new ground even though each of its components is old ground. That is why I score this question well above the midpoint but not higher.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

C57

Judgement. The drivers are concentrated on the profit side and diffuse on the revenue side, and the concentration is of the helpful kind for predictability: a handful of disclosed, dated, mostly contracted items rather than a single hidden assumption. Of the roughly £88m of EBIT improvement in the forecasts of record from FY26 (£264m) to FY29 (£352m), about £42m (nearly half) comes from three mechanical items that are already visible (Marlowe's full-year consolidation about £9m, Marlowe synergies rising from £7m to £30m about £23m, the telecoms loss drop-out about £10m); about £57m from retained efficiency (the FY26 margin-enhancement run-rate falling through in FY27 about £22m, then about £20m and £15m from Project PRIO and continuing savings); about £50m from volume at a 7% to 10% incremental margin on about £940m of extra revenue; against which about £70m of drags are modelled (net inflation about £31m over three years, client sharing and bid pricing about £25m, the Department for Work and Pensions demobilisation £15m). The difference between the next four years and the last six is therefore generated by two programmes (Marlowe, PRIO), one calendar (the statutory cost cycle), one date (the end of the Target Operating Model in March 2027) and one policy (the Public Interest Test from FY29), with volume supplied by 3,000 clients none of whom exceeds 10% of revenue. The same five things appear in the second set's list; the AlphaSense dispersion report, searching independently, found the same concentration and could find no disclosed Project PRIO savings target, which it rightly calls the material forecasting gap.

Why the shape differs from the last six years. FY21 to FY26 was revenue-rich and earnings-poor: revenue grew from £2.5bn to £5.6bn (Interserve, Marlowe, bolt-ons, surge work), the margin was rebuilt to 4.7% by FY24 and then frozen by cost shocks, adjusted EPS on the brokers' basis grew 4% and 7% in FY25 and FY26, statutory EPS fell two years running, other items doubled and the share count rose with each deal. The drivers of that period were themselves concentrated (two acquisitions, one pandemic, one inflation shock) but were acquisitive and external. The drivers of FY27 to FY31 are organic and internal, which is what makes them more forecastable: an integration whose actions are listed, a cost programme whose cost is disclosed (£20m to £25m) even if its benefit is not, and a statutory calendar set by one Budget a year.

Concentration by line, FY27 (the 18-month window). The FY27 EBIT bridge of +£40m has ten items, none larger than £22m (the retained savings) and none larger than 7% of EBIT. The largest single uncertainty inside the window is not any of them but the behaviour of the drag items: net inflation guided at £10m to £12m is a management estimate on a £135m gross hurdle, so a 5-point shortfall in the recovery rate (93% to 88%) costs about £7m. The second largest is volume: Technical Services' return to growth in H2 FY27 (modelled flat for the year after -5% in Q1) is worth about £6m of EBIT per 3 points of divisional growth. The third is Marlowe churn (£7.5m per 10% of revenue lost). Everything else is small or known.

Concentration by line, FY28 (the second half of the window and beyond). Three items dominate: the remaining £15m of Marlowe synergies (contracted actions, high confidence), the first retained PRIO savings (£10m, low confidence, no target disclosed), and the Budget (a shock of the FY26 scale would take £10m to £15m, about 20 basis points, off FY28). That is a real concentration risk: two of the three FY28 drivers are either undisclosed or outside management's control, and they are of similar size. It is why I weight FY28 below FY27.

The revenue side is diffuse and that is a strength. No client exceeds 10% of revenue; the public share is 45% to 50% but spread across central government (£377m in Business Services), immigration and justice (£319m), defence, health, local government and education; the private side spans 2,500 contracts. The record order book (£16.3bn, about 2.9 times revenue, 60% recognised within one to three years) and pipeline (£32.5bn, over 70% to be awarded within 18 months) mean FY27 revenue is largely contracted. The one concentrated revenue driver is the Marlowe annualisation in August 2026, which halves reported growth mechanically from 10% to about 5% and is arithmetic, not risk.

How the dispersion report's precedents bear on this. Forecasts that rested on one integration and one cost programme were met when the integration sources were overheads and property (Interserve; OCS-Atalian; Capita) and missed when they depended on retaining customers through a route-density model (Rentokil-Terminix; Green Landscaping, which cut its EBITA forecast 10% in 2026) or on a chief executive who then left. Mitie's Marlowe synergies are of the first kind (£16.5m of the £30m is support functions); its Marlowe revenue is of the second kind, which is why the churn risk is the one to monitor.

For predictability. Concentration in disclosed, dated items means that the H1 FY27 print on 19-Nov-2026 will resolve most of the FY27 uncertainty at once (the in-half synergy figure, the net inflation run-rate, Technical Services' trajectory, Facilities Compliance revenue), and the Budget four days from now will resolve most of the FY28 external uncertainty. That trackability is why the score is above the midpoint despite the dependence on two programmes.

D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

C63

Judgement. Two cones must be kept apart. The cone of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months is narrow for this sector and roughly symmetric: FY27 EBIT £292m to £312m around £304m (minus 4% to plus 3%), margin 4.9% to 5.2%, adjusted EPS on the company's basic basis 14.4p to 15.8p, free cash flow on the company's definition £130m to £165m, and for the half-year to September 2027 an EBITDA margin of 6.3% to 6.7% around 6.5%; the skew is slightly positive on FY27 profit (guidance is a floor that management habitually clears), roughly neutral on FY27 cash (now that the working-capital base is corrected) and modestly negative on FY28 (the Budget and PRIO retention are the two downside items of size; synergy outperformance on the Interserve precedent is the one upside item). The cone of the equity outcome is bimodal and far wider: about 219.8p in cash with a probability of about 89% to 91% (218.5p plus the 1.3p interim dividend that becomes payable if the scheme is not effective by 19-Nov-2026), against a break value of about 160p to 175p with a probability of about 9% to 11%, with a trough of about 130p in a bad break. The financial case is therefore highly predictable; the investment outcome is predictable in probability but not in path. Combining the two: for the memo's purpose of judging whether the forecasts will manifest, the answer is that they very probably will, and that whether anyone listed is there to receive them is the only large uncertainty.

The financial cone, built from the drivers in D-03. On FY27 revenue of about £6,000m, each sensitivity is small: 50 basis points of margin is £30m of EBIT (10%) and about 1.7p of EPS; 1% of revenue is about £5m (1.6%) if it is contract volume and £8m to £10m if it is project volume; a 5-point shortfall in cost recovery is about £7m; £10m of Marlowe revenue churn is about £2.5m; Technical Services at minus 3% for the year instead of flat is about £6m; net finance costs £3m above the second set's path (which I now adopt) is 0.2p of EPS; one point on the tax rate is £3m. Stacking the plausible adverse items (recovery shortfall, Technical Services weakness, Marlowe churn, higher interest) gives about £292m; stacking the plausible favourable ones (synergies ahead of the £15m run-rate, savings retained in full, Technical Services recovering in H2 as its 70% secured sales target implies) gives about £312m. Jefferies' published bracket for FY27 EPS (13.2p bear with a 4.8% margin and zero organic growth; 16.8p bull with 10% organic growth and a 5.2% margin) is wider at minus 10% to plus 15% because its tails assume organic growth of 0% and 10%, both of which are outside anything Mitie has shown in a non-pandemic year; I treat them as the 5th and 95th percentiles.

Why the cone is narrow by the sector's standards. First, most of the FY27 change is mechanical and most of the year's revenue is contracted (D-03). Second, the half-year print on 19-Nov-2026 will land before the window is a quarter old, and H1 FY27 should already show about 4.5% margin against about 4.3% a year earlier; a half-year that confirms the synergy and net-inflation run-rates collapses the FY27 cone further. Third, the business has low operating leverage on volume (a lost contract takes most of its cost with it), so revenue misses translate into small profit misses; the leverage is to margin assumptions, and those are the disclosed items. Fourth, the balance sheet cannot produce a surprise: leverage 1.1x against a 3.0x covenant, fixed-rate notes to 2034, no factoring. Against these, the dispersion precedents warn that the cone widens sharply in two situations that are both live in a break: the year a chief executive leaves without a named successor (Green Landscaping, Rentokil's North America), and the two years after a route-density acquisition when small clients churn (Terminix; Marlowe's own 20% spikes). Neither bites inside the 18-month window if the deal completes.

The skew, item by item. Positive: the guidance floor (the record is seven consecutive upgrades and beats on every floor set since 2023); Marlowe synergies (Deutsche Bank calls £30m "highly conservative" and sees £50m as possible; the Interserve ratio was 1.8 times target); the Budget's benign central case (a 3.7% National Living Wage and a possible youth National Insurance holiday); FY26 cash quality (an outflow of £35.7m of working capital absorbed, not an inflow enjoyed). Negative: PRIO retention (no target; peers retained less than half); the Budget's tail (a 5.5% wage rise or a new employer cost step, 15% to 20% probability, worth 20 basis points of FY28 margin); Marlowe churn (unquantified; competitors have withdrawn subcontracted work); Technical Services (five consecutive quarters of weakness are possible if the public-sector contract losses and lower military training hours persist beyond H1); interest (the record is of misses, and average net debt is running £60m to £80m above the second set's path); the recurring restructuring cash that sits below the adjusted line and depresses statutory EPS and company-definition cash. On balance the FY27 profit skew is positive by about £5m (I would centre a betting line at £306m rather than £304m) and the FY28 skew is negative by about the same.

The equity cone, and what the price says. The price of 210.8p against an expected receipt of about 219.8p in roughly four and a half months (mid-March 2027 on the parties' timetable), discounted at 4% a year, and a break value of 160p to 175p, implies a break probability of about 8% to 10%; at a 150p break value the implied probability is about 9%. My own estimate from the evidence is 9% to 11%: shareholder approval is secured; the European Commission and the Investment Security Unit have cleared; the combined share of UK facilities management is below 20% and of UK security and cleaning about 16% and 12%; the Interserve precedent was cleared at Phase 1 in seven weeks; the Authority's Phase 1 caseload fell 17% in the year to August 2026 and it clears most deals unconditionally or with simple remedies; and the long-stop date of 21-Jul-2027 gives nine months of room. Against that: the Authority can define local catchments narrowly in regional cleaning and security; third-party objections from rivals are plausible (a former Sodexo commercial manager says the deal "will materially reduce competition" for national integrated contracts); public-sector overlap is politically salient; and OCS can walk away from a Phase 2 reference without penalty. The spread is therefore fairly priced; a holder is being paid about 10% annualised for a 9% to 11% chance of a 20% to 30% loss. There is no edge, which is itself a conclusion for the memo.

The break cone within the 18 months. If the offer lapses (most likely between December 2026 and March 2027 on a Phase 2 reference), the standalone forecasts would be met or nearly met, but the equity would pass through three phases: an immediate fall toward 150p to 165p as arbitrage holders sell and the bid premium is removed (Direct Line fell 16% on the day Ageas walked; Sainsbury's drifted to 2003 levels after the Asda block); a period of three to nine months in which the buyback restarts (£81m remaining of the £100m programme; Deutsche Bank and the second set both expect an immediate restart; Jefferies estimates a full £100m buyback is worth about 5% of EPS), a successor chief executive is named and a new plan is written; and then a re-rating toward 9.5x to 10x only if FY27 delivers the 5% margin and the new plan is credible. Rightmove (held 10% to 15% above its undisturbed price after REA walked) and Anglo American (re-rated 20% on a radical simplification) show that a break need not be permanent damage; Hammerson and Renishaw show that it can be. Mitie's break case sits between: a strong operating record and balance sheet, but a leadership transition and a regime that refuses re-ratings to UK mid-caps.

Predictability, summarised. The financial forecasts for the next 18 months are among the most predictable in the Set 1 peer group (a FY27 EBIT cone of about plus or minus 3% to 4%, against Jefferies' minus 10% to plus 15% tails and Sodexo's 130 basis point miss). The equity outcome is a weighted coin: expected value about 212p against a price of 210.8p. The score reflects the narrowness and mild positive skew of the financial cone, tempered by the bimodal equity outcome that the forecasts cannot change.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

C54

Judgement. Moderately helpful, and unusually concentrated in time: over the next six months the company will give one full disclosure (the H1 FY27 results on 19-Nov-2026) and probably one thin one (a Q3 trading update in late January 2027, as in January 2026), and both are constrained by the offer period, during which the Takeover Code discourages new forecasts and Jefferies describes trading updates as "largely academic". Within those limits, Mitie's disclosure is better than the sector's on the cost side (explicit inflation bridges, recovery rates, synergy run-rates, programme costs, divisional revenue and organic growth by quarter, order intake, pipeline, renewal rate) and weaker on the three items the thesis most needs for the FY28 forecast and the break case: Project PRIO's savings (no target), Marlowe's post-acquisition revenue and churn (consolidated into Facilities Compliance, which is itself inside Business Services), and segment margins, which come only at the half year. The most useful monitoring tool over the six months is not a company disclosure at all but the Competition and Markets Authority's case page, which will publish the Phase 1 launch, the statutory deadline and the decision.

What the 19-Nov-2026 results will disclose, and what to test them against. Mitie's half-year reporting gives: revenue and organic growth by division and by sub-division (security, hygiene and landscapes, Facilities Compliance, central government, immigration and justice, Spain; engineering, defence, projects, health and local government); operating profit before other items and margin by division; other items by category; net finance costs; free cash flow on the company definition with a working-capital figure; average and closing net debt; the renewal rate; order book and pipeline; Marlowe synergies delivered in the half; the inflation bridge; and reiterated guidance. The tests, from the forecasts of record: H1 revenue about £2,905m (+8.5%; organic about 4%); H1 operating margin about 4.5% (EBIT about £132m) against about 4.3% a year earlier; EBITDA margin on the brokers' basis about 6.3%; Technical Services down about 3% with a stated H2 recovery; Facilities Compliance revenue about £250m with some statement of organic growth; Marlowe synergies about £6m in the half; net inflation tracking £10m to £12m; other items about £55m to £60m for the half (Target Operating Model, PRIO, integration, OCS costs); closing net debt £480m to £500m; a free cash outflow in the half consistent with a full year of £145m-plus; guidance reiterated in the Rule 28 form. Two wording points matter: whether the £150m free cash flow guidance is stated before or after OCS transaction costs, and whether management attaches any figure to PRIO savings for FY28.

What the disclosure will not show. Contract-level attrition (the first set's Risks report called this the hidden variable); whether any Marlowe small clients have left and how many of the 300 departures were field engineers; the Public Interest Test exposure by contract (the own-report on the Test confirms the company does not map it); compliance organic growth separately from acquisition carry-over; the price and volume split of organic growth except in passing; and anything about the post-FY27 plan, which the offer period and the pending succession both suppress. The AlphaSense narrative report is right that no broker has an integrated cash model that ties working capital, restructuring cash and amortisation together, partly because the company's free cash flow definition is not fully reconciled in its releases; the FY26 figures (company free cash flow £162m; FactSet operating cash flow £249m; a £35.7m working-capital outflow; about £63m of cash other items; lease capital repayments £67.5m) do not reconcile on the second set's assumptions, which means cash tax in FY26 was very low (consistent with Deutsche Bank's 15% cash rate) and the definition probably excludes something (transaction costs are the candidate). The memo should treat the company's free cash flow line as a guidance metric, not as a modelling line, until the H1 statement is read.

External disclosure that substitutes for the company's. Over the six months the following external items will move the case more than the company's own words: the Autumn Budget (28-Oct-2026: the April 2027 National Living Wage, any employer National Insurance change, pensions auto-enrolment); the Low Pay Commission's final recommendation (due by end-October); the Authority's Phase 1 launch and 40-working-day deadline, then its decision; the Cabinet Office's departmental five-year insourcing roadmaps and the Insourcing Unit's first guidance; the Transport for London cleaning pilot (250 staff seconded from 28-Sep-2026, twelve months); and peers' prints (ISS Q3 in November with raised organic guidance; Serco; Capita). Mitie's own disclosure of the statutory cost effect on FY28 will come, if at all, as a sentence at the H1 results.

Comparison with peers. ISS discloses more (quarterly organic growth, margin guidance and a mid-term target bridge), Serco about the same with better cash disclosure (debtor days 16), Capita less. Mitie's cost bridges are the best in the UK group; its contract and segment disclosure is average; its cash reconciliation is below average. In an offer period the comparison is academic, and the score reflects that the case can be monitored adequately for FY27 and poorly for the items that decide FY28 and the break case.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

C60

Judgement. The internal transmission is unusually clear from targets to cost and margin lines and to operating profit, and it then blurs in two places on the way to EPS: the share count (two bases, 13.6p and 12.6p, and a count that moved from 1,255m to 1,302m in six months) and the line between adjusted and statutory profit (other items of £113m, of which about £25m a year is recurring restructuring in substance). The external transmission is also clear, and Mitie is more explicit than any UK peer about it: statutory wage and tax changes enter through a disclosed bridge (gross cost, recovery rate, net drag), public-sector policy enters through the renewal rate on a disclosed share of revenue, and demand enters through order intake and pipeline with a known mobilisation lag. The weak links in the external chain are the lag and cap structure of indexation (annual resets; about 10% of contracts unindexed; change-of-law clauses that recover statutory costs only after negotiation) and the absence of any disclosure of which public contracts fall under the Public Interest Test. Overall the chain from "at least 5% margin and £150m of free cash flow" to the forecasts of record can be written down line by line, which is what makes the forecasts testable; the chain from the forecasts to a quoted EPS requires the memo to state its basis every time.

The internal chain, written out. Target: operating margin of at least 5% in FY27 on revenue growth in the high single digits over the plan (delivered a year early). Operational drivers: (i) Marlowe synergies, £15m run-rate by FY27 and £30m by FY28, from support functions (£16.5m), field-force optimisation, IT migration onto IBM Maximo and property exits, landing in Business Services' margin (6.25% to 6.45% in FY27); (ii) the margin-enhancement programme, £25m gross a year from back-office automation, organisational design and procurement (Coupa; 900 preferred suppliers for 70% of spend), landing in central costs (£58m to £52m) and divisional overheads; (iii) the telecoms exit, about £10m, landing in Technical Services' margin (5.15% to 5.3%); (iv) net inflation, £10m to £12m, landing across both divisions; (v) the Department for Work and Pensions demobilisation, £15m, landing in Business Services (central government revenue down 14%); (vi) volume, from a £1.6bn quarterly order intake and a £32.5bn pipeline with over 70% awarded within 18 months, landing at a 7% to 10% incremental margin; (vii) Project PRIO, £20m to £25m of cost in other items in FY27, savings undisclosed. Summed: EBIT about £304m, 5.05%. Below the line: net finance costs about £36m (£360m of notes at 4.65%, lease interest about £10m, facility usage), tax at 25% on adjusted profit, Landmarc minority about £8m, attributable profit about £192m on this update's inputs. Then the two blurred links: divided by about 1,285m weighted basic shares it is about 15.0p on the company's basis; divided by about 1,340m diluted shares it is about 14.3p to 14.5p on the brokers' and FactSet basis; and after about £52m of recurring restructuring, transformation and PRIO cash costs taxed at 25% it is about 12p of "clean" EPS, against about 8p statutory after all other items. A memo reader can follow every step; the risk is that the reader quotes the wrong EPS.

The milestones and measures to hold the company to. Marlowe synergies delivered (£7m FY26; about £13m FY27 in-year; £30m FY28 run-rate); Facilities Compliance revenue (£421m FY26; about £500m run-rate) and, when disclosed, its organic growth and margin (legacy Marlowe 6.7%); the renewal rate (84% FY26; 91% Q1 FY27; the second set assumes 85% to 90%); organic growth by division (group about 4%; Technical Services from minus 5% to flat); the inflation bridge (gross £135m, recovery £125m); central costs (£58m to £52m); other items by category and in cash; free cash flow against £150m; average net debt and covenant leverage (0.82x); return on invested capital (18.1% toward above 20%); the share count (voting rights monthly). Each is disclosed at least semi-annually except compliance margin and PRIO savings.

The external chain, written out. (i) Statutory labour costs: a National Living Wage rise of x% on a wage bill of about £3.3bn, of which perhaps £2bn is at or near the floor, produces a gross cost; indexation on about 90% of contracts recovers 93% to 95% of wage inflation with an annual reset lag of up to a year; change-of-law clauses recover about three-quarters of statutory employer costs after negotiation; the residue (£10m to £12m a year; £18.6m before savings in FY26) is the net drag on margin, 15 to 20 basis points. The Budget on 28-Oct-2026 sets the April 2027 inputs to this chain; the transmission to FY28 EBIT is arithmetic once the rates are known. (ii) Public-sector policy: the Public Interest Test applies from 1-Apr-2027 to central government contracts above £1m at re-tender; defence, security-vetted and health procurements are largely exempt or voluntary; the exposed book is perhaps £800m to £1bn (central government £377m in Business Services plus parts of health, local government and education in Technical Services); the effect runs through the renewal rate on 5-to-10-year contracts, so it reaches revenue from FY29 and profit at an 8% to 10% drop-through (about £3m to £5m a year in the base case). (iii) Demand: order intake converts to revenue with a 6-to-12-month mobilisation lag; projects (Facilities Transformation £1,401m in FY26) follow client capital budgets with phasing noise and about £25m a year of working capital. (iv) Automation: clients claim savings at once on cost-plus contracts and at re-tender on fixed-price ones, so Mitie's own deployment deflates revenue per contract in guarding and helpdesk work by perhaps 0.3 points of growth a year and lifts margin only where output is priced (engineering, compliance). (v) Competition and consolidation: the OCS combination removes a low bidder from national tenders (mild positive for sector pricing), while private-equity compliance platforms (PTSG, Andwis) contest Marlowe's small clients. (vi) Rates and currency: negligible (£1.2m a year per 50 basis points; 94% sterling).

Where the external chain is opaque. Nobody outside the company can see the indexation lag by contract, the share of contracts with change-of-law clauses, or which contracts fall under the Test; the AlphaSense report on the Test could not map a single renewal date. These opacities matter for FY29 onward and for the bear case, not for the 18-month window, where the only external input of size is one Budget.

Against peers. Serco's chain is simpler (indexed public contracts, 16 debtor days, little acquisition noise) and ISS's is better disclosed (quarterly organic growth, a published margin bridge to 2028); Capita's is murkier (business exits, cyber costs, remediation). Mitie sits in the upper half on transmission clarity and in the lower half on the clarity of the profit measure itself, which is the Industry Digest's "106% gap" criticism in another form.

D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

C55

Judgement. Over the latest six-month window the share price has moved entirely idiosyncratically (correlation with the equal-weighted sector basket 0.02; beta 0.09; R-squared 0%; Mitie +21.7% against the basket's +3.2%), and the multiple (12.0x 24-month-forward consensus EPS, the 95th percentile of the stock's own ten-year range of 7.3x to 11.3x at the dashboard's bands) is set by one thing, the 218.5p cash offer, not by the industry. That is the expected signature of a stock in an offer period and it carries a precise meaning for predictability: while the offer stands, the price is pinned and sector moves do not matter; if the offer lapses, the pin is removed and the stock reverts to a multiple the sector will set. The pre-bid data tell the Consumer what that reversion looks like. In the two years before the bid the rolling six-month R-squared ranged from 3% to 19% (beta 0.5 to 0.9), and in the three months immediately before the bid (windows ending April to June 2026) it rose to 29% to 33% with a beta of 0.73 to 0.85, the highest sector linkage in the series. Mitie was becoming more of a sector stock, not less, when the bid arrived, and the sector it was tracking had re-rated (ISS +44% over twelve months; Loomis +57%; Securitas +22%; facility outsourcers about +22%). The multiple is therefore company-driven today by construction, and would be industry-driven within weeks of a break.

What the rolling series shows, period by period. From November 2024 to September 2025 the correlation sat at 0.3 to 0.46 and R-squared at 8% to 21%: a mid-cap UK outsourcer moving with its European peers about a sixth of the time, with the rest explained by its own prints (FY25 results, the Marlowe announcement in June 2025, the surge-contract lapping). From October 2025 to February 2026 the linkage fell to R-squared of 3% to 6%: the Marlowe integration, the Q2 FY26 organic slowdown to 4% and the Technical Services weakness made the stock trade on its own news, and it fell 16.6% in the three months before the bid while the sector did not. From March to June 2026 the linkage jumped to R-squared of 29% to 33%: with no company news of size, the stock rode the sector re-rating led by ISS. Then the bid: the window ending 31-Jul-2026 shows R-squared of 2% and every window since converges to zero, because a single-day move of about 40% on 21-Jul-2026 and the pinned drift since dominate the variance.

The pairwise detail confirms the reading. No peer explains more than 5% of Mitie's variance in the latest window; the highest is Serco (correlation 0.22, R-squared 5%), the only other UK public-services outsourcer, which suggests the residual sector linkage that survives the pin is UK-policy-driven (the Public Interest Test and Budget headlines move both). ISS, the best European comparator on business model, shows a negative correlation (-0.11) in the latest window, which is meaningless statistically and simply says the bid severed the link that had been strongest in H1 2026.

The multiple itself. Mitie's 24-month-forward P/E of 12.0x compares with its five-year range of 6.5x to 12.4x and median 9.3x, and with the dashboard's ten-year bands (median 9.1x "prudent", 75th percentile 10.2x "defendable", 90th 11.3x "mania"). It also compares with the sector today: ISS 11.8x (63rd percentile of its own history), Serco 11.7x (32nd), Sodexo 13.6x, Rentokil 12.1x (1st percentile, after its own troubles), Elis 9.8x, Compass 18.1x. So the offer multiple is at the top of Mitie's history but only modestly above where the two closest listed peers now trade; the sector re-rating of 2026 narrowed the gap between the offer and "sector fair". That matters for the break case in two ways. First, a lapse would not return the stock to 9.1x by default: the sector-linked level is nearer 9.5x to 10.5x, and the pre-bid evidence that Mitie was tracking the sector at a beta of about 0.8 supports using it. Second, the discount a broken Mitie would carry to ISS and Serco would be for its own reasons (leadership succession, UK public exposure, the adjusted-to-statutory gap, arbitrage selling), so the break-case multiple is 9.5x to 10x rather than the peers' 11.7x to 11.8x, which is what gives my 160p to 175p (on 24-month-forward EPS of about 17p to 17.5p at the model's buyback price, or 17.8p at the second set's).

What this means for predictability and for the fair multiple. For the next 18 months, if the offer completes, the multiple is irrelevant: the return is the spread. If it lapses, the multiple is the largest single variable in the holder's outcome (each turn of P/E is about 17p to 18p, roughly 10% of the break value) and it will be set about two-thirds by the sector (where ISS and Serco trade, the direction of UK mid-cap sentiment, the market regime that currently refuses re-ratings) and one-third by Mitie (the H1 print, the succession, the buyback restart). The dashboard's pinned readings are consistent with this: technical momentum B (a pinned uptrend, the 200-day average rising for twelve months, relative strength percentile 73), sell-side earnings momentum C (revisions of +1% to +2%, a momentum score of 1, no buy ratings), valuation F (the 95th percentile of its own history). A stock whose price is 12.0x earnings because someone has agreed to pay that in cash is company-driven in the purest sense, and the F reading is a description of the offer, not a judgement of over-valuation. For the memo's topic #11, the guideline fair multiple of 9.52x is the right centre for the break case and I would put a range of 9x (a bad break with a long succession) to 10.5x (a clean break with the buyback restarted and the sector holding its 2026 re-rating) around it.
