Layer 4: Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

The Investment Analyst's heading The fourth set: Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative

The fourth set as a whole ❌ D48 written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Guidance, consensus and the narrative already reflect almost everything our assessment of the 18-month forecasts says, and the remaining gaps are small, academic or adverse. Guidance ("at least 5%" margin; £150m of free cash flow) is a floor set by a management that has cleared every floor since 2023, and it is locked by Rule 28 of the Takeover Code until completion; our forecasts of record sit a hair above it (5.05%; cash at or around £150m on the company's definition once the FY26 working-capital base is corrected), so the gap is favourable but worth perhaps £4m of EBIT and nothing to a holder who is paid 218.5p regardless. Consensus (three brokers, all Hold at the offer price, revisions of 1% to 3%, a buy share of 0%) is our operating path to the pound: FY27 EBIT £301m against our £304m, FY28 £331m against £333m, FY29 £353m against £352m; the only differences are composition (buybacks worth 2% to 4% of EPS from FY29 in a break case; the brokers' diluted EPS basis against the company's basic basis; the cash definitions; about £25m a year of restructuring cash that we treat as operating cost). There is no latent upgrade cycle to be found in a pinned consensus that already embeds the margin step, and in a break the first move would be down. The narrative is complete on the two things that decide the 90% outcome (the spread and the Authority's timetable), with two small arithmetical gaps in a holder's favour (the 1.3p interim dividend payable if the scheme is not effective by 19-Nov-2026 lifts the expected receipt to about 219.8p and the spread to 4.3%; retail commentary that quotes a spread to 221.6p likely double-counts a paid dividend), and incomplete on the standalone mechanisms that decide the 10% outcome, where what investors do not know is mostly unfavourable (the share count, the recurring cash costs, the Budget's grip on FY28, Marlowe churn) and only partly favourable (cash quality, the buyback restart, the sector's re-rating). The investment conclusion the memo should draw is that the price is right: 210.8p against an expected value of about 212p (about 90% of 219.8p discounted, plus about 10% of 160p to 175p) means the market has priced the break probability at roughly our 9% to 11% and has left no edge on either side. For topic #12, the model's return at the guideline fair multiple of 9.52x (minus 10.2% to September 2027; plus 6.6% to March 2029, with minus 25% from the multiple and plus 17% to 34% from earnings) is the break-case path only and should be presented as such; the completion case is a return of about 4% in four to five months (about 10% annualised) with no multiple, earnings or dividend component beyond the interim. For topic #10, the stock's lifecycle phase is "acquired": a thesis that broadened (compliance, projects, technology) and was bought before it could stall or re-rate on its own merits; in a break it would restart at "turning up" from a lower base under new leadership, with the fair multiple set by a sector that has re-rated above Mitie's own history. The gap between our assessment and what is reflected is therefore neither a reason to buy for upgrades nor a reason to sell for downgrades; it is a reason to hold for the spread only if the holder is content with a roughly one-in-ten chance of a 20% to 30% loss, and to treat every standalone number in this and the earlier sets as the floor they fall back on.

Guidance

1 question
I-0158
I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

C58

Judgement. Guidance will almost certainly be reiterated, not raised and not cut, at every event before completion, for a structural reason that overrides trading: the FY27 targets are profit forecasts under Rule 28.1 of the Takeover Code, and changing them during the offer period requires reports from the reporting accountants and the financial advisers. The relevant probabilities at the H1 FY27 results on 19-Nov-2026 are therefore about 88% reiterated, about 7% reduced (which would require trading bad enough to trigger a disclosure obligation, in practice a profit warning) and about 5% raised (a formal raise is procedurally heavy; the realistic form of a "raise" is a qualitative "ahead" or "upper end" remark, and the "at least 5%" formulation already permits a beat without one). At the Q3 trading update in late January 2027 the same split applies with the cut probability lower (about 4%), because the Budget's effects fall in FY28 and H1 will be known. If the offer completes in calendar Q1 2027, there are no further events for a listed holder, and the probability that FY27 guidance is in substance met or beaten at the point of delisting is about 85% on margin and about 70% on free cash flow (company definition, before or after transaction costs depending on how the company states it on 19-Nov-2026). In the break case (about 10%), the FY27 results in June 2027 would most likely show the margin floor met or beaten (about 80%) and free cash flow of £140m to £160m against £150m (met, about 55% to 60%), and new FY28 guidance would be set by a management in transition, most probably as a conservative floor ("at least" the FY27 margin plus 10 to 30 basis points) with a 30% chance of a rebase to absorb a new programme under a new chief executive. Secondarily, there is no guidance for 18 months to three years out: the plan ends in FY27, the post-FY27 ambitions (projects to £2bn, compliance to £1.5bn, share of wallet in the top 50 clients) carry no margin or cash numbers, and in the break case the earliest new mid-term targets would be a capital markets event under the successor in late 2027 or 2028, which on the ISS and Serco patterns would set a margin target of about 5.5% by FY30 and a cash conversion target; that would be a setting of targets, not a raise, and I put the probability of such targets being met over the following three years at about 60%.

What would drive each outcome on 19-Nov-2026.

  • Reiterated (about 88%). H1 organic growth of about 4% (Deutsche Bank's full-year assumption is 4%; Jefferies' 5%); an H1 operating margin of about 4.5% against about 4.3% a year earlier, which annualises to the 5% floor once the H2 seasonal uplift (indexation timing, project phasing, the Department for Work and Pensions drag lapping) is applied; Marlowe synergies of about £6m in the half against the £15m run-rate; net inflation tracking £10m to £12m; the renewal rate above 85%; the pipeline at or above £32bn; net debt seasonally higher at £480m to £500m; other items of £55m to £60m for the half. This is the base case because every item is either contracted or already visible in the Q1 update, and because management has every incentive (completion of the offer, the retention awards of 150% of salary for the executives, Rule 28) to deliver a clean, unchanged statement.
  • Reduced (about 7%). An H1 margin below about 4.3%, which would mean the Department for Work and Pensions loss and Technical Services' decline (worse than the minus 3% modelled for the half) were not offset by synergies and savings; a withdrawal or qualification of the £150m free cash flow guidance because of projects working capital, retail payment terms or the inclusion of OCS transaction costs; a disclosed Marlowe revenue loss of size; or a contract provision. The precedent for a cut in an offer period exists (Spire's 22% fall on its March 2025 labour-cost warning was outside an offer period but shows the sector's sensitivity), but Mitie's Q1 update, three months into the half, showed none of these.
  • Raised (about 5%). An H1 margin of 4.8% or more with synergies running ahead and savings retained in full, prompting a shift from "at least 5%" to a figure; the Code makes this administratively costly for no benefit to the board, which has already agreed a price. The more likely form of good news is qualitative: "trading ahead of our expectations" with the forecast unchanged.

The Budget (28-Oct-2026) and guidance. The Budget affects FY28, for which there is no guidance, so it cannot cause a FY27 revision directly; its indirect effect would be a sentence on 19-Nov-2026 about the April 2027 National Living Wage (indicative £13.18, +3.7%) and any employer cost change. A 5.5% wage settlement or a new employer step would be described as a FY28 headwind of a stated size (on the FY26 precedent about a quarter unrecovered); a youth National Insurance holiday would be described as a FY28 tailwind. Neither would alter the FY27 floor.

The record that frames these probabilities. Mitie's behaviour since the October 2023 Capital Markets Event is the "sandbagging precision" archetype on profit and cash and the honest-tracker archetype on revenue: floors set (£190m FY24 EBIT; at least £120m FY26 free cash flow; at least 5% FY27 margin; £150m cash), floors cleared (£210m; £162m), seven consecutive consensus upgrades, adjacent ambitions raised (projects to £2bn, compliance to £1.5bn, savings to £25m) while the headline margin target was never raised and FY26 revenue was slightly missed (£5,619m against about £5,650m). A management with this record, in an offer period, reiterates. Peers' latest prints point the same way for the sector: ISS raised 2026 organic growth guidance toward 7% and set 5.5% to 6.0% for 2028; Coor recovered to a 5.0% margin; Serco held its 6.2% margin while absorbing National Insurance; Sodexo is the exception (a 130 basis point fall and a flat FY27 transition year), for reasons of contract reviews and reinvestment that Mitie's indexed, overhead-synergy-led case does not share.

Our assessment against guidance. The forecasts of record sit at 5.05% against "at least 5%" (EBIT £304m against about £300m), and my free cash flow lean of £145m to £155m sits at the £150m guide (the second set's £135m was below it for the working-capital reason corrected in the preliminary). Our view is therefore marginally above guidance on margin and at guidance on cash, which is where a conservative guider's outturn should sit. The gap is small and, if the offer completes, unmonetisable: a beat of the FY27 floor accrues to CD&R. In the break case the gap becomes modestly valuable (a 5.1% to 5.2% FY27 outturn would be the first visible evidence of the standalone margin story and would anchor the successor's first guidance). The score reflects a favourable but small and largely academic gap, and a record that makes "met or beaten" the overwhelming base case.

Back to contents

Sell-side estimates

1 question
I-0250
I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

C50

Judgement. Consensus will be held, within about plus or minus 2%, at every event before completion, and the net breadth of revisions will stay near zero, because the only three brokers in FactSet (Jefferies, Deutsche Bank, Investec; Goldman Sachs restricted) rate the shares Hold with targets of 218.5p to 221p anchored on the offer, their fundamental revision cycle is suspended, and their estimates already sit on the guidance floor (FY27 EBIT £301m, 5.0%; free cash flow £150m). I put the probabilities for FY27 consensus EPS between now and completion at about 70% held (within 2%), 20% up by 2% to 4% (a clean H1 that lets Jefferies and Investec nudge the margin to 5.1%), 10% down by 2% to 5% (a soft H1 or a cash qualification). Breadth cannot improve: the buy share is 0% and will stay there, and coverage will cease at completion. The one certain revision is a mechanical one that matters for the memo: consensus FY27 EPS of 14.5p is on a diluted basis of about 1,310m to 1,340m shares, so the headline "+15% growth from 12.6p" is the brokers' comparison, while the company's basic-basis growth from 13.6p is about +7% to +12%; and Deutsche Bank's standalone count of 1,255m, unchanged after the buyback suspension, overstates its standalone EPS by 3% to 4%, a correction that would be made the day the offer lapsed. In the break case, consensus would move in two stages: first a cut to FY28 and FY29 of about 3% to 6% (the leadership transition, the share count, Project PRIO retention, the Budget, no buyback assumed) within weeks of the lapse, then upgrades of 2% to 5% over the following six to twelve months as the £81m remaining buyback restarted (Jefferies: a full £100m is about 5% of EPS) and the H1 and FY27 prints confirmed the margin; breadth would widen (coverage from three brokers to five or seven as Peel Hunt, Stifel and Berenberg resumed) and the buy share would jump from 0% to perhaps 50% to 70% at a price of 155p to 170p, which is 9x to 10x. Secondarily, for 18 months to three years out, FY29 consensus (EBIT £353m; EPS 17.9p on three brokers spanning 17.4p to 18.1p) is in line with our EBIT of £352m and our EPS of 17.9p before buybacks; the balance of revision risk is slightly up (buybacks, synergies above £30m, a benign Budget) against slightly down (the Public Interest Test from FY29, which we put at about 0.4% of revenue below consensus, and any new restructuring programme under new management), so held, with a modest upward lean of 2% to 4% in a break case where the buyback runs.

Why consensus is pinned now, and what the revision data already show. FactSet's weekly series shows FY27 (its "FY2026" row) consensus EPS at 14.7p mean, up 1.4% over one month and 2.9% over twelve; FY28 up 0.9% over one month and 3.2% over twelve; EBITDA revisions 0% to 1%; sales 0% to 1%; target prices up 3%, 8% and 10% over one, three and six months (toward the offer); the buy share down from 89% to 0% over six months; the dashboard's revision-momentum score 1 and its sell-side earnings momentum rating C. Those are the readings of a stock whose estimates drift up with the margin step already embedded and whose ratings have been retired by a cash bid. The latest estimate dates (Jefferies 9-Sep-2026, Deutsche Bank 7-Sep-2026, Investec 21-Jul-2026) show that Investec has not touched its model since the bid day; Deutsche Bank's 21-Jul-2026 note cut standalone EPS by 3% to 6% for the cancelled buyback and left the share count unchanged. Jefferies has not republished a full post-suspension model. Consensus is therefore both pinned and slightly stale, and the dispersion for FY29 (17.4p to 18.1p) is wider than for FY27 (14.2p to 14.6p) because the brokers differ on buybacks and on Marlowe's revenue path (Jefferies FY29 revenue £6,755m against Deutsche Bank's £6,442m).

What each outcome at the H1 FY27 results would need.

  • Held (about 70%). The base case in I-01: H1 organic about 4%, margin about 4.5%, synergies about £6m, guidance reiterated. Brokers would roll forward with FY27 EBIT at £300m to £305m and the mean EPS drifting by less than 1%.
  • Up 2% to 4% (about 20%). An H1 margin of 4.6% or more with the inflation drag tracking the low end (£10m) and Technical Services back to growth in the half; Jefferies and Investec would move to 5.1% and about 14.8p, taking the median to about 14.7p. The ceiling on the upgrade is the offer: no broker will put effort into a standalone upgrade for a stock whose price is fixed.
  • Down 2% to 5% (about 10%). An H1 margin below 4.3%, a cash qualification, a Marlowe revenue disclosure or a Budget headwind for FY28 large enough to pull FY28 estimates down (a FY26-scale shock would be worth about 1p of FY28 EPS). Downgrades to FY28 are more likely than to FY27 even in this case.

The break case in more detail, because it is where estimates would actually move. On a lapse the three brokers would unanchor within days (Deutsche Bank's pre-bid target was 195p on 10x EV/EBIT; Jefferies' was 170p on 12x FY27 EPS; both would be reset to standalone methods at 9x to 10x). Estimates would first fall: the share count correction (about 3% to 4% at Deutsche Bank), a leadership-transition haircut to FY28 (the Capita and Green Landscaping precedents show 10% to 14% cuts when a chief executive leaves without a successor, but Mitie's succession is announced and the chief executive stays until a name is found, so I would expect 3% to 6% on FY28), and the removal of any residual synergy optimism. Then they would rise: the buyback restart (the board resumed buybacks immediately after the Marlowe completion and after the FY26 results; Deutsche Bank's 30-Jun-2025 note said the paused programme would have restarted at once had Marlowe failed), the FY27 margin print, and the sector's revision momentum (ISS rated B on earnings momentum with a revision score of 12; Serco 10; Loomis 9). Breadth would recover before estimates did: the lapsed-bid precedents (Direct Line, Rightmove, Currys) show ratings moving to Buy on value within a quarter even where estimates were cut. The net breadth of revisions in a break would therefore read "ratings up, estimates down, then both up" over about nine months.

Our assessment against consensus, line by line. FY27: EBIT £304m against £301m (+1%); revenue in line; EPS 15.2p on the company's basic basis, about 14.6p on the brokers' diluted basis against 14.5p (in line); free cash flow £145m to £155m (company definition) against £150m (in line; the model's £169m is on a different basis). FY28: EBIT £333m against £331m; EPS 17.0p (company basis, with £50m of buybacks) or about 16.8p diluted without buybacks against 16.6p (in line to +2%). FY29: EBIT £352m against £353m; EPS 18.6p with buybacks against 17.9p (+4%) or 17.9p without (in line); revenue 0.4% below. The honest summary is that our operating path is consensus's operating path, and that the differences (capital allocation, cash definitions, the share-count basis, the recurring cash cost of other items) are composition, not direction. That leaves the gap between our view and consensus close to neutral for an investment case: there is no latent upgrade cycle in a stock whose consensus is pinned to a bid and whose standalone estimates already embed the margin step, and the only sources of upgrades (buybacks, synergies beyond £30m, a benign Budget) are break-case items that would first have to survive a break-case cut. I score it at the midpoint.

Back to contents

Stock market narrative

1 question
I-0342
I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

❌ D42

Judgement. The narrative is now almost entirely about the offer spread and the Competition and Markets Authority timetable, and on those two subjects it is complete and accurate, with one arithmetical slip (part of the retail commentary quotes a spread to 221.6p that double-counts the 3.1p dividend if it has been paid, and nobody mentions the 1.3p interim dividend that becomes payable if the scheme is not effective by 19-Nov-2026). The standalone operating story, which matters only in a break, is articulated unevenly: the headline items (the 5% margin target, the £150m cash target, the Marlowe synergy phasing and the Interserve precedent, the National Insurance pass-through, the Public Interest Test, the chief executive's retirement, Jefferies' 115p stress case and the 151p undisturbed price) are well known to the two houses whose research exists (Jefferies and Deutsche Bank; Investec's models are not in the database and Peel Hunt, Stifel, Berenberg and Goldman Sachs have published nothing on Mitie since the bid); the mechanisms behind the forecasts are known to one house each or to none. In particular, no one articulates (a) that the FY27 margin step is a release of self-help that a new statutory step would re-consume, so that the whole FY28 margin forecast is a forecast about one Budget; (b) that consensus FY27 EPS is on a diluted basis and the company's basic-basis growth is 7% to 12%, not 15%, nor that Deutsche Bank's standalone count is stale; (c) that about £25m a year of restructuring cash is recurring in substance, so that clean EPS runs about 1.5p a year below adjusted; (d) that FY26 cash was earned against a £35.7m working-capital outflow, which cuts the other way and is a point in Mitie's favour that nobody makes; (e) which public contracts fall under the Public Interest Test; (f) that the break-case leadership gap is an announced succession, not a vacancy. The dynamics that would surprise investors in a break are therefore mostly the negative ones they have not priced in detail (dilution, recurring cash costs, Marlowe churn, the Budget's effect on FY28), partly offset by positives they have also not priced (cash quality, the buyback restart, the sector's re-rating). For 18 months to three years out, the narrative is essentially empty: there is no post-FY27 plan to discuss, and the only three-year items anyone mentions are the Marlowe £30m and the Test.

Coverage, stated plainly. Houses identifiable as covering Mitie: Jefferies (Allen Wells), Deutsche Bank (David Brockton and James Beard are named in the AlphaSense material), Investec, Goldman Sachs (shown in FactSet as "Without" a rating, consistent with a restricted adviser), and, pre-bid, Peel Hunt and Stifel. AlphaSense holds research for two of them (Jefferies and Deutsche Bank); the narrative report covers both with their own documents, which is 100% of what the database holds and about a third of the houses that exist. Investor commentary is thin: the report found retail arbitrage threads discussing the "5.2% raw spread" and the EU and national-security clearances, and institutional warnings that Phase 1 timing is unpredictable; nothing about standalone operations. That is the normal shape of commentary on a UK mid-cap in an offer period and it means the memo cannot rely on any investor having stress-tested the break case.

Dynamic by dynamic: who articulates it, how, and the gap.

  1. The statutory-cost dependency of the margin step. Deutsche Bank's 8-Jun-2026 bridge has the pieces (gross inflation £135m, recovery £125m, £22m of margin enhancement absorbing the £11m residue) and Jefferies (29-Jun-2026) names unrecovered National Insurance as the material downside; neither states that the step exists only because no new shock arrives, nor that the Budget of 28-Oct-2026 is the pivot for FY28. Partly known; the mechanism is not.
  2. Marlowe synergy phasing and the Interserve precedent. Widely articulated by both houses since June 2025, with Deutsche Bank arguing the £30m is understated by more than half on sector precedent. Fully known.
  3. Marlowe dis-synergies and small-client churn. Deutsche Bank only (30-Jun-2025 on the £11,000 average client and the 25% drop-through; 8-Jun-2026 on competitors withdrawing work), unquantified; the 300 departures are noted by both without analysis of who left. Known to one house, not modelled.
  4. Project PRIO. The £20m to £25m cost is known; Jefferies raised the general risk of clients claiming automation savings in June 2025; no house models retention or has a savings figure, because the company has given none. Known as a cost, unknown as a benefit.
  5. Dilution and the EPS basis. The brokers use diluted figures and have absorbed the 102.1m potential shares; the press and the "10% beat" headline (13.6p against 12.3p) mixed bases; Deutsche Bank's post-suspension count is stale. The narrative report calls this "minimal analyst attention". Known in the models, unknown in the narrative.
  6. Other items and the 106% gap. The Industry Digest's named example; the brokers emphasise adjusted figures and Deutsche Bank defends them (19-Jan-2026); nobody treats £25m a year of restructuring as operating cost. Not articulated.
  7. Working capital and cash composition. Debtor days and projects working capital noted by both houses in fragments (Jefferies' £10m to £20m projects headwind dates from April 2024); no integrated cash model; the FY26 outflow is not discussed. Fragmentary.
  8. The Public Interest Test. Deutsche Bank's weekly of 19-Jun-2026 covers the policy, the 2028 Cabinet Office pilot across 83 buildings and the defence exemptions in detail; Jefferies flags public-sector exposure; neither maps contracts. Known as a theme, not as a number.
  9. Technical Services. Both note the minus 5% and the 70% secured sales target; neither gives a recovery timeline. Known, not quantified.
  10. The mechanical slowdown as Marlowe annualises. Deutsche Bank notes the £208m inorganic contribution; nobody frames the halving of reported growth as arithmetic rather than deterioration. Not articulated as a trap.
  11. The break case. Jefferies' 115p stress (8x on 13.2p) and Deutsche Bank's 151p undisturbed anchor are known; the leadership succession is noted by both; the buyback restart is noted by Deutsche Bank; the sector re-rating's effect on the break multiple and the order of events in a break (arbitrage selling, then buyback, then succession, then re-rating) are not discussed. Partly known.
  12. The Authority's timetable and the implied break probability. Dominant in both sell-side and retail commentary; Deutsche Bank's "below 20%" market-share argument is the consensus view; the ION Analytics and Mergermarket commentary on OCS's right to lapse is the only dissent; nobody publishes an implied probability. Fully known as a topic; the 1.3p interim dividend and the dividend double-count are the two arithmetical gaps.

Peers and the sector narrative. The sector's narratives are well articulated and largely disconnected from Mitie's specifics: de-equitisation (Morgan Stanley, 11-May-2026: listed European business services at 9.1x EBITDA against 12x in 60 private deals since 2020; the OCS offer at about 11.6x EV/EBIT sits inside the private range), ISS's technology and buyback story and its 5.5% to 6.0% target, Securitas's technology mix (35% of sales), Compass's pricing and 96% retention, Sodexo's reset, Coor's density and indexation lag, Rentokil's route-density troubles, Capita's artificial-intelligence deflation (contract values down 26% in three years in customer-experience outsourcing). Investors in the sector are focused on de-equitisation, on who can hold margin through wage inflation, and on whether artificial intelligence deflates or defends labour-heavy revenue. The first theme has already played out for Mitie; the second and third are exactly the standalone questions nobody is asking about Mitie because the bid has made them academic.

What a break would surprise investors with, in order of likely price effect. First, the order of events: forced selling by arbitrage holders into a thin UK mid-cap market before any fundamental news. Second, the share count: a standalone Mitie with 1,302m shares and up to about 50m further options to vest, against models at 1,255m. Third, the FY28 question: a successor's first guidance, PRIO's retention and the Budget's cost, none of which is in any published model. Fourth, the recurring cash cost of other items, which would show up in a FY27 free cash flow print discussed against £150m. On the other side: a cash business at 1.1x leverage that would restart an £81m buyback within weeks, a margin print at or above 5%, a credit rating that DBRS says would revert to BBB stable, and a sector trading at 11.7x to 11.8x for its two closest peers. The surprise would be net negative in the first quarter after a break and roughly neutral thereafter.

Scoring. For an investment case in the company, the question is whether what the narrative does not reflect is favourable. It is not: what is unknown is mainly the detail of the break case, and that detail is net negative in the window where it would matter; what is favourable and unknown (cash quality, the standalone margin step's plausibility, the buyback) cannot be monetised by a listed holder if the offer completes and would be swamped by arbitrage selling if it did not. The narrative is complete where it needs to be for the 90% outcome and incomplete, in the wrong direction, for the 10% outcome.

Co-movement of MTO-GB with its dashboard peers (computed 2026-10-23 from the master dashboard's daily prices)

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Prices only; the multiple's own history is in the financials block.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: MITIE Group PLC (MTO-GB), the UK's largest facilities services company (FY26 revenue £5,619m, year to 31 March 2026), reporting two divisions: Business Services (security, hygiene and landscapes, facilities compliance including the Marlowe business acquired in August 2025, central government, immigration and justice, Spain) and Technical Services (engineering maintenance, Facilities Transformation projects, defence, healthcare, local government and education); subject to a recommended cash offer from CD&R-backed OCS Group of 218.5p per share plus the 3.1p FY26 final dividend, announced 21 July 2026, approved by shareholders on 16 September 2026, with completion expected in calendar Q1 2027 subject to Competition and Markets Authority clearance.

Request: OWN-1

Question: For MITIE Group PLC (MTO-GB), what did the UK Autumn Budget of 28 October 2026 and the Low Pay Commission's final recommendation announce for employers of low-paid labour: the National Living Wage rate from April 2027 (the Commission's indicative figure was £13.18, per Morgan Stanley on 6 October 2026), any change to employer National Insurance rates, thresholds or reliefs (including any holiday for younger workers), any change to pensions automatic enrolment contributions, the implementation dates still to come under the Employment Rights Act 2025 and the statutory Two-Tier Workforce Code; what the Institute for Fiscal Studies, the Low Pay Commission, trade bodies and the covering brokers (Jefferies, Deutsche Bank, Investec) estimate as the gross annual cost of these measures to Mitie and to UK facilities services peers (Serco, Compass, ISS's UK business, OCS Group, Capita) for the year to March 2028, the share recoverable under contract indexation and change-of-law clauses and over what lag, and how Mitie and those peers have responded in their statements since the Budget, with reference to Mitie's disclosed FY26 recovery of 94% of wage inflation and 76% of employer National Insurance (results of 4 June 2026) and its guided FY27 net inflation headwind of £10m to £12m?

Request: OWN-2

Question: For MITIE Group PLC (MTO-GB), how does the company define free cash flow and how does its reported FY26 free cash flow of £162m (results of 4 June 2026) reconcile to the FY26 cash flow statement: cash generated from operations, the working-capital movement (the annual report records an outflow of £35.7m), the cash cost of other items (restructuring, Marlowe integration and transaction costs), net interest paid, cash tax paid and the effective cash tax rate (including the use of Marlowe's and Mitie's brought-forward tax losses), capital expenditure on fixed assets and intangibles, lease capital repayments, and whether acquisition transaction costs or any other item are excluded from the measure; what the covering brokers (Jefferies, Deutsche Bank, Investec) forecast for each of these components and for the FY27 free cash flow outturn against the company's £150m guidance, including their treatment of the OCS transaction costs and Project PRIO's £20m to £25m cost; what the H1 FY27 results of 19 November 2026 disclosed on each of these lines, on average and closing net debt and on the interim dividend of 1.3p permitted under the scheme if the acquisition was not effective by that date; and how Mitie's cash conversion and working-capital days compare with Serco, ISS, Compass, Coor and Capita on like-for-like definitions?

Request: OWN-3

Question: For MITIE Group PLC (MTO-GB), who owns the shares now and how would they behave if the OCS Group offer lapsed: how has the shareholder register changed since the offer announcement of 21 July 2026 (merger-arbitrage and event-driven funds, index funds, long-only holders that have sold, the position of Oasis Management, which held a 5.9% direct stake and a 9.9% cash-settled swap interest per the annual report of 24 September 2026 and gave irrevocable undertakings that lapse if the scheme is withdrawn), what share of the register is now held by arbitrage holders, what the Competition and Markets Authority's case page shows about the Phase 1 launch date, statutory deadline, third-party submissions and any remedies discussion, and what the precedents of UK recommended cash offers that lapsed or were blocked since 2015 (for example Sainsbury's and Asda in 2019, Ageas and Direct Line in 2024, Cargotec and Konecranes in 2022, Getty Images and Shutterstock in 2026) show about the volume of selling, the depth and duration of the share-price fall relative to the undisturbed price and to the sector, the speed of buyback resumption, and the frequency and timing of a renewed approach by the same or another bidder?
